# The Unity of the New Testament: A Modern Paraphrase

*The Unity of the New Testament, by Frederick Denison Maurice, restated paragraph by paragraph in modern English. This paraphrase follows the order and content of the original without adding interpretation. Drafted with claude-sonnet-5; reviewed by a human editor.*

## EXTRACT FROM THE AUTHOR'S PREFACE.

This book is exactly what its title says: a Synopsis. It does not claim to be a commentary, though I examine each book with some care and often point out even small details that seemed to me to reveal the writer's design. But my aim was not to explain particular texts. I believe you cannot really feel the force of individual sentences unless you connect them with the purpose of the book they belong to. So I have looked for that purpose in each Gospel and Epistle I take up. And still more, I have wanted to show that they share one common subject; that they point to a Living Person; and that when read in relation to him they have a unity that no comparing of passages by itself could uncover.

I have not burdened you with what are called practical questions—first, because I have always found them very unpractical; and second, because I do not think it reverent to use the Bible to point a moral or decorate a story of our own. I believe it contains a revelation. What I want to ask is what it reveals.

## LECTURE I.

### PART I.

MARK I. [I.]{.smallcaps}

*The beginning of the Gospel of Jesus Christ, the Son of God*.

*The passage:* This is where the good news of Jesus Christ, the Son of God, begins.

In these lectures I will divide the New Testament into three parts. First, the first three Gospels; then the Epistles of James, Peter, and Paul, together with the Acts that record their doings; and finally all the books ascribed to John.

It may seem an outrageous undertaking to treat such subjects in so little space. But I am glad of the limit; for what I intend, a wide field would tempt rather than help me. I want to ask whether one leading truth runs through these writings, binds them together, and accounts for their differences of form and their apparent contradictions. If such a truth exists, it should show itself on the very surface. It should stand up to close criticism, yet also reveal itself in the general course of the narrative and the discourses. No cleverness should be needed to find it; my only task should be to show that it forces the reader to see coherence in these writings, even when his theories incline him to deny it.

But if that is all I hope to do, why is the argument needed? These books have fed Christian people everywhere for centuries; scholars have poured out their learning on them. Must not the very suggestion that their governing principle still needs to be brought to light in 1846 imply that either the books are untruthful, or the man who offers to explain them is out of his senses?

I think so. If the principle I mean to set forth has never been stated in any Creed, or has held only a minor place there rather than being felt as the central one on which every article rests—if it has had no influence on the order of society in Christendom, and scholars and commentators are now hearing of it for the first time—then I admit it cannot be what I claim, a key to Scripture and to some of its greatest recent difficulties. But all I want is to bring out clearly what most of us already profess to believe, and what, as I hope to show later, has shaped events and the making of society in the modern world from the fall of Jerusalem to now. It seems to me we have gone wrong in studying Scripture not from too much simplicity but from too much refinement—looking far off for what lies at our feet, refusing to take words as they stand and to believe the writers meant what they say. If so, it may be a humble but useful duty to claim the New Testament as the ordinary traveller's own possession. When he grasps it, I am sure he will be readier than ever to acknowledge his debt to the scholar.

Let me explain what I mean. We usually call the first three Gospels biographies of Jesus of Nazareth. We assume they describe the acts and words that showed Him the most perfect of men and the greatest of prophets; that they record miracles proving His mission divine; and that then, by certain valuable phrases scattered through the story but not made prominent, they claim for Him a superhuman nature and origin. We say it was left to the fourth Gospel to declare that nature and origin clearly, and that this Gospel is far more doctrinal, the source of the Creed's high dogmas. We suppose the main support of those dogmas lies in the Epistles, especially Paul's. So we take the great difference to be that the Epistles tell us what to believe while the Gospels set before us a divine example of living. We admit this is not exact—the Epistles are practical too, and the Gospels contain high doctrine—but we think the division good enough for ordinary use, pointing to what to expect in each. The Apocalypse is harder to place: some say it lies beyond the everyday Christian's study; others say it develops one great practical matter treated less clearly elsewhere—the second coming of our Lord, which stands in such contrast to the humble coming the Gospels describe.

I think I have given a fair account of the common views held in this country about these books, which we all regard, more or less clearly, as different in authority and character from all others. I will not dwell on views held abroad by those who deny their authority altogether. But note that even the most extravagant of those views start from the same premise as our popular notions. The modern Tübingen school has pushed further than any other its theories of contradiction among the Apostles and Evangelists: it sets Paul's spiritual school against the Judaical school of James, Peter, and John; it grants Paul only four or five genuine Epistles; it holds Revelation to be genuinely John's *because* it opposes Paul's doctrine; it takes the fourth Gospel for a second-century work that first placed Christian theology on an Alexandrian footing. This school has brought its learning and philosophy to explain the discrepancies of aim and character in the New Testament books that it assumes we all quietly admit while speaking vaguely of them. I do not say that, if the notions our commentators and harmonists have sanctioned and spread through our schools are granted, there is *no* escape but the conclusions of Bauer and his followers. But I confess I think the fight against these scholars will be hard, and finally useless, unless we are ready to reconsider the ground we share with them. We may win a skirmish here and there, catching them twisting church history or misusing their criticism; but onlookers will treat it as a quarrel among critics. Without following the arguments, they will throw their weight to the attacker, saying: "You rest your faith on books that able men, not quite rejecting Christianity, say teach several different faiths. It takes much learning to defend them, if you can. How can you ask ordinary laymen to make such books their guide? Whatever they are, they are not what our fathers thought."

Reaching that conclusion would be harder if no one had tried to explain the Gospel narratives on a principle that treats them as worthless as history. Long tradition and habit shield people from mere critical cleverness; they would say, "These documents must have *some* common meaning; let the opponents show it before asking us to throw them away." But the rationalist knows this demand and has his answer. A writer whose influence among ordinary readers far exceeds that of a learned school like Bauer's set out years ago to show what worth might remain in the life of Jesus even when nearly all its records are taken as mere myth. It shows, he said, a great idea of humanity; it is one of the human spirit's attempts to conceive its own greatness; it explains the well-known human tendency to raise benefactors into kings and gods, as heathen records so often show. It came when other religions were worn out and there was a craving for something more universal and democratic than the old national faiths. Its rise fits everything we know of the world's decay at that time. The Christian mythology replaced the older ones because it had a broader human basis, and because its falsehoods as well as its truths suited its age. "It does not suit ours," these teachers go on. "The same decay, the same timidity and unbelief, now appear in its professors as appeared in the Jewish and heathen worlds when it began. But there is now, among philosophers and even ordinary men, a power to appreciate its kind, human idea that no earlier age had. Now is the time to separate that idea from its surroundings and offer it to the world as the final essence of the facts and doctrines men have so long believed."

No careful observer of our times can suppose one man would have spoken these words so plainly if the same thing were not already working in thousands of hearts. So whatever we make of the assurances of divines in the country where this doctrine first arose—that its teacher has little influence in their schools now, that others have long outrun him in his own direction, that he and his followers have seen how impossible was the half-faith he tried to keep—we may still be sure that all their reasonings, and all the earnest faith behind them, have not made Strauss's dogmas negligible to them or to us. Look, and you find them quietly adopted by a large class of thinking and half-thinking people in every section of our nation, old and young, poor and rich. They hold their ground against all the arguments Paley and his school urged for the authenticity of the Divine Records; indeed they are put forward in popular form before humble audiences as proof that those arguments are outdated. More, their propagators claim Christianity has gained in their hands, that they revere its principles more than we do. They ask: what are our harmonists' accounts of journeys to Nazareth and Capernaum beside their recognition of the living, divine truth in the sayings and life of Jesus? What are our dry attempts to prove His mission by miracle beside their frank admission that His moral teaching overthrew more falsehood and set up more truth than any other man's? What are our endless controversies beside their acknowledgment that He preached and lived an all-embracing charity and humanity?

I hope the existence and spread of such thoughts may excuse the inquiry I now begin. I have said I believe many students who start from the belief that the Gospels are mainly records of our Lord's life as a Teacher of Nazareth, and that the Epistles and fourth Gospel are the chief witnesses to His divinity, will end in Bauer's conclusions about the essential diversity of Gospels and Epistles. And I say now that many practical people, hardly students at all but sharing the habit of thought our popular teaching gives, will flee to Strauss's system and fancy for a while they have gained in faith and sympathy by the exchange. But since I believe neither class knows what it will finally lose—not in religious faith only, but in the love of truth, in any moral basis for personal and social life, and above all in that very humanity for which they would sacrifice everything else; since I think this Straussian doctrine, for all its pretensions, is feeble and narrow, and destroys all the blessings the Gospel has brought and will bring to the poor man, though it seems designed for his sake—I do not care how much I lose their respect, or their opponents', while I try to resist their falsehood by confessing what seems to me a great and dangerous one of our own.

Instead of beginning from our Lord simply as the Man of Nazareth, it seems to me that the first three Gospels, just as much as the fourth, begin by assuming Him to be the Son of God and the King of men. Their aim is to show how He fulfilled those characters. All the discourses and acts they attribute to Him are simple and natural on that assumption, and unintelligible on any other. My first lecture will make good these claims, first from the facts common to the three Gospels, then from their characteristic differences. In the next I will try to show that the Epistles of James, Peter, and Paul, seen together with the events in Acts, present the Gospel of the Son of God and His Kingdom at another stage and under three distinct aspects—but just as personally and livingly as the Evangelists do. Finally, that John's Gospel and Epistles harmonize the aspects traced in the other writers, and that the Apocalypse brings the history to a crisis all the other books had foretold—the full showing of the Son of God and His Kingdom—and reveals that, as this Kingdom fulfilled the meaning of all Jewish institutions and prophecies, so it would be the real foundation of all human society after those institutions dissolved. If the facts, seen plainly, bear out these conclusions, then Bauer's arguments for the diversity and contradiction of the New Testament books will actually establish their unity; and Strauss's arguments that they embody a conception of something supremely human will show that their basis is essentially divine. And belief in their authority will rest not on knowing old traditions or following clever special pleading, but on the testimony of eighteen centuries, which will tell whether the Kingdom the New Testament said would come has in fact come. That last inquiry belongs to a later part of these lectures; today I begin the first.

When I quote records found in all three Gospels, I will take them from Matthew, because his is said to be emphatically the Ebionite Gospel, the one most directly opposed to the doctrine of Paul, the teacher of the Gentiles.

#### THE PREACHING OF JOHN.

1st. The first thing all the Evangelists report is the same: "In those days came John the Baptist preaching in the wilderness of Judæa, and saying, Repent ye, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand."

The words "kingdom of heaven" confront us at the very start. They seem to be important words. Perhaps the account of John's preaching and actions will help us decide what they mean.

Those who take St. Matthew to have been a narrow Jew who merely grafted a physical Christianity onto his old Hebrew ideas will readily grant that by this phrase he meant a real sovereignty, drawn from the Jewish Scriptures. On their view he was dreaming of the restored kingdom of David, and called it a Kingdom of Heaven because he believed its power came from the Lord God of Israel—but he still meant one to be set up on earth. Such readers, who give Matthew the character the Neological critics assign him, will grant this most readily of him, and also of Mark, and somewhat less of Luke. I cannot see how we can conclude otherwise. It is impossible that a Jew using the word "Kingdom" meant only what modern theologians mean by a "Christian dispensation" or a "divine and miraculous doctrine." Whatever the old Prophets meant by a Divine Kingdom when they linked it with the son of Jesse, or Solomon, or Hezekiah, that same meaning it must have carried for an Evangelist raised on those Books and devoted to them.

What, then, did the words mean when Isaiah or Jeremiah used them? Did the kingdom of Solomon or Hezekiah differ from the kingdom of Pharaoh or Hiram merely in being outwardly more splendid? Take the Jewish kingdom at its most magnificent, just after the temple was built, when silver was reckoned as nothing and the world's treasures poured into Jerusalem. Would not any of the Prophets have felt that its glory lay in the same thing as in its worst oppression—when Sennacherib's armies had destroyed the fortified cities and were besieging Zion—namely, that the visible king was a witness to an invisible one, in whom all real rule dwelt? On what else do the Prophets' warnings rest, when calamity threatened? They tell the people they are not acknowledging the invisible King, that they have forgotten His covenant, that they are bowing before stocks and stones, and they call them to return to the God from whom they have revolted. They declare that He is coming out of His place to prove He is their King and Ruler of the whole earth—that whether king, people, or priests trust Him or not, He will show Himself King of kings and Lord of lords. Does anyone imagine that phrase means less than this: that He rules in a region other kings strive for but cannot reach, that He governs the inner workings of nature and the minds and wills of men? The seer is calling his people to turn from gross, slavish notions of mere outward dominion—the root of all idolatry—to Him in whom all real power dwells without measure. Only so could they see the difference between a king of Judæa and a king of Babylon, and only so could they grasp what the full kingdom was that all their hard discipline was preparing them for.

John, we are told, wore camel's hair and a leather girdle, ate locusts and wild honey, and preached in the wilderness. All these signs testify that he, like any old Prophet, came to draw men from visible things to an invisible Ruler. His stern, simple words hold the very essence of the old prophetic speech, and the people felt it; they went out to him in the wilderness and asked whether he was Elijah. They were sure he had a message for the nation and for each person, and their consciences answered it. They were not openly worshipping idols; nothing in their situation obviously resembled those Elijah had summoned to renounce Baal. Yet their hearts confessed the likeness: they knew they were idolaters as much as their fathers, and that they, with a synagogue in every city, were falling away from the Lord God of Israel as surely as those who had groves on every hill.

John tested that conviction. He came baptizing with water, calling Jews to submit to a rite that admitted Gentiles to the Temple-worship—so they had to confess they needed purifying. And what purifying? John spoke of it as most inward: it was for the remission of sins. He did not shrink from explaining his own sign. The most honored Jews were called a brood of vipers, warned of coming wrath, and told not to say to themselves, "We have Abraham to our father," for God could raise up children to Abraham from the very stones. So that no one could doubt whether the baptism meant washing off outward stains or the deepest inward reformation, they were told the axe was laid to the *root* of the trees, and whatever bore no good fruit would be cut down and burned.

All this language, I submit, is consistent with itself; there is no clumsy patchwork of later refinement grafted onto a Jewish stock. Read it by the light of the Jewish Scriptures—you can read it by no other; but that light shows that at a time when the last trace of native royalty had gone from Judæa, when it bore the shameful marks of a Roman province, when pretenders kept arising to recall the old glories and urge revolt against the oppressor, a voice spoke of the very kingdom all devout Jewish rulers had acknowledged as about to appear. It called that a Kingdom of Heaven because it was a kingdom over the inner man, the spirit and heart; it scorned all exclusive Jewish claims even while asserting the greatness of Jewish privileges; and it announced a crisis at hand that would shake the whole of existing society to its centre.

#### THE BAPTISM OF CHRIST.

*The passage:* John told the people, "I baptize you with water for repentance, but One mightier than I is coming after me, whose shoes I am not fit to carry; He will baptize you with the Holy Spirit"—and Matthew and Luke add, "and with fire."

Whether or not we include those words, the passage means the same. The word *fire*, and the mention of the *fan in the hand* that follows, show us One coming to purge, sift, and judge—One who sees through all appearances and works on the inner heart of society and of each person. But the words "He shall baptize with the Holy Ghost" already carry that meaning, and fix our attention on the kind of power this coming One will use. John's call to repentance was a call to an inward, spiritual act. He says the greater One will do perfectly what he himself did imperfectly, will strike deeper at the root of the tree, will make plain what his own baptism meant, and will give men the energy to do and be what John told them they must. Note that these supposedly carnal, Ebionitish, Jewish Gospels rest our Lord's whole history on this: their foundation is the proclamation, "He comes to baptize with the Spirit and with fire." I point this out as one of the most obvious surface signs of their shared purpose—one needing no skill to find, one no careless reader can miss. And I ask you to watch, as we go on, whether this surface fact is not the sign of something running through the heart of every narrative—something that it is as reasonable to suppose was inserted into them as to suppose a bricklayer inserted the idea of the Parthenon.

The story continues: Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan to be baptized by John. John tried to stop Him, saying he himself needed to be baptized by Jesus, not the reverse; but Jesus said, "Suffer it to be so now, for thus it becometh us to fulfil all righteousness," and John consented. As Jesus came up from the water, the heavens opened, He saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove and resting on Him, and a voice from heaven said, "This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased."

All the older heretics saw how central this narrative is to the Gospel history and to Christian doctrine. Every Gnostic had to interpret it and fit it into his theory of the relation between Jesus and the Christ. And those today who reject the opening chapters of Matthew and Luke must, to be consistent, treat this as the start of the Gospel record. Neither group can be wrong in thinking it held a most prominent place in the Gospel writers' minds, and that they connected everything they later tell of His ministry with it. No doubt the narrative itself gives them a pretext for charging these writers with carnal, superstitious notions. If there can be no visible signs of the invisible, and if believing an actual Man to be the Son of God is to be dismissed at once as anthropomorphic, then there is nothing left to debate; the matter is settled by one sweeping begging of the question. But if that is not granted, plain men will not be prevented—by being told the Dove or the Voice are merely Jewish or Pagan ways of projecting our own inner experiences outward—from seeing that this record carries us into the deep, inward ground that philosophy always seeks and never reaches. The Kingdom John said was near is declared to rest on a living Person. He who had always ruled it is now revealed: "This is my beloved Son" *is* the revelation. The Evangelists are to testify of a Son of God, clothed with the Spirit of God so that He may rule the spiritual world and all the inward powers of things. We are to see whether men so simple, raised in so narrow a school, make good their magnificent claims—whether they betray the emptiness of a mere dream by exalting a merely human Friend and Teacher, or whether they truly speak as men commissioned to set forth One who owed nothing to the accidents of His earthly position and everything to His relation to a divine and invisible Father.

#### THE TEMPTATION.

All the neological interpreters say the next step in the story shows the clearest signs of being a myth. It has nothing to do with the regular course of events; like the legends attached to great heroes, it was inserted to give the ordinary happenings significance, but it actually disturbs their sequence and reveals what a curious patchwork the whole composition is. I fully admit that if the Gospels are simply the record of the journeys of Jesus of Nazareth over one, two, or three years, from Judæa to Galilee and back, and if our knowledge of the facts is measured by our skill in dating these journeys and matching them to Jewish festivals, then the Temptation is an awkward obstacle. But if we take the Baptism to be what it appears—if Jesus was there declared to be the Son of God and sealed with the Spirit—then there is exactly the connection between the third and fourth chapters of St. Matthew that the composers of our Litany recognized. The Baptism asserts, "Thou art the Son of God"; the Temptation takes the form, "if Thou be the Son of God." He is endued with the Spirit of a Son and wrestles with the spirit of disobedience. He will not cut Himself off from Mankind by turning stones into bread for Himself, for Man is to live by every word of God. He will not throw Himself from the pinnacle of the Temple, for it is written that you shall not tempt the Lord your God. He will not receive the kingdoms of the world from Satan, for it is written that you shall worship the Lord your God. The idea of a Son who claims nothing for Himself, but trusts and obeys His Father in everything, is brought out here with a clearness, definiteness, and simplicity, and with an absence of all verbal pomp and of the usual coloring of a legend, that is at the very least strange. The idea of a spiritual conflict, a battle with a spiritual foe, is set out here without the material devices used by the crudest and the greatest artists alike—something you would hardly expect from a Jewish tax-gatherer, still less if his story had later been embellished by some theological doctor. But, I will be asked, does this not give a personality to the Spirit of Evil, and is that not exactly what one looks for in a legend? Certainly one cannot read the story without feeling that our Lord was engaged in a personal battle with a personal foe. It is no shadow-fight, and nothing in it has the look of a dream or a vision; everything is intensely real. Yet the wonder is that this reality and personality are sustained with such an absence of materialism. No image of the Tempter is offered—neither the kind a medieval painter would give nor the kind belonging to Milton's conception. We feel that the Son of God, clothed in a human body, was not a Person by virtue of that body; and likewise that the personality of the opposing power has nothing to do with an outward shape or visible surroundings. We are led to feel that there is a deep, radical evil, a spirit of evil, underlying all the shapes and forms in which it appears to us on earth; that He who could reach that radical evil and strip those shapes of it could alone assert the dominion of the God of Truth and Love over the world; and that this radical evil is nothing original, nothing God created, but essentially the spirit of disobedience, a perverted, rebel will, so that He who has the true obedient will must be its destroyer and the Redeemer of the Universe from it. Such an opening to a history of successive acts of redemption or deliverance shows no sign of having been transferred from some other record into a place it was never meant for. If it means anything, its meaning belongs right there; and to lack it would be a real cause of perplexity.

#### THE PREACHING OF JESUS.

*The passage:* When Jesus heard that John had been imprisoned, He went into Galilee and began to preach: "Repent, for the kingdom of Heaven is at hand." Mark records that He came preaching the Gospel of the kingdom of God, saying the time was fulfilled, the kingdom near, and that men should repent and believe the Gospel. Luke says Jesus returned in the power of the Spirit into Galilee, His fame spreading through the region, and that at Nazareth He read from Isaiah: the Spirit of the Lord was upon Him, anointing Him to preach good news to the poor, heal the broken-hearted, proclaim freedom to captives, sight to the blind, liberty to the bruised, and the acceptable year of the Lord.

Matthew tells us that when Jesus heard John had been imprisoned, He went into Galilee, and he quotes a passage he says was fulfilled by Christ's coming to the region of Zebulon and Naphtali. From that time Jesus began to preach, "Repent, for the kingdom of Heaven is at hand." Mark says that after John was imprisoned, Jesus came into Galilee preaching the Gospel of the kingdom of God: the time is fulfilled, the kingdom is at hand, repent and believe the Gospel. Luke says Jesus returned in the power of the Spirit into Galilee, His fame going out through the whole region, and then describes His coming to Nazareth, where He was raised, and His reading the passage from Isaiah about the Spirit of the Lord being upon Him to preach good news to the poor, heal the broken-hearted, free the captives, give sight to the blind, release the bruised, and proclaim the acceptable year of the Lord. A harmonist will reasonably connect these accounts as referring to the same visit to Galilee. But I quote them to point out a deeper and more important agreement, one concerning the nature of our Lord's preaching and its connection with the earlier history. When He comes to Galilee, He begins by preaching the Kingdom of God and calling men to repent because it is at hand. The first two Evangelists give no other words spoken by Him who had been declared the Son of God than these. John the Baptist's message is exactly the message of the Christ: the same announcement of a kingdom, the same call to repentance, the same reference to the fulfilling of the time and an approaching crisis. Only now this announcement is called a Gospel—good news that the kingdom is at hand and the time fulfilled; men are to repent and believe this good news. Luke expands and explains the others. He begins by saying Jesus came in the power of the Spirit into Galilee, and shows in one synagogue what kind of preaching it was that elsewhere won Him praise. His preaching announces One able to heal the sick and free the captives because the Spirit of the Lord was upon Him—a Deliverer who could undo chains no one else could undo, a King with dominion over the secret powers and inner being of man, now declared to be at hand. Men were to repent so that they might understand what His government over them was; to confess His dominion over them was to believe the Gospel.

#### CALLING OF THE DISCIPLES.

*The passage:* As Jesus walked by the sea of Galilee He saw two brothers, Simon called Peter and Andrew, casting a net, for they were fishers. He said, "Follow me, and I will make you fishers of men," and at once they left their nets and followed Him. Going further, He saw two more brothers, James the son of Zebedee and John, in a boat with their father mending their nets. He called them, and they immediately left the boat and their father and followed Him.

St. Matthew tells this story, and St. Mark's account is nearly the same. St. Luke joins it to the miraculous catch of fish, but his key-words, "From henceforth thou shalt catch men," belong just as much to the other two. Suppose we had only these narratives, with everything peculiar to St. Luke left out, and suppose we had heard nothing beforehand of a Kingdom or of a Son of God. Our first conclusion, I think, would be this: told so plainly and in so few words, the story is clearly meant to describe how some royal person claimed authority over the humble men around him, took them into his service, and gave them a high office—one they could scarcely understand, but which implied a remarkable influence and mastery over their fellow human beings. And if we then found that all the earlier records seemed to speak of just such a King, exercising a mysterious authority that He had come to assert and use on behalf of human beings, we would certainly think this narrative stood in a close and natural relation to the rest. On any other supposition—if the writer's mind had been filled with some other conviction—we would expect quite different language: far less direct and simple, and far less majestic.

#### THE MIRACLES.

*The passage:* Jesus went through all Galilee, teaching in the synagogues, preaching the Gospel of the Kingdom, and healing every kind of sickness and disease. His fame spread through Syria, and people brought Him all who were sick — those tormented by various diseases, those possessed by devils, the lunatic, and the paralysed — and He healed them.

This announcement in St. Matthew comes right after the call of the disciples. In St. Mark (chapter one, verses 21 to 35) you find the same events, with special attention to a man in a synagogue with an unclean spirit, who cried out, "Let us alone; what have we to do with Thee, Thou Jesus of Nazareth? I know Thee who Thou art, the Holy One of God"; then the cure of Simon's wife's mother of a fever, and the healing of many sick and the casting out of many devils. In St. Luke (chapter four, verses 16 to 44) there are the same constant references to cures, the same account of the unclean spirit in the synagogue and the cure of Simon's wife's mother, ending with the words, "Devils also came out of many, crying out and saying, Thou art Christ the Son of God."

If you compare these passages carefully, nothing strikes me more than how closely the so-called miraculous part is blended with the preaching. St. Matthew: "He preached the Gospel of the Kingdom, *and* healed all manner of sickness." St. Mark: "He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes; *and* there was in their synagogue a man having an unclean spirit"; and the people were amazed and asked, "What new doctrine is this? For with authority commandeth He the unclean spirits, and they do obey Him." St. Luke repeats nearly the same words in nearly the same connection, and his fifth chapter, even more than the others, shows the link between the "doctrine," the "word," and these acts of "authority."

Now suppose we take "preaching the Kingdom of God" literally, as I have taken it — suppose the actual King of the world was coming to claim His Kingdom, and He who by His word gave life to every creature was really come to show Himself and claim their homage. Then this connection is exactly what you would expect. You would not expect the Evangelists to say, as we tend to, that Christ delivered a beautiful discourse and then, to prove His commission, put forth strange new powers. You would expect them to say just what they do say: He declared His kingdom, and He healed the sick — using the powers He had always used, and declaring who it was from whom all healing had always come.

Other signs also show which view of Christ ruled the Evangelists' minds. If they were writing the legend of a hero doing more extraordinary things than any man, the acts would be as outward and showy as possible. An ordinary physician works by reaching the hidden source of a disease to overcome its outward symptoms; the legendary miracle-worker acts by touches and charms on the visible ailment before him, and lets the world admire how fast it vanished. But suppose a writer describes One who has dominion over all the secret springs of human life, whose servant the physician has always been. Then you would not be surprised if he told you this Person spoke to something within the man and set that right first; that having given the blessing He did not want it made known; and that He treated the vanishing of outward symptoms merely as proof of a cure at the root. These are indeed the features of the Gospel narratives; everyone notices them. They are cited as signs of the writers' simplicity and their freedom from any straining after display — but they are not, I think, dwelt on as marking the very purpose of the narratives.

Closely related are the frequent references to casting out devils. The Evangelists connect particular kinds of sickness with diabolical possession, yet somehow leave the impression that all sickness has this origin. At any rate they point to this exercise of power as the most characteristic of Christ, the one that explains all the rest. Commentators generally want to pass this over as a difficulty. Some say the Evangelists adapted themselves to Jewish speech; some say possession belonged to that age; some argue it has not been unknown in any age; some speak of the anthropomorphism and superstition natural in such writers. Those are plausible, high-sounding words, and I doubt those who use them freely have weighed their meaning. By anthropomorphism I mean conceiving the spiritual under a human shape — yet the Evangelists never give the devils a human shape. By superstition I mean putting above us what belongs below, or making our object of worship an object of fear — yet the Evangelists present the devils not as powers above but as powers working within, and present Christ not as teaching men to fear or worship them but as delivering men from them and teaching them to worship God. And if that is the aim of all these acts of power, they agree marvellously with the story that opens these Gospels as a preface to them: He who was proclaimed Son of God and endued with the Spirit of God was led by that Spirit into the wilderness to be tempted of the devil. He warred with the root-spirit of disobedience that had claimed dominion over men; now He wars with every form in which that evil power works their harm. That power shows itself most directly in whatever affects man personally and morally, whether or not mixed with physical disease. The *unclean* spirit — the spirit of despair that drives a man among the tombs, makes him cry out and cut himself with stones and terrify his fellows, whom no chains can bind — is the most inward tyrant of all. Christ, by commanding him to come out of the man, proclaims that everything diseased or corrupt in body or mind is not from above but from beneath — not part of the original order of the universe, not from God, but from rebellion against God — and is therefore to be undone by the Holy One of God, the Son of God, who comes to establish the Kingdom of God.

So instead of dwelling on the other miracles as the only satisfactory proofs of His mission while trying to avoid the stories of casting out devils as awkward puzzles, we should, if we follow the Evangelists, look in these for the general law of all the miracles, since they explain the nature of His work and the purpose of His coming better than any others. Whatever else they omit or report differently, all three fix our attention on these and keep suggesting their relation to the rest. All three, with some difference of detail, report the cure of the man (or men) among the tombs as the most characteristic. All speak — St. Matthew more than once — of how the Pharisees explained the miracles: "He casteth out devils by the Prince of the devils." All record His answer, "A house divided against itself cannot stand." All record the words, "all manner of sin and blasphemy shall be forgiven unto men; but the blasphemy against the Holy Ghost shall not be forgiven" — words which, even without St. Mark's explanation that they said He had an *unclean* spirit, lead us to conclude that their sin lay not in denying Christ's power but in calling good evil and evil good, a state of mind that hates the very character of God. All also attribute to Him these words, so baffling to anyone who sees His signs merely as startling portents: "If I by Beelzebub cast out devils, by whom do your children cast them out? Therefore shall they be your judges." In what sense could their sons be their judges? Was their power like His? If He was the true King of the world, all power ever exerted was His power, and He came to show from where such power came; so if His power was evil, then all power ever used by any Jewish physician to heal disease or madness was evil too.

Lastly, all the Evangelists connect faith in the person healed with most, if not all, of these acts of power. Now if the miracles were merely proofs of a divine mission, unconnected with its nature, you would expect the most overwhelming displays where the unbelief was most stubborn — as legends usually arrange it, with faith produced in the most reluctant by the spectacle. But if these signs were tokens of the presence of One who came to claim the human spirit and raise it out of bondage to other masters, then we see at once that there is something more kingly and mysterious in an act that calls the man himself into trust and hope than in one that merely rectifies his body or mind. Not only is the limb straightened and the bleeding stopped, but the person who wields the limb, through whose veins the blood flows, is called into life and health by the voice of the life-giver.

These remarks are not new; they are worth anything only as they order thoughts we all share. No reader of the Gospels has ever doubted that the graciousness of Christ's miracles belongs to their very nature — a quality writers on evidence have laboured over more than was needed, for the heart takes such impressions more readily when they are not forced. The apparent exceptions — the cursing of the fig-tree, the destruction of the swine — have been explained with painful ingenuity, as if we needed convincing that a moral lesson meant to cure human beings may be got through the death of a tree or an animal. What I want is that we follow out the conviction such phrases imply, and admit that the Evangelists saw these miracles as ways in which the great Deliverer revealed Himself as Deliverer, actually breaking the fetters that bound human bodies as well as spirits. Seen this way, all the other miracles they record will be felt to have their own fitness in the divine economy, for later instruction as well as immediate, especially to free men from superstition. That the disciples should learn the winds and waves obeyed their Master, and the multitude feel that it was He who gave them bread, was as needful as feeling that He restored the powers of body and soul. The Jews needed such a lesson, being still prone to tremble before the powers of nature and to think man lives by bread alone and not by the word of God. But what a lesson too lay in store for the worshippers of Neptune, Ceres, and Æsculapius — what a witness that the powers they thought divided among different capricious gods were really gathered up in the one Lord and Friend of man!

#### PUBLICANS AND PHARISEES. THE NEW AND OLD GARMENT.

*The passage:* As Jesus went on, He saw a tax-gatherer named Matthew at his booth and said, "Follow me." Matthew rose and followed. Later, as Jesus ate in the house, many tax-gatherers and sinners sat down with Him and His disciples. The Pharisees asked the disciples why their Master ate with such people. Jesus answered that the healthy do not need a doctor, but the sick do, and told them to learn what "I will have mercy, and not sacrifice" means, for He came to call not the righteous but sinners to repentance. Then John's disciples asked why they and the Pharisees fasted often, but His disciples did not. Jesus replied that the wedding guests cannot mourn while the bridegroom is with them; but when the bridegroom is taken away, they will fast. He added that no one sews new cloth onto an old garment, for the patch tears away and makes the rip worse, and no one puts new wine into old wineskins, for the skins burst and both are lost; new wine goes into new skins, and both are kept.

All three Evangelists tell this story in nearly the same order, with Levi in two accounts where Matthew stands in the other. I may seem to be joining two passages that have no direct link. But I am trying to follow the Evangelists closely, and where all three keep the same order I assume they felt a connection in the parts of their story and wanted us to feel it. I think even their simplest readers have sensed this: that the question of John's disciples grew naturally out of our Lord's answer to the Pharisees, and that His comparisons of the new and old garments and the new and old wineskins were meant to explain a deep reason for the difference between His disciples and those of the Pharisees or of John.

I. In the call of Levi, or Matthew, we see the same power over the person's will that appeared in the call of Andrew and Simon, James and John. The words are not those of a disciple attaching himself to a master out of preference; they plainly convey One speaking with authority—an authority the conscience and heart recognized, though nothing in His outward appearance supported it.

II. But Matthew is not a fisherman; he is a rich tax-gatherer. As soon as he obeys, he gathers men of his own class and invites his Master to eat with them. This class had more than a bad reputation: many of them must have been extortioners, and the general dislike of them must have thrown them among people who truly deserved the name of sinners. To imagine Matthew's guests were merely people labelled sinners through Pharisaic prejudice is to twist the letter of the Gospel and, still more, to set aside its meaning. The Pharisees do not complain that our Lord showed kindness to wrongdoers, but that He mixed with them as friends, eating and drinking with them. Did not the Law justify their own avoidance of impurity? The Evangelist offers no direct answer to these questions. He overrules them all with the words, "They that be whole need not a physician, but they that be sick." If he saw his Master as the King of Men, come to restore an order that had been broken; if he believed that the righteousness all the Law's precepts pointed to dwelt in Him, and that He came to plant that righteousness in the hearts of those who had strayed furthest—then the proverb applied plainly and truly. In other words, if he felt that our Lord's Kingdom was the kingdom over men that had lain beneath all legal rules, and that it asserted its power by fulfilling their purpose even when it seemed to set them aside, we can see how this story fits with all that has gone before. On any other view the Pharisees' objection would seem unanswered.

III. If this explanation is right, then the difficulty raised by John's disciples—though quite different from the Pharisees'—had a similar solution. John's disciples might recall that their master had won a readier hearing from publicans and sinners than from scribes and Pharisees, and that he had called some who came to his baptism a "generation of vipers." But was not his most obvious mark self-restraint, indifference to fleshly comforts? Would he not most wish his disciples to imitate that, even if it meant adopting the practices of the Pharisees? The new Teacher's eating and drinking seemed directly opposed to this. It was not so much the company that startled them as that a Teacher of righteousness should go to feasts at all. Why should fasting not mark His followers as it marked earlier schools? The first answer is, "The bridegroom is with them"—a strange expression for the head of a school, quite outside the associations such a role suggests. Yet it was no new idea to those who had read the forty-fifth Psalm, who had heard of a King delighting in the beauty of his promised Bride, riding on for truth and meekness and righteousness, and of her leaving her father's house to dwell with him. I do not now weigh the full force of these words. I only say that such language would inevitably make a Jew connect the one who used it of himself with ideas of royalty and divinity. His disciples could not fast because they had already reached the end fasting was meant to serve—the finding of the Lord and Ruler of their spirits. A time would come when they would feel deprived of Him, and then they would fast.

But the idea comes out more fully in the second part of the answer. No one has ever doubted that the old garment and old wineskins mean the institutions of the ancient order, and the new ones those Christ would establish. All have seen that our Lord meant, in some way, that it would be harmful merely to re-enact the old forms and customs until the substance and life beneath them had been revealed. But surely, unless His Kingdom was the everlasting Kingdom that all Jewish institutions had imperfectly displayed, these comparisons and the argument on them would not hold. He would be swapping new wineskins for old, not pressing out the wine to fill them. No more beautiful picture could be found of the claim that the Kingdom of God, once unfolded, would produce a clothing fitted to itself, and not merely keep the clothing it wore while still undeveloped. If that were not the Evangelists' meaning, then they used illustrations of the plainest, most natural kind with a most artificial and unreal significance.

#### THE APPOINTMENT OF THE APOSTLES.

*The passage:* Jesus called his twelve disciples and gave them power to cast out unclean spirits and to heal every kind of sickness. Their names are listed. He sent these twelve out with instructions: do not go to the Gentiles or into any Samaritan city, but go to the lost sheep of Israel. As you go, preach that the Kingdom of Heaven is near. Heal the sick, cleanse lepers, raise the dead, cast out devils; you received freely, so give freely. Carry no gold, silver, or brass, no bag, no second coat, no shoes, no staff — the workman deserves his food. When you enter a city or house, find who is worthy and stay there. Salute the house; if it is worthy, let your peace rest on it, and if not, let your peace return to you. If anyone will not receive you or hear you, shake the dust off your feet as you leave.

This passage differs in a few details from the matching accounts in Mark and Luke. Both of them separate the calling of the Apostles from their being sent to work. Our Lord's commands are far more detailed in Matthew chapter ten than in Mark chapter six or Luke chapter nine, and Luke assigns some of them instead to the seventy disciples. But the words I have quoted — except those about the Gentiles and Samaritans — appear in all three. I need not labor how much importance the Evangelists attach to this commission; everyone feels and admits it. It would waste time to prove that our Lord constantly speaks to His Apostles as heralds of a Kingdom, as men entrusted with spiritual powers, as the founders of a society, as men whose immediate task was to prepare the way for far higher tasks given them later. Someone will say this is exactly what we would expect — a list of directions written after the fact. The Evangelists wrote knowing what the Apostles had done, and so read back into this scene events they themselves may have seen or been told of. What I want to point out is simply that this narrative fits exactly with all that came before. John the Baptist came announcing a Kingdom was near; our Lord preached that the Kingdom of Heaven was near; the twelve were sent with the same message. The Kingdom John announced was plainly a Kingdom over the human spirit — he said the one coming after him would baptize with the Spirit. All the acts by which Christ testified of His Kingdom were acts of spiritual power, of dominion over spirits, and the same powers are said to be given to the Apostles for the same purpose. Everything in the Evangelists — their tone, their deliberate words and their accidental ones, where they agree and where they differ — shows a settled conviction that they are describing the acts of a Ruler: One who had ruled in the past and would give mightier proof of His rule to come, who had called out the twelve tribes as His national witnesses, and who was now preparing men to represent those tribes and carry their purpose to all the families of the earth. There is no wavering on this point, no lapse into some other idea; it is the assumption running through the whole narrative — never forced on us anywhere, yet felt everywhere.

#### THE SABBATH-DAY.

*The passage:* Jesus walked through the cornfields on the sabbath, and His hungry disciples plucked and ate ears of corn. The Pharisees objected that this was unlawful on the sabbath. Jesus answered: had they not read how David, when hungry, ate the shewbread reserved for the priests? And that the priests in the temple work on the sabbath yet are blameless? Here, He said, is One greater than the temple. Had they understood the words "I will have mercy, and not sacrifice," they would not have condemned the guiltless, for the Son of Man is Lord even of the sabbath. Later, seeing a man with a withered hand, they asked whether it was lawful to heal on the sabbath, hoping to accuse Him. He replied that any of them would lift a sheep out of a pit on the sabbath — and a man is worth more than a sheep — so it is lawful to do good on the sabbath.

The stories of the cornfields and the man with the withered hand appear in all three Evangelists. Each stresses these and other Pharisee complaints about Jesus neglecting the sabbath, and all present this as the first great provocation that led them to plot His destruction. Mark says this was when Jesus looked round on them with anger, grieved at their hard hearts. Luke returns to the theme repeatedly — in his fourth, thirteenth, and fourteenth chapters. Given how brief the Gospels are, it is striking how much room they give this subject, and how necessary they felt it was to showing what Jesus' life was. But why should that be? The sabbath was one of the most memorable Jewish institutions. If the Pharisees observed it to extravagant excess, we might have expected a great teacher to warn also of the opposite danger, the laxity the Sadducees were probably prone to. This was not a mere tradition of the elders but a positive commandment, like honoring father and mother. Yet the whole weight of Jesus' example and authority seems thrown onto the side of permission and toleration — the side we would think already heaviest.

"The Son of Man," says Matthew, "is Lord also of the sabbath-day." "The sabbath was made for man," says Mark, "not man for the sabbath; therefore the Son of Man is Lord of the sabbath." Luke repeats these last words, tying them directly to David eating the shewbread. Here the Evangelists themselves explain why they weigh these dialogues so heavily. He who had instituted the sabbath, and to whom it had borne witness, had come to claim His own dominion over it and to declare what He had meant by it. The Pharisees denied that lordship and set aside that meaning entirely. Their fault was not an error of excess but a fatal contradiction of the whole nature of this commandment, and of every commandment. They had no sense that the sabbath was made for man; so how could they feel the force of "Thou shalt rest, and thy man-servant, and thy maid-servant, and the stranger that is within thy gates"? The Pharisee cared nothing for the servant or the stranger; he cared only for the day itself, stripped bare of all its life, and to it the servant and the stranger were to be sacrificed. How then could such men see the Son of Man — who came as King and Deliverer of servants and strangers — as Lord of this day? How could they welcome a Person come with such a purpose, when their whole spirit was fixed on the husk of the institution? I say the husk, not the letter, for they could not read the letter. The letter witnessed against them: it spoke of the relation between man and God, of God sharing man's labor and man sharing God's rest — the very truth they denied when they called Christ a blasphemer. All the hidden inhumanity and real atheism of the Pharisee lay wrapped up in his feelings about the sabbath. No wonder they plotted against Christ for the acts and words that exposed those feelings; no wonder the holiest anger was roused by men turning God's commandment into a practical denial of Him. But all this profound morality, this assertion of the very principle the Pharisees were undermining, becomes meaningless if the Gospels are not what they claim to be: throughout, the history of a Son of God and a King of Men.

#### THE PARABLES.

Almost the whole of the thirteenth chapter of Matthew is given to our Lord's parables. Many more appear in later chapters; Mark records only a few. Nothing so distinguishes Luke from the other two Evangelists as his choice of parables. Three are found in all three Gospels: the Sower, the Mustard-seed, and the Lord of the vineyard and the husbandmen.

I intend to examine each of these, together with as much of the surrounding text as all the Evangelists thought needed to explain them.

I. In every Gospel the Sower is treated as a specimen of this way of teaching, and its explanation is tied to the reason for speaking in parables. So it deserves close attention. From it we may learn whether our Lord's teaching confirms or weakens the conclusion we drew from His baptism, temptation, miracles, and arguments with opponents. We may begin with His answer to the disciples' question prompted by this parable, "Why speakest Thou unto them in parables?" — "Because unto you it is given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven, but unto them it is not given."

These words link the parables to the phrase we have met so often, "the kingdom," which so far has kept a fixed meaning. But here for the first time the word "mysteries" is joined to "kingdom." Joined how? Were the Apostles not to preach the kingdom of God? Was what their Master told them about it secret knowledge, meant only for themselves or for a circle of initiates they would gather? If so, the use of parables could not be squared with the rest of the record — and no charge of pretence or self-glorification laid on the Evangelists would ease that difficulty. Everywhere else they present their Master, in contrast to the learned Jewish doctors, as the Teacher of the poor and ignorant. Here they would be needlessly confessing that they had received special private instruction, which in the same breath they were foolishly making public. And the discourse itself makes this view still harder to hold. The interpretation of the Sower was plainly what they were to make their hearers know *by every possible means*. They were taught the obstacles that keep the Word from taking root and bearing fruit, precisely so they could warn others. He speaks in parables because the people cannot receive His lesson any other way. The thing is hidden from them, and His discourse tells why it is hidden, where the veil lies, and how and by whom it was drawn. The people's own inner life was hidden from them because their hearts had grown gross and their ears dull. The hidden work going on within them was just what they could not perceive. The rule exercised over their own hearts could only be shown them through the outward things their eyes were used to — and even those they saw poorly, often not at all. It was a revelation to remind them of the secret processes by which seed becomes stalk, ear, and full corn. The natural world was almost as a landscape to a blind man's eye; yet they could not be wholly unaware of facts they handled daily. They might be awakened to notice these, to see an order in the familiar works of nature and of man, and thence to discover an order and dominion nearer still to themselves, working always for their discipline and deliverance.

Those, on the other hand, who were groping, however confusedly, for the light that most concerned themselves — who cared less for the earth and its seeds than to be freed from their ignorance and made right and true within, hearts craving guidance, seeking a resting-place, children crying for a Father — these had an immediate hold on the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven; to them it was given to know them. The others saw those mysteries only cast outward into the visible world.

Some such view of these words is, I think, the usual one. I do not want to offer anything new, but only to show how exactly it fits and carries out the idea of a Kingdom whose first and highest work is in man's inner being, but which reaches into every part of his life, through society, and through the whole intellectual and living universe. Suppose the actual King of men had come to make plain the nature of His government — to show what rule He had been exercising over them and to what ends, how it was thwarted, and why they did not perceive it; and suppose that King of men were also King of nature, the Creator and Lord of the world in which man lives, who made that world especially for man, set him at its centre, and ordered it by laws that show themselves fully only as they bear on man's character and acts — then we should see why the Sower is the key to all parables, why it sets forth a mystery of the kingdom of heaven and at the same time a mystery of the kingdom of earth, why it belongs to each man's life and is closely bound to the history of the race.

That the Son of Man plants His word in a voluntary being who can stop it from having its natural effect — this is the plain doctrine of the parable. The matching fact in nature is that someone puts seed in ground whose qualities may make it barren. In both cases we are reminded that fruitfulness is the law and barrenness the anomaly, the result of some perversity — yet a perversity likely to appear, and needing a power beyond the human or natural subject to remove it. Each is meant to receive that power and submit to it; then its hidden capacities are unfolded, and in due season the results become visible. In both the processes are secret and orderly; in both the failure comes from the productive power not reaching deep enough; in both what finally appears on the surface looks quite unlike its cause. Hold these great landmarks of the discourse together in your mind, and you reach such a sense of the reality of the distinction and the resemblance between the two great spheres of our universe as no definition or philosophical scheme can give. And you feel at the same time that these spheres must be under a Person's authority, that "Kingdom" applies to them literally, not by metaphor, and that no other word could replace it without being feeble and awkward.

II. I said the Sower concerns the life of the race as well as the individual. If it is the history of the Divine Will working on human wills, it cannot be confined to particular experiences; the history of man must be in it. Still, I readily grant the common feeling that it bears less obviously on the movements and growth of human society than some other parables. The Mustard-seed I gladly admit is, as commonly said, a prophecy of the future unfolding of the Church as a whole. But note that the character of this unfolding is essentially the same as before. The mustard-tree was not the greatest or stateliest of trees; its size is not the point. The point is clearly the growth, first of a herb, then of a tree, out of the smallest of seeds. Underground processes are implied here too. The Apostles had already heard the Sower and taken in something of its spirit; they would apply its interpretation here as well, supposing that this seed, like the corn, was sown and watched by a Divine husbandman, planted in human spirits, and that these were somehow brought into such unity that they could be pictured as one great tree with many branches. I do not suppose elaborate reasoning went on in the minds of Galilean fishermen. The beauty of this teaching was that no such reasoning was needed: the natural image joined to their inner experience took its place. The Divine Teacher had already written His lessons on the visible world; He had only to open His disciples' eyes to read them there. The one tree with its branches, and the birds lodging in them, though only an imperfect picture of spiritual unity and its diverse living forms, came far closer to that reality than any school discussions of unity could. The image showed that no artificial combination of persons and wills — like no combination of leaves and branches — could produce the oneness; it must have a root beneath the soil, an original unity as the ground of the diversity, not its result. So the Mustard-seed shows the kingdom of heaven under a new aspect, yet with the same essential mark: there is One who rules, in whom it consists, from whose secret life all its varied energies come.

III. Still, one element of the Gospel narratives seems missing from both these parables. The sower is said to be the Son of Man; the mustard-seed's growth is a kingdom's growth, implying a king. But is this Son of Man, this King of men, the Son of God? Can we say such a person is declared the source of all the agencies at work in human society and in nature? There is a third parable common to the three Evangelists, which each stresses strongly as the one that struck most directly at the consciences of the chief priests and scribes, which they felt was aimed at them, and which stirred them to seek revenge. A vineyard was let to husbandmen; the owner sent servant after servant to ask for the fruits; one was beaten, another stoned. Last he sent his son, thinking they would respect him. But the husbandmen said, "This is the heir; come, let us kill him, and seize his inheritance," and they cast him out of the vineyard and killed him. When the owner comes, what will he do to them? They answer that he will destroy those wicked men and let the vineyard to others who will render the fruits in their seasons. Jesus then asks them whether they had never read, "The stone which the builders rejected, the same is become the head of the corner? this was the Lord's doing, and it is marvellous in our eyes."

This passage, you will see, agrees strikingly with all we have already considered. (1) A Son comes after a succession of servants, yet it is plainly meant that the Son was before the servants. The first agent in all God's works declared Himself last; the power to whom all the rest were subordinate, and from whom they came, was revealed. (2) He came, like those before Him, to seek the fruits of the vineyard — or, as our Lord interprets it, to see whether those entrusted with the kingdom of God were worthy of the trust. (3) The crowning act of rebellion sprang from wanting to claim the inheritance. The husbandmen would not acknowledge the Son because they would not acknowledge a Father. They wished to seize as their own what belonged to Him, which they could hold only as the son held it — by faith in Him, by renouncing themselves. (4) So this parable, like John's preaching, like our Lord's own preaching, His discourses, and His miracles, looks forward to a judgment near at hand, declaring that the axe is laid to the root of the tree, and that, bearing no good fruit, it will be hewn down and burned. The more we look into these parables — those found in the first three Gospels but not the fourth — the more the truth comes home that the Evangelists' first aim was not to show us a human teacher with a set of maxims and examples, but to declare a King of men and a Son of God, who came to reveal the kingdom implied in the Jewish kingdom, and to overthrow the one by establishing the other.

#### HEROD HEARING OF CHRIST.

*The passage:* At that time Herod the tetrarch heard of the reports about Jesus and told his servants: this is John the Baptist, risen from the dead, and that is why these mighty works are at work in him.

This announcement, with some variations, appears in all three of the first Evangelists; in the first two the story of John's death is added to it. I bring it in because I do not want to pass over any passage the three share, even if it seems trivial. I do not think this one is trivial, especially when it is joined to a passage we shall meet shortly, and to some we shall look at later in the fourth Gospel. The question of the relation between our Lord and John the Baptist bears in a striking way on the history of early Christian opinions, and touches closely on our subject. I will say no more of it here, except this: Herod's wish to connect Christ with John, or with some old Prophet, shows the restless fear that ran through his whole family—the fear that the teacher might turn out to be a king. They would happily let the people entertain any notion, even the loftiest, about a favorite teacher, so long as it spared them that dangerous alternative.

#### THE SIGN FROM HEAVEN.

*The passage:* The Pharisees and Sadducees came to test Jesus, asking Him to show them a sign from heaven. He answered that they could read the sky — red at evening means fair weather, red and lowering in the morning means storms — yet they could not read the signs of the times. He called them hypocrites: a wicked and adulterous generation looks for a sign, and none will be given it but the sign of the prophet Jonah. Then He left them. Mark records the same event, adding that Jesus sighed deeply and asked why this generation seeks a sign, saying none would be given it. Luke gives the corresponding words, that as Jonah was a sign to the Ninevites, so shall the Son of Man be to this generation.

This matter of signs comes up so often, and the Evangelists stress it so strongly, that it deserves careful thought. The Pharisees clearly believed they were asking for a proof quite unlike anything Jesus had already given them. Healing the sick and casting out devils did not count, in their minds, as adequate proof of His power. It showed He had *some* power, but was it power from *heaven*? To settle that, there had to be a visible token in the *heavens* showing He came from there and drew His power from there. This is the crucial test that reveals what they thought heaven was. However their rabbis divided the first, second, and third heaven, all of these had the same sense-bound character. However they condemned heathen idolatry, they were themselves worshipping a mere God of nature, a cloud-driver, as much as any Greek did. What they wanted was a visible sign in a visible sky; that alone counted with them as a sign from God. Must not He who came preaching the Kingdom of heaven, His Father's Kingdom, have sighed deeply when the very people chosen from all nations to witness to the true God showed they could no longer recognize any God but a God of the senses? His words say exactly what that sigh meant: a wicked and adulterous generation seeks a sign — a generation wholly given over to the senses and to self, unable to look on heaven except as a shadow cast by the earth, which alone seemed to them real. And the words "no sign shall be given them but the sign of Jonah the prophet" match exactly what we have found in the preaching of the Baptist and of Jesus. Whatever else the sign of Jonah may mean, its first and plainest sense is the one these passages give, the one Matthew and Luke put forward most prominently, and the one that explains Mark's flat "no sign" — for if the only sign given was Jonah's, then no sign would be given in the sense the Pharisees wanted. Jonah came preaching repentance to the Ninevites, calling them to turn from their evil ways or see their long-standing city destroyed. That marked him as a prophet from heaven. The people accepted him because he spoke to their consciences and exposed their evil. The scribes and Pharisees would not accept *this* mark of a prophet. The other kind of sign would come to them in due time: there would be signs in heaven and earth that the city of David was to be left desolate, a horror and a hissing to all peoples. This discourse about signs is thus important for showing the purpose of the three Evangelists, and for showing how far they were from being the crude materialists that the clever men of our day suppose them to have been.

#### THE LEAVEN OF THE SECTS.

*The passage:* Jesus warned His disciples to take heed and beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and of the Sadducees.

The warning is reported somewhat differently in each Gospel. Matthew names the Pharisees and Sadducees. Mark (chap. ix. ver. 15) names "the leaven of the Pharisees and of the leaven of Herod." Luke (chap. xii. ver. 1) speaks of only one sect: "Beware ye of the leaven of the Pharisees, which is hypocrisy." In Matthew and Mark the warning is tied to the disciples' having forgotten to bring bread; they imagined He was afraid of poison. In Luke the words belong to a general discourse, and the reason given is that nothing covered will stay hidden, but all will be revealed and known.

The word "leaven" might naturally have suggested to the disciples a suspicion that was not wholly groundless, given what they knew of these sects' practices and their special malice against Jesus. Yet the use of the word in their own Scriptures, read in light of the Passover, should have shown them—had they entered into their Master's spirit—that this was not the meaning He most likely intended. He rebukes them directly for not trusting Him to give them the bread they needed, after all the proof of His power they had seen; and He rebukes them by implication for seeing nothing in its inward meaning and everything in its coarse, fleshly sense. In doing so He also makes clearer what mixture the Pharisees, Sadducees, and Herodians all introduced into their teaching, however sharply they differed and opposed one another. The Pharisee's leaven was especially, as Luke says, hypocrisy. Ordinary bread is leavened, yet leaven is thought to make it less pure—it gives the other elements consistency, but in a sense by destroying their proper qualities. So the Pharisee mixed the earthly with the heavenly until the whole substance was changed. God's Law and Covenant were meant to separate a man from his evil nature and bring him under a new principle; the Pharisee instead fused the Law and Covenant with that evil nature, using its motives and tendencies to hold together the maxims of the Divine Law and make it work. So his whole life, outwardly consistent, became one great practical contradiction. He was an actor. A mask made in imitation of the true, living, divine Form took its place. His inner being died a little more each day, because what should have kept it alive had itself been turned into the instrument of its destruction. To the Pharisee the words applied above all: what is hidden shall be known, what is covered shall be revealed. The secret ground of his heart, the inner man growing ever more unknown to itself, buried under outward practices and hidden by fleshly desires and religious self-deceit, would be brought into the open. The man would be exposed to himself, and by his evil acts to the world; and a clear line would be drawn between the divine bread he had defiled and the leaven he had mixed into it. All this fit the Pharisee most exactly—he was emphatically *the* hypocrite, since no other sect recognized, and so perverted, divine spiritual principles in the same way or degree. But the Sadducee and the Herodian were each, in his own way, also leavening the divine food given for the nourishment of themselves and all Israel. The one did it with the maxims of merely earthly, prudential morality, the other with the rules of state-expediency, both turning God's righteousness into a human system useless for guiding men, serving their pride, and favoring dishonesty. They too were hypocrites, though not religious ones like the Pharisee. Each had a reputation to keep up—the Sadducee for wisdom and freedom from vulgar prejudice, the Herodian for political shrewdness and for wanting to preserve Jewish custom and religion alongside submission to the Cæsars. Each tried to blend contradictions, to reconcile the traditions of their fathers with their own narrow, corrupt, grovelling aims. This was the leaven the disciples had to beware of. It belonged to no single sect or opinion, but was sure to debase anyone who did not take pains to shut it out; no notions or practices guarded against it. They could be saved from it only by remembering that they had a Lawgiver and King always with them, who was working to separate the chaff from the wheat—the part of them that wanted truth from the part that clung to earth and nature, the divine seed from the evil seed an enemy had sown in their hearts.

#### PETER'S CONFESSION.

*The passage:* When Jesus came to the region of Cæsarea Philippi, He asked His disciples what people said He, the Son of Man, was. They answered that some said John the Baptist, some Elias, some Jeremias or one of the prophets. Then He asked what they themselves said He was, and Peter answered, "Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God." All three Evangelists give nearly the same account. In Mark the answer is simply "Thou art the Christ"; in Luke, "Thou art the Christ of God." All three agree that He then charged His disciples to tell no one that He was the Christ.

No one can doubt how much weight each Evangelist gives this story, and I think there is little disagreement about its main meaning. As all the Gospels indicate — Matthew most clearly — Peter had somehow reached a conviction that the people generally did not share, even those who thought most highly of Jesus. They could believe He was a prophet, or a miraculous figure, or someone long dead returned, or John the Baptist raised with new powers. But the idea of a Son of God, the Christ of God, had not occurred to them; they could not say He was this. And the disciples were told not to make it known — He strictly charged them not to. Why? Was it not the very purpose of John the Baptist, and of the miracles, parables, and life of Jesus, to declare the Christ? Yes — to declare the King who was ruling *invisibly* over men's hearts and spirits. So while He was visibly present, He was not to be *proclaimed* as the Christ; announcing it that way would have lost the whole power and mystery of the words. In acts of healing love, in the Gospel to the poor, in His smallest acts, in His face, the truth came out as truth. Turned into a bare proposition and offered to carnal hearts, it would have been turned outward and made false. The time for that form would come later, when the preacher's main message would be "Jesus is the Christ." For now the message was "Repent, for the Kingdom of Heaven is at hand," and the disciple was to make the nature of that kingdom felt. To do more would be to set up the kingdom of a Herod or a Cæsar, not of a Christ. One can see how well Matthew's words fit this — "Flesh and blood have not revealed it unto thee, but my Father which is in Heaven" — and equally how the rebuke fits, "Get thee behind me, Satan; for thou savorest not the things that be of God, but the things that be of men," spoken when the same Peter showed he still could not reconcile the King with the sufferer. I will not go into the famous words about the rock, since they belong only to Matthew, nor dwell on the rebuke of Peter, since Luke does not mention it. I say this much because the very next passage, common to all three and linked by them all to the question at Cæsarea Philippi, holds the essence of that rebuke. I hope I have shown, without adding anything new to the usual explanation, that these three Evangelists were possessed by the sense that the Kingdom they spoke of was, in the highest sense, spiritual, and that they never thought of Jesus as anything other than the Son of God.

#### TAKING UP THE CROSS.

*The passage:* Jesus told His disciples that anyone who would follow Him must deny himself, take up his cross, and come after Him. Whoever tries to save his life will lose it; whoever loses his life for Christ's sake will find it. What good is it to gain the whole world and lose your own soul? What could a man trade for his soul? For the Son of Man will come in His Father's glory with His angels, and reward each person according to what he has done. And some standing there would not die before they saw the Son of Man coming in His Kingdom. The three Evangelists agree here more closely than almost anywhere else.
