The only notable differences are that in Matthew Jesus speaks to His disciples, in Mark He calls the crowd along with His disciples, and in Luke He speaks "to them all" — apparently meaning the disciples; and that Mark and Luke add here the important words, "Whosoever shall be ashamed of me and of My words, of him shall the Son of Man be ashamed," words that had already appeared in a slightly different form in Matthew's tenth chapter.

No passage is quoted more often than this, as though it were a stand-alone maxim or proverb; but I think its meaning can hardly be grasped if we forget where it stands in all the Gospels — between Peter's confession and the Transfiguration. It is striking how each Gospel brings in the title Son of *Man* right after the declaration, "Thou art the Son of *God*." This shows, more plainly than any words could, the very nature of our Lord's own self-denial, and so points to what He asks of His followers. He accepts His Apostle's confession as true and rejoices in it; yet at that very moment He shows that the glory of the Son of God consists in giving up all self-subsisting greatness, in choosing to have nothing of His own, in sharing man's sufferings and death. How this fits with the story of the Temptation, and how it bears on the Baptism, will, I think, commend itself to everyone's conscience. But the point I chiefly want to press is the difference between the morality of the passage seen this way and the morality of it taken as an independent maxim that tells Christ's disciples to look after their souls and count nothing so precious as themselves. No one who reveres Him can deny that obeying His words in their fullest sense saves the soul, and forgetting them loses it. But we must not overlook His actual language in order to reach that result faster by a notion of our own. This very passage says that whoever saves his soul shall lose it. I will not settle how we ought to render ψυχή; but I do say that however we read it in one part of the passage, we must read it the same way in another. We cannot make it animal life where He speaks of losing it, and something wholly different, even opposite, where He speaks of saving it. In some sense He tells us that everything belonging to us — animal life, intellectual life, spiritual life, our very selves — must be given up and lost if we would have it saved, and if we would be His disciples. To use this passage as a ground for constantly urging men simply to seek the safety of their souls involves a dangerous contradiction, and risks setting aside both the letter and the spirit of the command. But if the whole context is kept in view — if Christ, the King of men and the Son of God, is truly seen as the great self-denier, not because these glories were not His but because they were, and because He could only assert and display them by glorifying His Father and giving up Himself — then the self-denial of His disciples is seen to consist likewise in giving up everything that is individually theirs, owning it as wholly their Lord's, realized, possessed, and enjoyed only in Him.

#### THE TRANSFIGURATION.

*The passage:* Six days later Jesus took Peter, James, and John up a high mountain by themselves, and was transfigured before them. His face shone like the sun and His clothes became as white as light. Moses and Elijah appeared, talking with Him. Peter said, "Lord, it is good for us to be here; if you wish, let me build three shelters, one for you, one for Moses, one for Elijah." While he spoke, a bright cloud covered them, and a voice from the cloud said, "This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased; hear Him." The disciples fell on their faces in fear, but Jesus touched them and said, "Arise, do not be afraid." When they looked up they saw only Jesus.

However hard commentators have worked to fit the Gospels into schemes of passovers and journeys to and from Galilee, they have not been able to miss that this event, supernatural as it is, is one of the great landmarks in the three Evangelists—one all of them felt bound to record, and which they saw not as standing apart from the history but as explaining both what comes before and what follows. No wonder the follower of Strauss seizes on such an event as proof that myth is mixed with ordinary earthly narrative. On his starting assumption it must simply be discarded as having nothing to do with the life of Jesus of Nazareth—only one of those grand inventions by which the human spirit has expressed its sense of its own dignity and deified itself. But anyone who has followed my inquiry so far will ask whether every narrative we have looked at does not rest on the same principle as this one, and must stand or fall with it. And anyone who studies the narrative itself, without leaning toward either Strauss's scheme or mine, will ask whether the human spirit ever found so strange a way of expressing its reverence, for itself or for a hero, as what appears here. There is no pomp of words in any of the Gospels, no attempt to startle the reader by the writer's own excitement; the language is orderly, calm, plain to the point of bareness—yet it is such a description as the greatest painters have felt held an awe they scarcely dared to render. The vision is preceded by words of humiliation and nothingness and followed by words and acts of the same kind; in its outward form it is fleeting, almost momentary, leaving behind only the words heard at the Baptism, "This is my beloved Son," and those other words showing that His Word, which spoke to the inward heart, was mightier and more divine than the face shining like the sun or the garments shining like light—indeed, that it was the invisible, inward glory that produced the change in the bodily form which the eye could hardly bear. If this passage were one of the exaggerated departures from the ordinary story that Strauss supposes, it is strange that the crude, blundering Galileans, the earthly, sense-bound Hebrew, managed to keep so entirely to the style of ordinary speech and to avoid all inflation; stranger still that the writer seems afraid to linger on this outward manifestation, and eager to show that the greatness of the Son of God rested on higher proofs than these. But if we grant that the purpose of all these narratives is to set forth the Son of God—declared throughout as possessing a power that begins in the innermost region and is meant to spread through to the most outward and sensible, renewing the human form and at last the whole earth with the heavenly life poured into it—then to omit a passage like this would be a mark of weakness and incompleteness; the heart would look for something it did not find, and the Gospels would not truly be Gospels of the Kingdom of God.

#### THE EPILEPTIC BOY.

*The passage:* A man knelt before Jesus, begging mercy for his son, who was lunatic and badly afflicted, often falling into fire or water. The man had brought the boy to the disciples, but they could not cure him. Jesus said, "O faithless and perverse generation, how long shall I be with you? How long shall I suffer you? Bring him here to me." He rebuked the devil, it left the boy, and the child was cured from that hour.

Raphael's fine instinct saw at once that this event had to be joined with the very different scene we have just discussed. He felt that both the unity of place and the unity of time had to be given up for the sake of a deeper and more mysterious unity that all three Evangelists had seen — the unity that made them set the earthly crowd and the faithless disciples at the foot of the mountain as part of the same picture with the still and awful scene at its top. If the painter broke the formal rules of his art, he did so, I think, in obedience to a higher principle of art — not to make a glaring contrast, but to give reality to our sense of the Transfiguration and to connect it with ourselves. This was his daring experiment. All rules of art rest, I suppose, on some ground deeper than themselves, which they point to but cannot reach. And the theological truth embodied in this meeting of opposites lies at the very heart of Christianity: a truth words cannot express, seen fully only in the life of Christ, which we can grasp even faintly only when we acknowledge that the one who suffers alongside man and delivers man is, first and in His inmost being, the well-beloved Son of God. This illustration of the principle I am pressing is too remarkable to leave out. But since I have already spoken of our Lord's miracles in general, I have no reason to dwell on the details of this one, striking as its circumstances are.

#### PROPHECY OF THE PASSION.

*The passage:* While they were staying in Galilee, Jesus told them that the Son of Man would be handed over to men, who would kill Him, and that on the third day He would rise again. And they were very sorrowful.

Connected with this narrative is another of Christ's prophecies of His death and resurrection, kept by Matthew, Mark, and Luke, and apparently placed by all of them at the same time. The words of all three evangelists — St. Mark and St. Luke even more than St. Matthew — leave no doubt that they saw the Transfiguration as the appointed prelude, not to a single discourse, but to a whole series of discourses on this subject; that from this point on it became the main theme of our Lord's teaching among His own disciples; and that this prepared them for His final entrance into Jerusalem. Anyone who compares the brief passages about this period will, I think, agree that even for the chronology of the Gospels they are the most important we can find. I do not claim to shed any light on that chronology, but I am convinced the light must come from where commentators have looked for it least — from the parts of the narrative that are most plainly supernatural and heavenly.

#### THE GREATEST IN THE KINGDOM OF HEAVEN.

*The passage:* The disciples came to Jesus and asked who is greatest in the Kingdom of Heaven. He called a little child, set him among them, and said that unless they turned and became like little children they would not enter the Kingdom; whoever humbles himself like this child is the greatest, and whoever receives one such child in his name receives him.

The same conversation appears in Mark, at the ninth chapter, verse thirty-three, where the occasion is put a little differently: in the house Jesus asked what they had been disputing on the road, and they were silent, because they had argued which of them should be greatest. Luke, in the ninth chapter, verse forty-six, follows Mark with a slight change: a reasoning arose among them about who should be greatest, and Jesus, perceiving the thought of their hearts, took a child. In Matthew the discourse begins from the child's face and winds on for some time; in Mark and Luke it is soon broken off by John's words, that they had seen someone casting out devils in Jesus' name and had forbidden him. References both to the disciples' dispute and to the subject itself occur often in the Evangelists—one very memorable instance in Luke's account of the Paschal Supper. But the face of the child shows that these three narratives refer to the same conversation, even where details of time and place do not agree.

John, our Lord, and the disciples he sent out had all proclaimed that the Kingdom of Heaven was at hand. Anyone would expect such an announcement to work powerfully on those who heard it, and still more on those who had spoken it: the Kingdom has been at hand a long while—is it not now actually coming? The Transfiguration, preceded by the words that some standing there would not taste death till they saw the Kingdom of God coming with power, must have greatly heightened the expectation of its speedy appearance, and the warnings about their Master's rejection and death, which the Apostles could not understand, would hardly quiet it. So everyone has seen in the Gospels signs of a growing impatience in the disciples to know when the promise would be fulfilled and what their own places would be when the new reign began. And nearly everyone has seen that our Lord's discourses, especially once he set his face toward Jerusalem, were aimed at just this feeling. But the point most worth noting is that he never once changes the language he had used before. He never suggests that it is not a Kingdom in the strict ordinary sense that he has come to set up. He only frees his Apostles from certain common but perfectly natural notions about a kingdom, which they shared with most of their countrymen—Pharisees as much as Publicans—and with most people in every age and land. They tied to the idea of a kingdom the triumph of the few over the many, rivalry among those few, and the dominance of one. These have indeed always been the accidents of earthly kingdoms, but they were its destructive accidents. No kingdom ever lasted by the ambition of the few, the weakness of the many, the rivalry of the great, or the eventual tyranny of the shrewd or lucky chief. It lasted by its power to resist these forces, which were always working to overthrow it, by the higher impulses stirring even in selfish citizens and leading them to seek its good at the cost of their own. If the rule of a single tyrant was felt to be better than the anarchy leading to it, that is because the tyrant was less selfish than the crowd, did more work for the whole, was more its servant. So the law that the greatest of all is the servant of all had really been the law of all society, implied in its very existence; all wrong came from breaking it. The eagerness to break it, to treat it as the principle of human fellowship, was precisely the lie of the evil nature and the evil spirit, the thing that all tyranny and falsehood lived upon, and the thing the Son of God must come to cast out. So when he took a child and set him among his disciples, he did not say he was founding a kingdom to be called the kingdom of Jesus, or the Christian kingdom, which could set itself up and boast that it was unlike all kingdoms and came to overthrow them. He said, in keeping with all he had said before, that he came to reveal the kingdom lying beneath all other kingdoms and implied in the existence of all; that the true King, who is the true servant of all, had appeared to show what he was and what his government meant, and to make some of those who rule under him aware of their own true position—that true rule means subjection, that selfish rivalry is a contradiction—and so ready to feel with him that becoming little children, giving up supremacy, and owning their weakness is the one way to make the government he exercises through them feared, loved, and obeyed.

#### DIVORCE.

*The passage:* Anyone who divorces his wife (except for unfaithfulness) and marries another commits adultery; and whoever marries a woman so divorced commits adultery.

This saying is joined in Matthew and Mark to a discussion of marriage; in Luke it comes, strikingly, between the parable of the unjust steward and that of Dives and Lazarus. I may look later, when I treat the differences among the Evangelists, at how it relates to those discourses. Here I only note that in all three the Pharisees prompted the remark, and that in all three it bears on the nature and purpose of the Law. In two of them the Pharisees say Moses allowed a man to give his wife a certificate of divorce and send her away; and Jesus answers that Moses gave this rule because of the hardness of their hearts, but that from the beginning it was not so. Luke reports it differently: the Law and the Prophets were until John, and since then the kingdom of God is preached and everyone presses into it; it is easier for heaven and earth to pass away than for one small stroke of the law to fail — and then the words about divorce follow.

So this is plainly set out as a striking case that shows the relation between the Law of Moses and the Kingdom of Heaven. The Pharisees, who often charged Jesus with relaxing the Law, here suspected that in this one matter He made it stricter. He does not deny it. He condemns the use of a privilege they had allowed themselves and that Moses had permitted. And He does this in exact agreement with the principle He had laid down about the sabbath, where He was so often accused of laxity. There was no laxity in either case. He asserted the true idea and principle of the sabbath, as expressed in the commandment itself, and insisted that keeping this meaning was essential to keeping the commandment: forget the meaning and the day was not kept, even if no ear of corn was plucked. In the same way He asserted the principle and meaning of marriage, which was assumed in the very law that sanctioned divorce. The permission was granted for their hard hearts — a measure for an evil emergency, which the Pharisees, mistaking mere decrees and statutes for laws, had confused with one of the primary and inviolable institutions of human life. People were pressing into the Kingdom of Heaven, hoping to find an easier yoke than the letter-worshippers had laid on them. They would find it easier, because it was the yoke the human heart and spirit were made to wear — a yoke that is the pledge of freedom, not of servitude. They would not find it easier if they were looking for mere licenses and exemptions. It would not cause one small stroke of the Law to fail. It brought to light, re-established, and set on its deepest foundation all that belonged to the true order of humanity. The right actions that laws, in their weakness, could not compel would come forth from the life of the Lawgiver; and the irregularities the Law was forced to tolerate He would undermine.

#### BLESSING THE LITTLE CHILDREN.

*The passage:* People brought little children to Jesus so that He might lay His hands on them and pray. The disciples rebuked them, but Jesus said to let the children come to Him and not to hinder them, for the Kingdom of Heaven belongs to such as these. He laid His hands on them and went away.

There is a sentimental interest that has gathered around this record, and I would be sorry to see it stripped away, though I wish it rested on something more solid. People think all the Evangelists felt bound to include it because it was such a proof of the human sympathy and tenderness of Jesus. And so it was; the higher our idea of Him, the more precious every such token of His real humanity becomes. But we have no right to read into these writers a purpose other than their own words state. They tell us He said, "Of such is the Kingdom of Heaven." It is because He said this that the incident deserves a place in the record of the life of the Son of God on earth. Blessing little children was part of the revelation of the Kingdom of Heaven. That Kingdom would consist of such — not of men putting on the airs of children, pretending to become children by giving up the intellectual powers of men, pretending to be simple and so being, at the deepest level, artificial: without a child's frankness, openness, or trust, full of craft and subtlety because they will not and dare not be manly. Not such, but real little children, ready to receive, opening every avenue of the spirit to receive, full of wonder, aware of their own ignorance, craving knowledge and light, believing that all treasures are meant for them — the treasures of earth and heaven, even the infinite wisdom and love of God Himself. As has often been said, the man of the deepest science is, and must be, a little child: he must stop seeing himself reflected in the things around him and be content to see everything as it reveals itself by its own light, not as measured and colored by his. Of such, then, must the Kingdom of Heaven be — only those who can take everything as a gift, who think of the object rather than their own seeing or believing, of Him who works in them rather than their own acts, can be true brethren and fellow-citizens of Him who did not glorify Himself, but His Father who sent Him.

#### THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE RICH MAN.

*The passage:* A man came to Jesus asking what good thing he must do to have eternal life. Jesus asked why he called Him good, since only God is good, and told him that to enter life he must keep the commandments. Asked which, Jesus named the commands against murder, adultery, theft, and false witness, and to honor parents and love one's neighbor. The young man said he had kept all these from his youth and asked what he still lacked. Jesus told him that to be perfect he should sell what he had, give to the poor, and follow Him, and he would have treasure in heaven. Hearing this, the man went away sorrowful, for he was rich. Jesus then told His disciples that a rich man can hardly enter the kingdom of heaven — easier for a camel to pass through a needle's eye. The amazed disciples asked who then could be saved, and Jesus said that what is impossible for man is possible for God.

The three Evangelists agree closely in reporting this meeting and what followed. Readers have always felt it holds an important place in the Gospel history. Much has been said about it, but its meaning is not exhausted; it still repays examination in its context and its details.

Most people must have noticed the link between the way the young man framed his question and the weakness Jesus exposed in him. "What good thing shall I do, that I may inherit eternal life?" was exactly the thought of a man used to measuring everything by market standards, who naturally used market language even about life and the kingdom of heaven. Jesus' reply, "Why callest thou me good? There is none good but One," was aimed at this state of mind. He seized on a phrase the man used only conventionally to set before him what he really needed, something no market standard could measure: God alone is good; to know Him is the life you seek. But how is that life obtained? Here came another humbling lesson: you already have the commandments, given for this very end. They point out the things that keep you from knowing God, the acts and tendencies that put you at a distance from Him. Keep them, hold them fast, shape yourself by them, and you will be on the way to knowing their Giver, the true Good, the true Life. But the man had kept them all from his youth. Was that a false boast? We are not told so; everything points the other way. He had kept them, but not with a view to the end Jesus set before him. He had kept them habitually, by instinct; and as he grew more purposeful, he kept them hoping for a reward. He thought obeying them would buy him blessings in a future life, or rescue him from its punishments — yet he felt more was needed for security. He never put it plainly to himself that he was bargaining with God; his gentle, gracious mind would have recoiled from such words. That was his misfortune: he did not see his own case clearly. He lived in a dim twilight, unaware of his own feelings, ignorant how entirely his idea of heaven was shaped by his earthly circumstances. So he was willing to give something over and above keeping the commandments, to make his claim to that dreamed-of happiness — and his escape from the terrors his conscience spoke of — absolutely certain. Jesus took him at his word: you will give up much to gain heaven; then sell all you have, give to the poor, and follow me. You feel something hinders you from being all you ought and wish to be — you are right; there is such a hindrance, something that twists all your thoughts about right and good and gives them a false object. Give up the thing you love better than God, and you will know what He is; you will have treasure in heaven. Here was the test. He had never known what his heaven was; now he found out. The life he sought was earthly life, though he called it eternal — by "eternal" he meant the endless prolonging of the good he had here. The Evangelists do not tell us the outcome. But they give one cheering hint on which we may build likely guesses: he went away sad. He had come to know himself as never before, and to feel a discontent with himself he had never felt. Good may have come of that sadness. His past keeping of the commandments, and the gracious qualities that St. Mark says Jesus "beheld and loved," may not have been wasted. If he could not smash his own idol, God may have smashed it for him; or he may later have become one of those who called nothing their own and laid all their goods at the Apostles' feet. Of him we know nothing more. This fragment is recorded because Jesus grounded on it the saying, "How hardly shall a rich man enter into the kingdom of God." Why was it so hard? The Apostles were astonished, for who then could be saved? The rich man had time to give to God's service and money to help the poor. If he could not fulfil the duties and earn the prizes of the Divine Kingdom, who could? Their thoughts were perfectly natural — if the Kingdom of Heaven were not something wholly different from what the young ruler, and the Apostles at that time, supposed. But if it was the kingdom John had proclaimed, and that Jesus had shown in all His parables and miracles — a kingdom at hand, throned in the heart and spirit of every person, the reign of the Son of God over creatures made in His image — then it was certain that everything driving them to seek treasures in the outer world, rather than in Him, was a hindrance to acknowledging His rule, a hindrance man cannot overcome, though God can. And if the Kingdom is a universal one, open to people of every rank, then plainly the outward possessions that made one man think himself above another, and unwilling to stand among his brethren, were nearly insurmountable barriers. Unless the rich man could become poor in the most inward and essential sense, he could no more shrink to the size that fits through the narrow gate than a camel could pass through a needle's eye.

#### THE TEMPTATION OF THE POOR MAN.

*The passage:* Peter tells Jesus that they have left everything to follow Him, and asks what they will get for it. Jesus replies that those who have followed Him will, in the regeneration when the Son of Man sits on His throne of glory, sit on twelve thrones judging the twelve tribes of Israel. Everyone who has left home, family, or lands for His Name's sake will receive a hundredfold and inherit everlasting life. But many who are first will be last, and the last first.

The words about the regeneration and the Son of Man on His throne of glory are found only in St. Matthew, and I will look at them later.

The exact wording of Peter's question, "What shall we have therefor?" also belongs only to St. Matthew, and this, I think, accounts for the differences in the rest of the passage. Much of the emphasis of Matthew's story clearly falls on the last verse, which becomes the text of the parable of the laborers in the vineyard. St. Mark does not give Peter's demand the same selfish shape; he only says, "Lo, we have left all, and followed Thee." But since Mark had used that phrase in his 8th chapter, where Peter objected to the prophecy of Christ's humiliation and was sternly rebuked, I take it he meant nearly what the other Evangelists imply. So although Mark leaves out the parable, he still gives the words, "Many that are last shall be first, and the first last," in the same pointed way. St. Luke gives only "Lo, we have left all, and followed Thee," and omits "Many that are first shall be last." Those words appear instead in Luke's 13th chapter, in answer to the question whether few will be saved.

I could not fit these remarks in when speaking of the direct differences among the Evangelists. They help show how seemingly accidental omissions or additions, in accounts that are basically the same, bring out the meaning they share. From all four Evangelists there can be no doubt that our Lord meant to tell Peter that the blessing of those who truly left all for the Gospel could not be overstated: for them there were houses, lands, persecutions, friends, family, and everlasting life in store. But it is just as clear that while Peter was still asking "what shall we *have* therefor?" he himself had not left all. He was bargaining for wages, wanting to gain more than others by his sacrifices. At the end of the day, if that state of mind held, he would be angry when the great Householder gave those who he thought had worked less the same blessing as himself. So the lesson to the poor fishermen is essentially the same as the lesson to the rich ruler, and the two discourses cannot be separated. The disciples were still self-seekers as he was — in a measure, for no one can think they had followed Christ on a calculation. They were drawn after Him by a power, a love they could not resist; they clung to Him and lost themselves in Him, and so far they were already in seed what they later became. But the habit of mind belonging to their country, to the evil nature in every man, and fostered and almost embodied by pharisaic religion, still clung to them. Like the ruler, they still imagined that certain measurable sacrifices would secure a certain measurable happiness; the thinking of the marketplace had not left their view of eternal life. One great lesson comes from the way He answers them: the Divine teacher does not treat men's mistakes by the rule the great human teacher follows — that a stick bent one way must be bent the other. Instead, with both the ruler and His own disciples, He goes all the way with them. He grants that eternal life is gained by sacrifice, and only shows them that the selfishness of their minds is what makes real sacrifice impossible.

#### THE GOING UP TO JERUSALEM.

*The passage:* Going up to Jerusalem, Jesus took the twelve aside on the road and told them: we are going up to Jerusalem, where the Son of Man will be handed over to the chief priests and scribes; they will condemn Him to death, deliver Him to the Gentiles to be mocked, scourged, and crucified, and on the third day He will rise again.

I have already discussed two announcements of the Passion, especially the one after the Transfiguration. But the passage that comes right after the conversation with the ruler and with Peter is introduced in all three Evangelists so pointedly—small phrases in each show how deeply it marked the disciples—that we cannot treat it as mere repetition of what came before. Here is the passage, in which Jesus tells the twelve on the road that they are going up to Jerusalem, where the Son of Man will be betrayed to the chief priests and scribes, condemned to death, handed to the Gentiles to be mocked, scourged, and crucified, and will rise on the third day.

I want you to notice that each Evangelist puts the emphasis on the words, "Behold, we go up to Jerusalem."

Our Lord had already spoken of His passion and resurrection in connection with His own character as the Son of God. But there is another way they must be seen: in connection with the holy city and the people who would reject Him. St. Luke, perhaps, shows us more clearly than the others how constantly this thought was in our Lord's own mind. Yet the more carefully we study the other two as well, the more we find that they too regard the entry into Jerusalem as the crisis in the history of their nation, and therefore of all nations. They never lose sight, in their view of the great Deliverer, of an approaching judgment; they never forget that a baptism of the Holy Ghost is also to be a baptism of fire. We shall see how necessary this thought is when we come to the next step in the narrative.

#### THE DESCENT FROM THE MOUNT OF OLIVES.

*The passage:* As Jesus and his disciples neared Jerusalem at Bethphage on the Mount of Olives, he sent two disciples to fetch an ass and her colt from a nearby village, telling them to say "the Lord hath need of them." This fulfilled the prophet's words: tell the daughter of Zion, your King comes to you, meek, riding on an ass and a colt. The disciples brought the animals, laid their clothes on them, and set Jesus there. A great crowd spread garments and branches in the road, crying "Hosanna to the Son of David; Blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lord." When he entered the city, all Jerusalem was stirred and asked who this was, and the crowd answered that it was Jesus, the prophet of Nazareth in Galilee.

This story appears in John's Gospel as well as in the first three; I set aside John's reasons for including it. Everyone feels how important a place it holds in the synoptic Gospels, and everyone sees that it describes a royal entrance into David's city — an entrance long prepared for, which even at the time the disciples, taking their cue from Jesus himself, regarded as the start of a series of great and solemn events. What this would bring him, why the man who had been only a prophet among Galilean fishermen should invite in his enemies' capital the vengeance he seemed to expect, why, if he had to enter the city, he did not more carefully than ever avoid anything that might rouse the Roman Governor's suspicion or hand the Pharisees a new pretext — all this the disciples might puzzle over in vain. But that this entry stood in close relation to that Kingdom of which all his parables had spoken and all his miracles had illustrated, they could not doubt. If he wanted them to think he was not a king, or a king only in some imaginary, figurative sense, why choose just this moment and this manner to leave an impression on their minds that later events could only deepen?

This is the sort of question we would naturally press on those who read the Gospels with full faith in their genuineness and inspiration, but treat them as the history of a Divine Teacher rather than of a Divine King. Those who take the Evangelists to be common men bound by their nation's superstition into believing that Jesus meant to depose the Caesars and restore David's line in Judea will of course seize on this passage as evidence. And they are entitled to whatever the most exact meaning of the words gives them; they may fairly demand that no vague, uncritical sense be put upon an event the writers clearly thought so important — the one they date their last and greatest period from. If, on close reading of these words from Matthew — the "Ebionite" Evangelist, the one said to retain, beneath all later editing and spiritualizing, the strongest marks of old Jewish materialism — anything is found inconsistent with the idea of a real, present kingdom, a kingdom that was the true fulfilment of David's, yet, precisely because real, present, immediate, Davidic, and Jewish, therefore in the deepest sense spiritual, lying at the root of things, existing in One who from the first had upheld all things by the word of his power; if there is one phrase in Matthew that under the hardest examination clashes with the impression his whole preceding history has left on us — then I grant not only that this story of the descent from the Mount of Olives is itself perplexing, but that it throws all that comes before and after it into confusion.

But there is no such word. Everything here is royal, yet none of the trappings of royalty are present. He calls for the ass, and its owner feels it must be surrendered because the Lord needs it. The ass is the ordinary mount of the old judges, yet it is also the symbol of lowliness. He is greeted with palm branches and hosannas, but by a band of insignificant followers. Turn the story any way you like, take its general impression or its smallest details, and everything bears out the sense of a quiet, unseen power that makes itself felt but does not come with display, that claims the homage of human hearts as the highest tribute it can receive, before it shows those who refuse that homage what master they are surrendering to. This is exactly the teaching we have found in every passage we have studied in these Gospels. If there were a history made of such passages, set in Judea, every step tied to an approaching crisis in the land and its chief city, would we not expect this climax, and feel a gap in the story if it were missing? All Christendom has felt this — expressed in art and in books of devotion, by those meditating the Scriptures for their own good and by those reading them as records of the world's history — that the meekest and lowliest of men entered Jerusalem to say, "I am your Ruler and Lord. Will you own me as such? If not, the stones of your city will cry out; the real, invisible bond that holds them together will be destroyed, and soon not one will be left upon another." I point to this deeply rooted, many-voiced conviction as proof of my first claim: that I am not proposing a novel theory about these Gospels, but only drawing out the truth the conscience of modern Europe has implicitly recognized, and showing that it rests not on symbolic or mystical interpretation but is the only reading compatible with the plain sense of these books, the only one that explains how their parts connect. What has kept this belief from its full power, I think, is the want of a fuller, franker acknowledgment that Christ is the King of Men, and not merely the King of the particular portion of men allowed to bear his Name. Our selfishness has robbed him of more than half his glory, and threatens at last to deprive us of the blessing we have refused to share. But I am anticipating what is still to come.

#### JESUS GOING INTO THE TEMPLE.

The last passage was one of the few places where the part of St. John's Gospel before the passion agrees with the other three Evangelists; the passage that follows contains almost the only striking apparent disagreement. St. John seems to place the story of Jesus entering the Temple and driving out those who bought and sold there at the passover following His first miracle, while the other three all connect it with His final entry into Jerusalem. St. Matthew's account matches theirs: Jesus went into the temple, drove out the buyers and sellers, overturned the tables of the money-changers and the seats of those selling doves, and said that His house should be called a house of prayer, but they had made it a den of thieves. All three Evangelists show that this act made a strong impression on the chief priests, and all seem to link it with the question, "By what authority doest thou these things?" and the parable that arose from it. Matthew and Mark include the barren fig-tree — a miracle everyone has felt to be closely tied to the withering of the Jewish nation — while Luke, who had just described Jesus weeping over the city, puts the same meaning into words instead. But why should entering the temple and claiming a right to cleanse it strike the priests as such a bold assertion of authority? Why should the Evangelists connect it with deeds and words that pointed to a coming catastrophe? I believe the rest of the narrative explains these feelings. If riding into Jerusalem on an ass was a claim to the kingly honor He had seemed to refuse before, entering the temple was an equally significant claim to be the divine Son — a title His disciples had acknowledged but were as yet forbidden to proclaim. The first act gave evidence for the charge before Pilate; the second proved to the chief priest that the Sanhedrim must condemn Him as a blasphemer. To call the Temple His Father's house, and claim the right to drive out its invaders because it was His Father's house — was this not the clearest proof that He had committed the offence the law punished with stoning? So the parable I have already considered, earlier only as an example of parables in general, bears most directly on this very act: the Son had come to claim the fruits of the vineyard from the husbandmen, and the stone that was the foundation and corner-stone of the Temple had been brought in, so that it might be seen how the builders would treat it.

#### THE BAPTISM OF JOHN.

*The passage:* When Jesus came into the temple and was teaching, the chief priests and elders asked him by what authority he did these things, and who gave it to him. He said he would ask them one question, and if they answered it he would tell them: the baptism of John — was it from heaven or from men? They reasoned that if they said "from heaven," he would ask why they had not believed John; but if they said "from men," they feared the people, who all held John to be a prophet. So they said they could not tell, and Jesus said he would not tell them by what authority he acted either.

I must not pass over these words, common to all three Evangelists, that introduce the parable. Part of their value, as everyone has seen, lies in the general law they witness to: those who have not benefited from an earlier, preparatory stage make themselves unfit for a higher one. But we must not forget that it is the chief priests who are singled out here, set apart from — even in contrast with — the people at large; in Matthew's Gospel, set in contrast even with the coarser part of the people, the publicans and harlots. So this passage should be compared with John's words to the Scribes and Pharisees when they came out to his baptism: "Oh generation of vipers, who hath warned you to flee from the wrath to come? Think not to say within yourselves, We have Abraham to our father; for God is able of these stones to raise up children unto Abraham." That sentence exposes the whole sin of the Pharisees, both the height and the meanness of their ambition. They were proud of being Abraham's children and thought that privilege could never be taken from them; they did not care to be God's children. They preferred the lower honor kept to themselves over the higher honor shared with people they regarded as mere stones. So they were wrapped up in a religious atheism, content without any sense that they stood in a relation to God. John called them to repent of this sin, which proved they had forgotten the blessings of their own covenant. They were to turn from Abraham to the God of Abraham; not to glory in their strength or wisdom, but in knowing Him who executes righteousness and judgment on the earth. If they did that, He would reveal Himself to them: there was One among them who would baptize them with the Holy Ghost and with fire. The chief priests had not heeded that call, and so they could not know by what authority Christ did these things. They wanted to be rulers themselves in the temple, at the moment when its true Lord had suddenly come to refine and purify it. They did not know who He was, but they sensed that He was one they ought to obey; and so they said, "Come, let us kill him, and the inheritance shall be ours."

#### PAYING TRIBUTE TO CÆSAR.

*The passage:* The Pharisees plotted to trap Jesus in his words, sending their disciples with the Herodians. They flattered him as a truthful teacher who plays no favorites, then asked whether it is lawful to pay tribute to Cæsar. Jesus, seeing their wickedness, called them hypocrites and asked to see the coin. When they showed him a penny, he asked whose image and inscription it bore. They said Cæsar's. He told them to give Cæsar what is Cæsar's, and to God what is God's.

The parallel accounts are in Mark 12 and Luke 20. The three Evangelists differ very little here. The largest difference is that Matthew puts the parable of the king's marriage feast for his son between the parable of the husbandmen and the sending of the spies, while Mark and Luke trace the plot directly to the stung conscience and bitterness of the chief priests, who saw these words were aimed at them. If we look closely at that second parable, the difference is not weighty: it is close in spirit, purpose, and even form to the first, and could well have sharpened the rage the first had stirred. I need not dwell on the question about the tribute-money. Every reader sees that it touches Christ's claim to be a king, and was meant to ruin His reputation with the people or to prepare a charge before the Roman Governor. Nor has anyone, so far as I know, suggested that Christ in His answer gave up or softened in the least the dignity He had claimed when He entered the city. My real complaint against the interpreters is this: they have taught readers to admire a kind of cleverness in Christ's answer, as though it were an evasion — as though it did not carry the whole meaning of His earlier teaching into a fresh and striking application, and were not full of solemn rebuke to the Pharisees and Herodians and a deep lesson to the whole nation about the secret of its slavery. What deliverance did the Pharisees dream of? Freedom from paying tribute to Cæsar. And why did they mourn that so much? Because their hearts were enslaved to covetousness; they knew no more shameful bondage than what the publican's presence signified, and no greater freedom than his being sent away. But whose image and inscription is on the coin? Cæsar's — so why should it not go to him? And whose image are you made in? What inscription is written on your hearts? Give those to the invisible God, claim Him as your King, and your chains fall off; you are slaves no longer. So Christ did not merely say, "I am not come to disturb the rule of the Cæsars." He showed why He was not come to disturb it, and why He could not truly free the nation by exempting them from tribute. He came to reveal the Kingdom of God to them, to tell them they were God's servants, not Cæsar's. In that way He came to accomplish everything the people expected of Him, had they only known what they expected; He came to undermine the tyranny of the Cæsars, the tyranny of the Pharisees, and every other tyranny in the world. Pharisees, Herodians, and Romans had reason to fear Him. He was far more truly dangerous to them, and they were beginning to see it, than all the agitators who had ever urged Israel to throw off its yoke.

#### THE SADDUCEES AND THE RESURRECTION.

*The passage:* That same day the Sadducees, who deny that there is a resurrection, came to Jesus with a case: Moses ruled that a man's brother must marry his childless widow to raise offspring for him; seven brothers in turn married one woman and each died childless, and then she died too — so whose wife will she be at the resurrection? Jesus answered that they were wrong because they knew neither the Scriptures nor God's power. In the resurrection people do not marry but are like the angels. And as for the resurrection, had they not read God's own words, "I am the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob"? He is not the God of the dead but of the living. The crowd who heard this were astonished at his teaching.

When our Lord spoke about the leaven, He plainly hinted that the opposing Jewish sects shared one habit of mind. It is not enough to say their common dislike of Him proved this deep likeness; it was certain that as their opposition unfolded, its inner nature would show itself. Here the Sadducees took up an argument that would normally have set them directly against the Pharisees — a case that must have furnished many a debate and many a jest when they refuted the other school. Yet our Lord found in it the very temper He had just exposed in the men who asked whether tribute should be paid to Cæsar. The idea of God as Lord of the heart, a spiritual Being related to His spiritual creature, was missing in the Pharisee and missing in the Sadducee alike. So neither could enter into the history of their forefathers. To the Pharisee, the old commonwealth of Israel, David's kingdom, and the city rebuilt after the captivity were not really governed by an invisible Lord reigning over it age after age; they were only a nation set apart by certain religious privileges, entitled to look down on others. The Roman yoke galled them bitterly, but it never made them cry out for a real, divine government — only for one in which they would be supreme. The Sadducees had no more sense of a living God than this. They were not called atheists — even the fiercest Pharisee would probably not have named them so — but their belief was in a dead god, one who had laid down a law or moral maxims that men were now to keep by themselves, to regulate their own conduct. That this Being still stood, or ever had stood, in any actual relation to His creatures, that He could speak to them or they truly call on Him, they did not and could not believe. So they logically, honestly rejected the resurrection. It was a loss, no doubt, to be without even the shell of such a conviction — to have told themselves plainly, "Our life is bounded by this world; when appearances vanish, we vanish with them; the laws that govern us are only our circumstances; man has nothing in him but what time and space measure." But the Pharisee really believed the same thing. His doctrine of a resurrection was merely the extension beyond the grave of all the low, selfish, worldly maxims that had ruled him this side of it. To this we may perhaps trace the mildness of the rebuke here — "You are wrong, not knowing the Scriptures nor the power of God" — compared with the sharper "Why tempt ye me, ye hypocrites?" of the earlier case. Yet the practical correction went deeper, and was simpler, than the one that distinguished what is owed to Cæsar from what is owed to God. "Have you not read that God says, I am the God of Abraham, and of Isaac, and of Jacob? He is not the God of the dead, but of the living." Whatever you think of the prophets, you grant some worth to the plain records of Genesis, and the principle of the resurrection is there. Once believe in God as the same yesterday and today, the God of men from generation to generation, truly connected with them, ever alive — and you believe in their life. Then you are freed from the notions that misled you when you asked whose wife the woman would be, for you begin to see men in their higher relation to God, and so in higher relations to one another. Here is the ground of all the later teaching about the resurrection: laid in the deepest theology, yet in the earliest revelation of God. So both Sadducees and Pharisees were taught that to understand and believe in a resurrection they must find Someone in whom they could realize an actual relation to an actual, living Being. And so the question was set before them: whether their dislike of the idea of a Son of God was not the real inward ground of their dislike of the resurrection — and whether, without belief in a Son of God, a resurrection is anything but a dark and fearful vision of the world's misery spread and prolonged.

#### WHAT THINK YE OF CHRIST?

*The passage:* While the Pharisees were together, Jesus asked them what they thought of the Christ — whose son is he? They said, the Son of David. He asked how David, speaking in the Spirit, could then call him Lord, saying, "The Lord said unto my Lord, Sit thou on my right hand, till I make thine enemies thy footstool." If David calls him Lord, how is he his son? No one could answer him, and from that day no one dared ask him any more questions.

This question is given, with slight differences, in Mark 12 and in Luke 20. It is one of the few questions our Lord addressed directly to the people or the scribes without their having provoked it. It stands close to the passages we have lately considered, and all three Gospels place it shortly before the last Passover. Clearly the Evangelists thought that although it led to no obvious result, was short, went unanswered, and raised a difficulty our Lord did not himself solve, it still deserved a lasting and emphatic record. Did not every event they were recording contain this very question? Did not those events, seen alongside the whole past history of the nation and the whole future history of the human race, supply the answer? How can Christ be both the Son of David and the Lord of David — yet how can he fail to be either, if the Scriptures are true? This was the terrible paradox that tormented the Pharisee, a paradox in his own being as well as in the sacred Books, and he could find only one way to escape it. Eighteen centuries later it is still the question set before every Jew and every Christian, and Christians as well as Jews are inclined to be rid of it much as the Pharisee was. If there is to be a King, a Son of Man over men, must he not be the Son of God? If there is a Son of God, must he not be King over men? Could the Christ fulfil one character without fulfilling the other? The Crucifixion was man's attempt to remove the difficulty; the Resurrection was God's.

#### THE PROPHECY OF THE LAST DAYS.

*The passage:* When the disciples pointed out the magnificent stones of the temple, Jesus foretold its total destruction and spoke of wars, false Christs, tribulation, the desolation of the holy place, the coming of the Son of Man, and the end of the age — urging His followers to watch, since no one knows the day or hour.

We now come to those famous chapters reporting our Lord's conversation with His disciples when they showed Him the goodly stones of the temple. I will follow St. Matthew, as before, noting any important differences in the other Evangelists as I go.

I. There is no dispute about the occasion. The disciples were struck by the temple's fine stones, and whatever else His words meant, they plainly declared that not one stone of that building would be left standing. This remark is obvious, but its very obviousness may have kept it from getting the attention it deserves. Think what that building meant to a Jew. The tabernacle was the sign of God's presence in the nation; its loss at Shiloh was seen as the nation's temporary downfall; its move to the hill above Jerusalem was bound up with the covenant with David, with the nation's whole history, and with the deepest and most rapturous Psalms. Think what Solomon's building and dedication of the temple and his bringing in of the Ark implied; how much of Isaiah turns on his vision, in the year King Uzziah died, of the glory of the Lord filling the temple; how the whole of Jeremiah and his Lamentations concern that temple's desecration; how Ezekiel's visions begin, turn, and end with its desertion and restoration; how its downfall and a future abomination of desolation connect with Daniel and the captivity; how the temple is plainly the subject of Haggai and Zechariah; and how the expectation of One who would come to purify it and its priests is central to Malachi. Consider all this, and we see how this subject relates to every book of the Old Testament, and so to God's whole training of mankind. Here was the place that told the Jew, and declared to the universe, that a God whom no eye can see and who can be pictured in no likeness nevertheless truly exists, is really connected with His creatures, dwells near them, and may be approached by them. Of this building our Lord said not one stone would be left on another. What could the disciples make of it? St. Matthew states, and the others imply, their feeling: privately, on the Mount of Olives, they asked, "Tell us when these things will be, and what will be the sign of Your coming and of the end of the age." Either the temple's destruction meant God had left the world — the start of outright atheism — or it meant the start of something higher: the kingdom of One greater than the temple, who would draw God and man nearer and prove the God of Abraham to be Lord of the whole earth. The Apostles, I think, must have taken one of these. Amid utter confusion they had the faith and courage to believe their Master spoke truly when He said the Kingdom of God was at hand. We seem to have decided they were mistaken. Our Lord did not tell them so.

II. I will not inquire into the exact force of the words "Your coming, and the end of the age," nor even into what the Apostles meant by them then. Their real meaning is precisely what we must learn from the later history and revelation — this very discourse being one of our great guides. The Apostles were of course confused here, as about everything: about the Christ, His kingdom, His relation to themselves, their nation, and the world. The Evangelists assume this confusion throughout, so those who trust their record must admit it more readily than any modern philosopher. But it is quite another thing to say the words they used were wrong, or that they expected too complete a fulfilment, or were wrong to place it in their own time. Religious people today may attach very vague and false notions — borrowed from heathenism and self-contradictory — to the words Heaven, Hell, and Judgment; yet those who try to correct these errors may feel that the words themselves carry a tremendous reality, weakened rather than exaggerated by common misunderstanding. So too the Apostles may not have used words too large when they questioned our Lord — words we must dilute before we can adopt them; they may simply not yet have learned to give them their full, literal strictness.

III. "Take heed that no man deceive you: for many shall come in my Name, saying, I am Christ, and shall deceive many." Few doubt that this was a warning to the very men then looking at the temple. It fits all His earlier teaching exactly. The great danger to the Apostles, as to all men, was being led astray by a false Christ — being led to look for a King coming with the signs the Pharisees or the people demanded, signs wholly unlike those with which Jesus came. Many such would arise, He warns them — men claiming David's throne and the marks of a divine mission. How, they might ask, could they ever, after believing Jesus was the Christ, think another was? But the warning was given, and in time they would learn why they needed it.

IV. "You shall hear of wars and rumors of wars: see that you are not troubled; these things must be, but the end is not yet. Nation shall rise against nation, and there shall be famines, pestilences, and earthquakes in various places; all these are the beginning of sorrows." Every word here is addressed directly to the Apostles: "*you* shall hear; see that *you* are not troubled." To suppose this does not refer to actual reports reaching their ears — of insurrections in Judæa, quarrels among nearby rulers, threats from the imperial power — shows strange carelessness about language. If anyone objects that "nation against nation, kingdom against kingdom" is too grand for a city in one province, read the history of the Roman Empire and see how local events stirred the legions there into rivalries and military revolts that spread like magnetism through the empire, until every part joined the strife. Or read Tacitus, not Josephus, on the years from Nero's end to Jerusalem's fall, and see how the greatest convulsions were tied to Syria, of which every Jew had bitter experience. So whether or not we admit a divine purpose behind Jerusalem's calamities, we must admit as fact that the whole world must have seemed to a Jew to share the convulsions shaking his own land. I do not use "convulsions" to explain away "earthquakes, pestilences, and famines" as metaphor; I take those words to mean exactly what they say, and no reader of Tacitus can doubt they happened just so, *in various places*. There was to be no single, general earthquake matching the great political one, but enough to serve as signs that the physical world sympathizes with the moral and obeys the same Ruler. Our Lord's aim seems to have been to warn His disciples that, while they noticed these signs, they must not be distracted by them or mistake *them* for the great tokens that God had come to judge the earth. "All these are the *beginning* of sorrows."

V. "Then they will deliver you up to be afflicted and killed, and you will be hated of all nations for my Name's sake. Many will be offended and betray and hate one another; many false prophets will arise and deceive many; and because iniquity abounds, the love of many will grow cold. But he who endures to the end shall be saved. And this Gospel of the Kingdom shall be preached in all the world for a witness unto all nations; and then shall the end come." I take the whole paragraph because its closing words stand in direct contrast to the last one's. There: "the *beginning* of sorrows." Here: "then shall the *end* come." Wars, earthquakes, and famines are not signs of the end but preludes to it; the preaching of the Gospel is the sign the end is near. I also join these verses because the difficulty supposed to lie in verse 14 is removed by verse 9: "you shall be hated of all nations," and "the Gospel shall be preached... unto all nations." The phrase "the whole world" would hinder no one, since anyone recalls that Augustus taxed "all the world" — the same expression. The stumbling-block is "all nations." But those words were just used in a sentence that *must* apply to the Apostles. Preaching the Gospel as a witness to all the Gentiles is exactly what a Jew would have understood: if all the Gentile races the Jews knew, in whose lands they lived, were told that Jesus was their King and that they too belonged to God's Kingdom, then the witness spoken of would have been borne. If it came to special pleading, I could plead too — since the great European and American peoples were then still in Asia, it does not follow that all the earth's tribes were not reached, even without adopting vague traditions of apostolic missions. But I need no such arguments; I simply keep to the text. The Apostles were told they would be hated by all nations; in whatever sense they took that, they must have understood they were to be the instruments of preaching to all nations, and so, after something they had done, within their age, the end would come. Many shrink from the idea that iniquity abounded and Christian love grew cold in the apostolic age; whether the facts justify that doubt we shall judge better when we come to the apostolic writings and certain testimonies of Christian antiquity, which bear also on the rise of false prophets then.

VI. "When you see the abomination of desolation, spoken of by Daniel, standing in the holy place (let the reader understand), then let those in Judæa flee to the mountains; let no one on the housetop come down for his goods, nor the man in the field turn back for his clothes. Woe to those with child and nursing in those days! Pray that your flight be not in winter nor on the sabbath, for then shall be great tribulation, such as was not from the beginning of the world, nor ever shall be; and except those days were shortened, no flesh would be saved, but for the elect's sake they shall be shortened." Without settling exactly what desecration is meant, notice that our Lord keeps fixing His disciples' thoughts on the very object their eyes were on — the temple — which is truly the subject of His whole discourse, as its opening led us to expect; and all these calamities, greater than any the world has seen or will see, and the flight He commands, are bound to it. If the family of Mattathias had no trouble knowing when the abomination of desolation was set up in their day, the disciples might hope, even in their half-belief, to be shown wherein this fuller desertion of the Divine presence would consist.

VII. "Then if any say, 'Here is Christ,' or 'there,' do not believe it; for false Christs and false prophets will arise and show great signs and wonders, so as to deceive, if possible, even the elect. I have told you beforehand. So if they say, 'He is in the desert,' do not go out; 'He is in the secret chambers,' do not believe it. For as the lightning comes out of the east and shines to the west, so shall the coming of the Son of Man be. Wherever the carcass is, there the eagles gather. Immediately after the tribulation of those days the sun shall be darkened, the moon shall not give her light, the stars shall fall, and the powers of the heavens shall be shaken." We would naturally take "then" to refer to the event just spoken of, and "you" to mean some of the questioners, unless we had strong evidence otherwise; and until such evidence is proved, we must not read these words less strictly merely because they concern events of great solemnity. Taken in their obvious sense, would they have led the Apostles to expect the Son of Man's appearing in their own age? I think there is no doubt of it. False Christs and prophets would appear; the true Christ would appear to confound them, and confidence in *His* appearing would be their only escape from *their* deceptions. Second, would this appearing be visible? Most visible in its effects and in its accompanying signs, but not visible as the false Christs would be visible. For their falsehood would consist precisely in this: they came to reform the world on the opposite principle to God's Son — to change the surface of things — while He came to lay the axe to the root. In whatever judgment He appeared, He came for that. So if the disciples truly took these words in, not twisting them to their old notions but using them to correct those notions, would they not have understood Him to say: "All these visible appearances of men leading you into the desert or the chamber will tempt you sorely, because the greatest temptation is to seek Me in the outward world, to turn Me into a Cæsar rather than a real divine King, to picture Me on a lofty throne like earth's lords rather than as the Judge who searches every motive and lays every heart bare, before whom all falsehood flies." As the lightning fills the whole sky, so shall the coming of the Son of Man be — mighty words to shatter every sensual dream that puts an apparent Christ for the real one. "Do not think of the desert or the chamber, but of a light felt at once through all heaven and falling as a terrific, illuminating power on earth. And do not suppose this judgment will be felt only where the temple stands. You Jews are scattered over all the earth, everywhere bearing the sign of a divine covenant, meant to be life-givers to the world. But the life has left the body; it has become a carcass, and wherever any part of it lies, the birds of prey — God's commissioned destroyers — will gather. In all these places there will be a manifestation of the Son of Man, clear tokens that He has come to judge the chosen people, and through them the whole earth."

VIII. "Immediately after that tribulation... then shall appear the sign of the Son of Man in heaven, and all the tribes of the earth shall mourn, and they shall see the Son of Man coming in the clouds with power and great glory. And He shall send His angels with a great trumpet, and they shall gather His elect from the four winds. Now learn a parable from the fig-tree: when its branch is tender and puts forth leaves, you know summer is near; so, when you see all these things, know that it is near, at the doors. Truly I say to you, this generation shall not pass till all these things are fulfilled. Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away." The Evangelists agree here, except that St. Luke is more vivid: "signs in the sun, moon, and stars; distress of nations in perplexity; the sea and waves roaring; men's hearts failing them for fear of what is coming; for the powers of heaven shall be shaken." Comparing the two shows that actual signs in the visible heavens must be meant, as before. Whoever reads these as mere political changes, to escape any physical reference, does violence to the text. Luke especially shows that physical convulsions would startle men most, as such things always do, and be felt as most ominous. But ominous of something — they are not themselves what they foretell. So there is warrant for those who make "the powers of heaven" mean more, not less, than signs in moon and stars. When they say it means *dynasties*, I cannot condemn them as weakening the words, for the fall of a dynasty is far greater than a volcano's eruption. But I do not think they have reached the words' full force, or read them literally enough.

All the old world's dynasties confessed something higher than themselves — kings who bowed to gods above and powers beneath. Astrology was bound up with worship, and demons ruling in sun and stars were thought to uphold earthly thrones. So the simplest reading of our Lord's words is that these powers of heaven were to be shaken, the thrones of these demons and demi-gods to totter. This interpretation demands no strain, agrees with the ordinary language of His hearers and with the facts of heathen life, and involves only the very confusion between the visible and invisible that really existed in false worshippers' minds — a confusion the words, if true, ought to express. Whether they were fulfilled in this sense we must ask later; I deal now only with the prophecy. The same applies to "then shall appear the sign of the Son of Man in heaven, and all the tribes of the earth shall mourn, and see Him coming in the clouds with power and great glory." I will not now say what the sign was, or how the tribes mourned, or how they saw Him; I only say that, taken with what precedes, the words must mean a victory of the Son of Man over those powers men had worshipped — a victory felt by the tribes of the earth in some striking, terrible way, with visible accompaniments. It must be the victory of Christ as a living Person, not of a religion or doctrine — a victory understood at once by those who saw in God's light, understood in part by some on earth, and leaving the deepest marks in the world's history, marks that would at last make plain that the greatest of all crises had occurred. Whatever the sign and the appearing were, they must answer these conditions to fit the words, and cannot contradict the words just before about the lightning filling the whole sky.

The next words — "He shall send His angels with a great trumpet, and they shall gather His elect from the four winds, from one end of heaven to the other" — exactly match the description in the seventh chapter of the Apocalypse. I will consider their meaning carefully when I treat that book. For now I only note that, so far as the context shows, they seem closely linked to the temple's desecration and overthrow. No language could be more exact than "immediately after the tribulation of those days," which governs the whole passage. Judged by this Gospel and the others, these words suit exactly the tenor of John the Baptist's first prophecy and all our Lord's earlier prophecies. The people who boasted "We have Abraham as our father" would be left without capital or temple, the great witnesses of their election's meaning. But the purpose of the election would stand; God would raise up children to Abraham, show why He had called them, and reveal a deeper society and kingdom hidden beneath theirs.

For how does He continue? "Learn a parable from the fig-tree: when its branch is tender and puts forth leaves, you know summer is near; so, when you see all these things, know it is near, at the doors." All these events, in which at first one sees only winter's icy hand withering and destroying, are really foretastes of a coming summer; death prepares for life. Instead of being cast down, you are to lift up your heads when they appear, for they speak of redemption, not ruin. The world's regeneration — the manifestation of the kingdom your fathers died expecting, which I said is at hand and have come to reveal — will be the fruit of that judgment which leaves not one stone of this building on another.

He says this, then adds: "Truly I say to you, this generation shall not pass till all these things are fulfilled. Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away." He had said "*immediately* after the tribulation," and "when *you* see all these things," and now "this generation shall not pass." No language could be more awful. I do not ask those who think the speaker was only Matthew the publican, or the fallible report of a good man, to weigh it heavily. But I do ask those who believe these are the words of the Son of God, who spoke as never man spoke, and who insist on the record's inspiration and authority, to take care how they trifle with "Truly I say to you" and "Heaven and earth shall pass away." Let them notice that the language grows more exact and emphatic in the parts they suppose *cannot* have been fulfilled at the temple's fall than in the parts they eagerly claim, with quotations from Josephus, were fulfilled then. Let them weigh the whole sequence, and then ask whether they can be satisfied with the explanation of "generation" that Mede made popular and that others have adopted in slavish deference, or in eager relief at any escape from such severe language. Did our Lord, saying the temple should be destroyed, mean only that it would fall before the Jewish race died out? Did He not mean γενεά (generation) in its ordinary sense at all? Were they not to expect the city besieged by Roman armies within that age? Consider that it is one thing to say a prophecy has a double or treble sense — meaning that what is true of one time is even more clearly true of another, as historians note that the same principles ruled Charlemagne and Napoleon, that facts repeat, and that their governing laws come out more fully in later events — and quite another thing to use words in a double sense, meaning that within the very same discourse their meaning changes, so the interpreter may make them mean one thing here and another there. To say a prophet must go beyond a single event when he lays down a great law of the Divine Mind is to grant him the very insight his name implies; but to say he juggles with words in a double sense is to call him a false prophet, a heathen oracle. Had Mede's followers only said, "Christ's words cannot pass away; they were not exhausted at the temple's fall; the law of the Divine Mind fulfilled then must be eternal, for He is eternal; we live under His government as they did, so what came to pass in that generation is a guide for later ones" — how one would have honored and thanked them for helping us read the future by the past! But when they say, "Our Lord did not mean that *all* these things would come in that generation, though He used these very words, but only *some*, leaving us to decide which belong to that time and which to ages hence," then all security in reading the Divine Word is lost; no neologian undermines the authority of the Evangelists, or of our Lord Himself, more effectively than these honest, earnest believers.

IX. "But of that day and hour no one knows, not the angels of heaven, but my Father only. As the days of Noah were, so shall the coming of the Son of Man be. For as before the flood they ate, drank, married, and gave in marriage until Noah entered the ark, and knew nothing until the flood came and swept them all away, so shall it be. Then two shall be in the field; one taken, one left. Two women grinding at the mill; one taken, one left." Right after His emphatic "all these things shall come in this generation," He adds, "of that day and hour no one knows... but the Father only." The Apostles had asked *when*; He answers plainly, in varied yet harmonious ways: "It will be within this age, within a human lifetime, within the years some of you will live to see. Some of you may be brought before rulers and killed before the moment comes, but all of you will witness the events I have described as its forerunners. More I cannot tell you. The day and hour are not revealed — men know them not, angels know them not, only the Father. Men so deeply concerned will have all the preparation they need; the inhabitants of the invisible world, no less concerned, will understand more clearly when it has happened and what it means. But the first must understand by watching and waiting, keeping an attitude of spiritual expectation, not by guessing at times and seasons; the others will not need to guess, resting in the assurance that past, present, and future are in the hands of Him who is perfect goodness and truth and will establish His Son's Kingdom on earth." How deeply this went into the disciples' hearts — how sure they were the coming was in their generation, yet how careful to warn their followers against asking the day or hour — we shall consider later. Here I only ask you to see how our Lord strengthens their conviction that a great era was ending by having them compare their generation with the one that ended an earlier era: "As the days of Noah were, so shall the coming of the Son of Man be." The words that follow surely mean there would be the same security and indifference about the coming crisis as about that ancient one. Yet it too would be a crisis of destruction and of restoration, showing what the earth and men are without God and showing Him as its Ruler and Renewer. All that follows accurately describes a great judgment, where one is taken and another left, the onlooker knowing not why, only feeling an invisible power at work — which he either curses as arbitrary and hostile or blesses as righteous, believing its purpose is to set righteousness in the earth. It is the description of a judgment — not a sudden sweeping destruction but a searching, penetrating fire sent to try men's hearts. All this fits the belief in an unseen Lord of the earth making His government felt in men's hearts and through the visible world, fits the appearing of the Son of Man in His Father's glory with the holy angels — but it does not in the least fit the advent of a visible prince in the tinsel of ordinary royalty. Such an advent would be announced precisely as to day and hour; its visible tokens would be the decisive ones; the secret witnesses would matter little; the command would be, "expect me, prepare in this place, for there I will appear." But not, "Watch, for you know not, any more than those around you, at what hour your Lord comes."

"Watch, therefore, for you know not what hour your Lord comes. Know this: if the householder had known when the thief would come, he would have watched and not let his house be broken into. So be ready, for the Son of Man comes at an hour you think not. Who then is the faithful and wise servant, made ruler over the household to feed them in due season? Blessed is that servant whom his lord finds so doing; truly, he shall make him ruler over all his goods. But if that evil servant says in his heart, 'My lord delays,' and begins to beat his fellow servants and eat and drink with the drunken, his lord will come in a day and hour he does not expect, and will cut him in pieces and give him his portion with the hypocrites, where there is weeping and gnashing of teeth." I want to press the point I made in the last section before I finish this chapter, because I know the moral obstacle to the literal view I have taken is far stronger than any other. If our Lord really meant all these things would come in that generation, what has the chapter to do with us? What promises or warnings does it hold that we may not set aside as mere adaptations, not spoken to us by His own lips? My answer: its promises, warnings, and commands will be far weightier and more awful for us if we believe every one of them was strictly fulfilled in that day. For then we believe the Kingdom of God has actually come with power, has come to us, and that we live under its shadow. Then the question, "how have we used this gift, how have we behaved as subjects of this kingdom?" brings before us not a vague fancy of some future judgment but the clear knowledge that we must be judged — a steady watching for signs that the Judge is at the door, and a direct use of our Lord's comparison of the world's first period with its second, as a ground for judging the third by the second.
