If what follows were not so clearly part of this design, I would be tempted to linger on it for another reason — it strongly confirms the view I drew, from the other three Evangelists' accounts of the last days, of the coming of the Son of Man and his kingdom. Luke places here several sayings the others attach to the discourse about the temple. He gives them in answer to the Pharisees' question, "When will the kingdom of God come?" The answer: "The kingdom of God cometh not with observation; for, behold, the kingdom of God is within you." This saying is often quoted, but always with the qualifier that there is also an outward kingdom, and that all Gospel talk of the Son of Man's coming refers to that outward kingdom, not this inward one. But how does that fit the context? Luke immediately moves to the very language used elsewhere to describe the Son of Man's coming, as part of the same answer to the Pharisees, expanding his rebuke of their materialism. What else can this mean but that the great outward events would reveal the very kingdom that does not come with observation — bringing to light the kingdom within, at the root of each man's being and of society itself, the order God established from the beginning, which would emerge in new power once the outer fabric of Jewish polity broke apart? That the gospel of the Spirit should declare this, that the Evangelist of the new, human dispensation should record its permanent, eternal foundation, and that this announcement of the true and new Jerusalem should come amid warnings preparing the disciples for Christ's rejection by the old — all this gives the words a fitting consistency and solemnity for him who is said to have spoken them.

##### CHAPTER XVIII.

It is pointless to ask on what principle the chapter divisions were made; presumably parables were meant to be grouped and separated from other discourses where possible — otherwise why detach the parable urging constant prayer without fainting from the preceding warnings about the Son of Man's coming, though it ends with "when the Son of Man cometh, shall he find faith on the earth?" The dividers had reason to think this parable has worth of its own apart from its occasion: it captures the very impulse to pray, the sense of being tormented by an adversary, showing that men will pray so long as they believe in any Judge of the earth at all, even an unfeeling one — and that the deepest encouragement to prayer lies in believing that Judge actually cares to free them from their oppressor. But this timeless meaning is realized far more powerfully if we picture a moment when everything looked dark and terrible, when the Son of Man seemed to have abandoned the earth he had visited, when the disciples searched in vain for any sign of his day, when the adversary seemed unbeatable and judges cared for neither God nor man — and then, exactly then, amid thunder and lightning, came an actual revelation of Christ's power, presence, and permanent kingship. That would be a lasting consolation, an encouragement to God's elect to cry day and night in their worst circumstances, certain he was hearing them and about to answer just when he seemed to delay longest.

Luke uses the words "his own elect." Could words be more genial or encouraging? Without believing himself called by God, what strength has a man to pray? How can he ask deliverance without believing God wills to deliver him, and that his adversary is God's adversary too? But did this confidence carry no danger — might it not breed contempt for others, self-glorification? And what would that do to his prayer? Our Lord answers in a parable that must be read as the necessary companion to the first. A man who thinks himself unlike other men cannot really pray — he feels no adversary, flies to no judge. Unless he says, "God be merciful to me a sinner," taking the lowest place among men and seeking deliverance simply as a man, not as better than others, he doesn't know what he needs deliverance from, or who could give it; he has no faith, no ground for his conscience to stand on. Here is a paradox: the elect man must see himself as one with all other men, or he cannot feel that God hears or justifies him. A man who stands on his own separateness cannot feel God has called him, for his very effort to separate himself from the world proves he belongs to it — for what is "the world," in its bad sense, but individuals trying to rank themselves above each other, to sever themselves from one another? This is the paradox of Christianity itself, one we will find Paul continually pressing, and one that has confused readers of his epistles who try to resolve it by discarding one or the other of the two facts he actually held together — which I think we will find he reconciled.

I have given full weight to the wish that this parable be read universally, without even mentioning — though almost every reader notices it — that its two figures are a Pharisee and a Publican. This ties it directly to the larger theme of these discourses: the exposure of the falsehood by which the Jew inverted God's purpose in calling him, bringing on himself the very exclusion he supposed God intended for others. The verses to the 30th have already been treated at length; what I said there applies especially here.

I have also already discussed the passage from verse 31 onward, but note it here as one of several allusions to the journey up to Jerusalem that give these chapters their pathos, and that keep before us the goal to which every warning, encouragement, and event is subordinate. This last incident connects movingly with the disciples' failure to understand, noted in verse 34. How their Master, the great King, could become the great sufferer — scourged, spat upon, killed — no words, not even Christ's own, could teach them; only suffering could teach that. The blind man who cried, "Thou Son of David, have mercy upon me," whom the disciples tried to keep away, discovered for himself, and teaches us, that only one who shares man's sorrows as a brother can be his Lord and deliverer.

##### CHAPTER XIX.

I connect the opening story of this chapter with the closing one of the last. The old view of Zacchæus as an extortioner reformed by Christ's Spirit, though set aside by some modern interpreters, still seems to me most consistent with the facts. The Pharisees were too used to our Lord's dealings with publicans to be struck by an ordinary case; their remark, "he is gone to be guest with a man that is a sinner," suggests, absent other evidence, that Zacchæus was worse than his class — that his wealth came by means common in that trade but not universal. His own words support this probability; they sound like a new resolution, not a denial of ever having wronged anyone. So our Lord's words, "This day has salvation come to this house, forasmuch as he also is a son of Abraham," both recognize an outcast as included in the covenant and affirm that its great blessing is deliverance from the very sin that kept Abraham's children from God. That this particular sin was the one our Lord had been exposing throughout, and had foretold as the cause of the nation's coming ruin, gives this moral miracle special weight, explaining why it should be paired with the physical healing of the blind man as testimony to the kingship Jesus was about to claim on entering the city. Without this purpose, the story would seem too minor to record at such a crucial point in the history.

Again, we are told he spoke a parable because he was near Jerusalem and they supposed the kingdom of God would appear at once. This parable of the pounds is often compared to Matthew's parable of the talents. Their obvious differences: there, varying sums are given; here, one pound each. There, the faithful servants' reward is entering the Lord's joy; here, it is ruling over ten or five cities. There, no citizens revolt or are punished; here, as with Matthew's marriage-feast parable, that is a central, deliberate feature. In fact I think the rest of the parable must be read in light of it. The journey to Jerusalem had led the disciples to expect some grand display, even the actual establishment of Christ's kingdom. The answer: No. First, a message from the citizens: "we will not have this man to reign over us." Then an interval in which the king's servants are given power to use or neglect. Then a reckoning of what each has done. Then judgment on the citizens. All this before the kingdom is established. I believe this maps precisely onto the period up to the Ascension, the apostolic age after the Spirit was given, and the events leading to and culminating in the destruction of Jerusalem. Whether the parable loses force for the later Church depends on our estimate of that Church's endowments. If every believer, beyond whatever special talent suits his own calling, also receives the higher gift of an indwelling Spirit, his responsibility for that gift is no less than that of Christians in the intermediate age between the passing of the Old Dispensation and the full arrival of the New, who were called to account for their stewardship — rewarded with greater usefulness and authority in proportion to their gain, or stripped of what they had treated as unreal. Only if the Church has for eighteen centuries been barren of what Scripture says is its very essence does this parable, however perfectly suited to apostolic times, stop being a guide for ours.

Here we reach the meeting point of the Evangelists: the descent from the Mount of Olives. Luke gives a more direct and obvious preparation for it than the others, and enlarges it in one important way: "I tell you," Christ says to the Pharisees complaining of the disciples' hosannas, "if these should hold their peace, the stones would immediately cry out." Those words are explained by the tears he sheds moments later, approaching the city: within a few years, the very stones held together by children's praise would indeed cry out — "For the days shall come upon thee, that thine enemies shall cast a trench about thee... and lay thee even with the ground... because thou knewest not the time of thy visitation."

##### CHAPTERS XX. XXI. XXII. XXIII.

I have already spoken at length on the first two of these chapters. The account of the Last Supper in chapter 22 has three notable peculiarities. First, the new feast is set apart from the old far more sharply than in the other Gospels: the words "I will not eat any more of this passover, until it be fulfilled in the kingdom of God" and "I will not drink of the fruit of the vine, until the kingdom of God shall come" are spoken with clear distinctness after he had eaten the unleavened bread and passed the paschal lamb, and before he takes bread and says, "This is my body," and the cup, "This is the new testament in my blood." I know no clearer proof that Luke was, and knew himself to be, the Evangelist of the New Dispensation in a special sense. Second, and nearly as significant: Luke records a dispute at this very feast over who among them should be counted greatest, and Christ's answer — that the Gentiles' kings lord it over their subjects and call themselves benefactors, but "ye shall not be so"; the greatest must be as the youngest, the chief as the servant; for is not the one who serves greater than the one who sits at meat, though Christ himself serves them? Yet he appoints them a kingdom as his Father appointed one to him, that they may eat and drink at his table and sit on thrones judging Israel. That such a dispute should arise at such a moment is among the most humbling, and therefore most instructive, facts in the New Testament: first they ask who will betray him, then who among them ranks highest. That this feast — the institution of a sacrificial meal by one who was himself to be the sacrifice — should be the very occasion for breaking down notions of rivalry and precedence, of "benefactor" meaning only "greater servant," fits its purpose exactly. And even as their ideas of worldly power are crushed by his words and acts, how confidently he promises them real power, repeating all he had told them of a kingdom and their place in it — a kingdom founded on sacrifice, actually to be proclaimed in the world, in which, if they could give up themselves, they would be kings and judges. Third is the passage about the purse, the bag, and the sword — much debated by interpreters. I am not sure I fully understand it, but it looks to me like a re-consecration of ordinary things, which the disciples had proven they did not strictly need but which Christ would now hallow into his service. They had learned their Master could sustain them without these things; now they must depend on him even while possessing them — for the earth itself was to be redeemed, and the ploughshare, the sword, and the money-bag could all serve God in the new order as in the old. If this reading is right — and it is close to what many have held, and perhaps underlies the medieval fancies about the "two swords" — it fits Luke's larger design that he should record this conversation, just as he shortly after records the disciples' mistaken use of the sword and Christ's correction of it.

In the account of the Arraignment and Passion in chapter 23, Luke has several notable variations: he alone records Christ being sent to Herod; he alone gives the words to the daughters of Jerusalem on the way to Calvary; he alone gives the words from the cross, "Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do"; he alone tells the story of the penitent thief; and he alone gives the last words, "Father, into thy hands I commend my spirit." I merely note these differences for your own reflection. A thoughtful, devout reader needs few words from me on such a subject; he will already have learned, better than I could teach, the power of these passages. I only suggest that far from being breaks or accidental additions in Luke's narrative, they fit its whole structure and purpose so closely that substituting either other Evangelist's account here would jar, though we might not say why. I cannot easily prove this without going into refinements I would rather avoid, which might do the reader's inward perception more harm than his understanding good. But it is worth noting, as most readers probably have noted for themselves, that the Evangelist who recorded the woman weeping at Jesus' feet and the parable of the Prodigal Son is exactly the one from whom we would expect to hear, "To-day shalt thou be with me in Paradise" — words which, I believe, Matthew was, for good reason, not given to know or record.

##### CHAPTER XXIV.

The whole story of the journey to Emmaus is unique to Luke. Many have found that this story, more than any other, gives them a grasp of the risen Lord and the kind of fellowship that might exist between him and his disciples — different from before the Passion, yet more real, more intimate, mixed with greater awe yet producing greater warmth of heart, sparer in words yet deeper in instruction, making clear what had before been only dimly heard, spreading peace, opening the heart to mysteries it had only vaguely sensed. The sense of a body freed from death's chains, essentially the same as before, now using naturally powers once hidden or only occasional, is one part — though perhaps not the chief part — of the revelation. Its power to vanish and reappear suggests the possibility of a spiritual presence that might remain continually near, in which men are meant always to dwell.

If this is the impression Luke's words leave on us, they carry us beyond the forty days after the Resurrection, leaving us certain we stand at the beginning, not the end, of a history. If there was a Resurrection, there must be an Ascension. Men have believed it — why? Because of a great mass of external proof? Where is it? Who has ever found it, or believed such a stupendous fact on its strength? For eighteen hundred years men have accepted the Ascension on the testimony of this one Evangelist, confirmed by a few words in Mark, not because the evidence compelled them but because, believing already in a Son of God and King of men, it was not incredible that it should be so — rather incredible that it should not be so: that he should be bound by chains of space and time, not lead captivity captive, not be as actual and personal as ever, though freed from the fetters that keep us from realizing our own personality; that he should not actually sit at God's right hand, actually be the bond of union among men. The Evangelist of the New Dispensation casts this belief before the world without demanding special deference to his own authority or piling up crushing evidence, offering it simply as good news which the needs of men and the wisdom of God in ages to come would establish — even if every earthly witness to it failed, even if all the power and wisdom of the world declared that God had not set his Son on the holy hill of Zion, and that the rulers of the earth owed him no homage.

## LECTURE II. ACTS OF THE APOSTLES, EPISTLES OF ST. JAMES, ST. PETER AND ST. PAUL.

### ACTS OF THE APOSTLES

ROMANS I. 1—4.

*The passage:* The gospel of God, promised beforehand through His prophets in the holy Scriptures, is about His Son Jesus Christ our Lord — descended from David according to the flesh, and declared to be the Son of God with power, according to the Spirit of holiness, by His resurrection from the dead.

Most readers notice how large a place the life and travels of St. Paul occupy in the Acts of the Apostles. They think this natural in a writer known as Paul's companion, for whom the twelve so prominent in the Gospels might well shrink to nothing beside the teacher of the Gentiles with whom he lived and suffered. But some ask: does this weight given to a man whom our Lord did not mark out on earth as a herald of His kingdom contradict the book's title and clash with the rest of the New Testament? Does it show that there was a separate Pauline element in the Church, at odds with the authority, if not the doctrine, of the older Apostles — so that later editors, or Luke's own leaning toward compromise, brought in the earlier part of the book to soften the later? Such questions have occurred to readers in every age, and in our own day they have taken formal shape and deserve serious attention.

I think I have already partly dealt with these questions. I have plainly admitted the difference between St. Luke and St. Matthew as Evangelists and examined what it consists in, so far as comparing the two documents allows. The difference is not that Matthew affirms less clearly than Luke that Jesus is the Son of God and King of men. It is that one shows His Sonship chiefly in acknowledging a divine Father, the other in the gift of a divine Spirit to men; that one sees Christ's kingdom as fulfilling a particular dispensation, the other as the form of a universal one. In Matthew a Hebrew character appears, with a human character shining through it; in Luke the aim is to present the Gospel-kingdom as the one originally designed for mankind. Yet we have also found Luke — not once, but continuously through his whole Gospel, and especially in the long series of discourses after the Transfiguration — tying every word about the future to Jerusalem, to Jesus' claim to be King over that city, and to that claim's rejection by its rulers.

If these points hold, and this was the steady tenor of Luke's first treatise, then I find nothing out of place, no sign of patchwork, in what follows. The new treatise opens with the Ascension, which Luke seems to regard as the necessary sequel to the Resurrection and the proof of Christ's claim to universal rule; its witnesses are the Apostles He chose; they complete the number He had fixed, answering to the twelve tribes; they are met at a great Jewish festival gathering men from across the Roman and Parthian empires; the Spirit's descent is declared the witness of Jesus' Sonship and kingdom, shown by enabling Galilæans to speak of God's wonderful works so that each hearer understood in his native dialect. The society that held this early pledge of universality is yet confined for a time to the city of its birth, and proves its human character there by no one counting his goods his own; then it spreads. Circumstances apparently accidental lead to acts of discipline and fuller organization; a deacon discovers, even before an apostle, that the temple and the customs of Moses would not last forever, becomes the first martyr, and his death spreads the kingdom into Samaria and soon into Syria. A Hebrew of the Hebrews rages against the church for breaking the barrier between Jews and the outside world, then learns by revelation that he is a sinner as much as the Gentiles and that Gentiles share in Christ as he does; yet another apostle, not Luke's friend and teacher, is the first to preach to the Gentiles, taught not to call common what God had cleansed. Saul spends a long time at Antioch — among Jews sprinkled with some Greeks, where believers first came to be called Christians — learning what his work was, until he is directly appointed to it. From then on the book's main subject is how he declared a universal kingdom to Roman governors in Cyprus, to Lycaonian savages, to Asiatic Greeks, to Macedonians, to Athenian philosophers, to the worshippers of Aphrodite at Corinth and of Diana at Ephesus. It is filled out by his journey to Jerusalem to defend the Gentiles' right to the new covenant without submitting to the old form, by another journey in which he declared Christ had sent him to the Gentiles and so drew on himself the zealots' plot and the high priest's condemnation, and by his examinations before Gentile governors until he came a prisoner to the capital of the world.

I cannot imagine a more orderly and coherent history, one whose every part is explained more naturally by the governing idea we have traced in the writer's mind. I have not denied but eagerly asserted that his being Paul's companion fitted him to write it. Only I cannot find that his personal friendship drew him one step from his purpose. If he meant to describe how a human kingdom, lying hidden at the root of the Jewish kingdom, unfolded by God's will through human agents and against human opposition — how men were prepared to help establish it by divine arrangements they could not control and barely understood — this is just the narrative we should expect: one that gives no honor to individual men, that shows their errors and weaknesses, and that shows how mighty they became when they confessed a calling and were content to fulfil it.

I readily admit that the name "Acts of the Apostles" describes the book poorly. If we look for biographies of the eleven whom Luke, as much as any Evangelist, says our Lord called to sit on thrones judging the twelve tribes, we are wholly disappointed. For the countries they traveled to, how they preached, and how they died, we must rely on vague church tradition; the Scriptural historian is silent. He says little even of those our Lord most favored. Andrew appears only in the list in the first chapter. The martyrdom of James the brother of John gets a single sentence. The beloved disciple is mentioned only with Peter healing the impotent man, once before the Sanhedrim, and once in Samaria.

These omissions are, I think, the clearest possible proof of a purpose that never for a moment aims to satisfy a reader's curiosity. Whatever concerns the growth of the universal society out of the Jewish one is noted carefully and minutely. Human agents' works are mentioned only so far as they illustrate this subject. The highest honor an apostle could receive was to be shown as living and dying only for the kingdom of God, caring nothing whether his name was written in brass or wholly forgotten, provided he did his given work.

This second treatise of Luke is invaluable as an introduction to the Epistles — not because it supplies life-details we could not gather from the writers themselves (their letters are their best biographies), but because it removes an impression about them that we fall into naturally and that badly darkens their meaning. We assume they are meant to set out the Christian religion, and by that we usually mean a doctrine about the state after death and how a man may secure a blessed one for himself. Assuming this is the great subject of the Epistles, we ask earnestly why documents meant to guide our thought and conduct present views so different — how one apostle can say faith saves and another ask, "Can faith save?" These words seem to baffle us on the very point we most want clarity about. A man possessed by the one truth, feeling it the staff of his life and the Church's ground, indignantly rejects the other, and no canonical authority reconciles him to it. Milder men feel there is truth in both and cannot cast either away, though only at certain seasons do the ingenious explanations of divines really take hold of them. Bolder thinkers in a critical age readily accept the simpler solution: that men who said such different things were heads of opposing schools or factions, each holding its own theory of how the rewards of a future life were to be secured.

Now, without weighing these suggestions yet, we must say that if we take Luke's plain words for our guide — still more the whole context of his history — we shall not accept the popular account as fairly stating what any of these apostles thought of his divine office. The book we call the Acts, as far as we have considered it, is the history of a kingdom actually set up in this world — not confined to it, of course, but by its very nature joining the visible to the invisible, the temporary to the eternal, the earthly to the heavenly, the human to the divine — yet no more about the future than about the present. Every apostle's preaching, as Luke reports it, points the same way. When Peter speaks on the day of Pentecost, he declares that Jesus, whom the rulers had crucified, was both Lord and Christ, risen from the dead because death could not hold Him; and those pricked to the heart were not told of some distant punishment or bliss, but to repent and be baptized for the remission of sins and the gift of the Holy Ghost, and to save themselves from *that* untoward generation. So too, addressing those amazed at the healing of the lame man, Peter says all the prophets foretold *these* days, that God is fulfilling the covenant with Abraham that in him all families of the earth should be blessed, and that God raised up His Son Jesus to bless them by turning each of them from his sins — a present blessing, an immediate deliverance, with future ones spoken of only as flowing from this. The joy and singleness of heart of the first community at Jerusalem sprang, on Luke's account, from feeling they were now brothers of one another and of a common Lord, not from dreams about another world. Their thanksgiving when the house was shaken sprang from their conviction that Jesus was the King David spoke of, against whom the Gentiles raged in vain, and whose kingdom they were to attest by acts and words of healing — the language of joy in a deliverance already accomplished, not of some good hoped for or evil to be escaped after death. Stephen, his face shining like an angel's, might be expected to dwell on what awaited him, but his defence is a history of God's kingdom gradually shown on earth and his countrymen's resistance to it; and the sight that cheers him before he sleeps is of Christ standing at God's right hand, a Conqueror and King. The great joy Philip brings the Samaritans is not by telling what shall be, but by preaching Christ who now gives the Spirit whose power the enchanter tried to imitate and would gladly have bought. The revelation to Paul — who had cried, "Who shall deliver me from the body of this death?" and who in Gamaliel's school may have wrestled with questions of a world to come — was not of a way to better his state hereafter, but of Jesus the deliverer from present sin and an evil conscience, who had brought reconciliation to Gentile as well as Jew. So when he speaks most solemnly in the synagogue at Antioch in Pisidia, warning that despisers may wonder and perish, his message is that a work has been done in *these* days which they would not believe, that God has fulfilled to their children the promise made to the fathers — Jesus raised from the dead not, as we might expect, to prove that they will rise, but to prove He is the only-begotten Son to whom the sure mercies of David are given. To the Lycaonians Paul speaks not of future rewards or punishments but of the God who made the world, has done them good, and now bids them turn from their vanities to serve Him. To the Athenians he speaks of a God in whom they are *now* living, moving, and being, whose offspring men are, who has overlooked their ignorance but now calls all men to repent. He does add that God has appointed a day to judge the world by the Man He has ordained, assured by raising Him from the dead — a clear claim that a judgment is coming on the earth to which all men, wise and unwise, worshippers of known and unknown gods, are subject. But this is not the kind of information about the future that those bred on Tartarus and Elysium, eager for any new doctrine of either, would have expected. A present God, a present King, a present Judge, was his message here and everywhere.

If you keep this in mind, you will be ready to read the apostles' letters and find that in them, as in their preaching, the doctrine concerns an actual kingdom that men are invited to join or already belong to. If so, we ought to understand each writer's position within this kingdom; that may clear away perplexities and contradictions we have tried in vain to remove by other means. Besides the Evangelists, there are, so far as we know, only five contributors to the New Testament canon: St. James the less, his brother St. Jude, St. Peter, St. Paul, and St. John. I take them in that order. I put James first because it is plain, from the 15th and 21st chapters of the Acts, that he held the place all antiquity assigns him: whether because the other Apostles had left Jerusalem or for some cause unknown to us, he became overseer of the Mother-Church of the world. I turn at once to ask what aspect of the Gospel his Epistle shows us.

### ST. JAMES.

I think it has been shown clearly that this Epistle was not written to soften or counter any hard sayings in Paul's letters: the two were aimed at quite different people, those who read one in the first century were unlikely to read the other, and James's was probably written earlier. But this does not prove there is no great difference between the mind and aims of the two Apostles. The difference shows on the surface of their letters, and we grasp it better the deeper we go. The question is whether this difference affects the unity of the New Testament books in their witness to Christ and His Church. Is James in his Epistle *not* speaking of a Son of God? Is that *not* the leading idea he is bringing out?

James calls himself a servant of Jesus Christ. The name occurs only two or three times in the letter—one of its most remarkable features. He writes to the twelve tribes; whatever we say today about lost ten tribes and restored two, he recognizes no such distinction. He treats the Jewish nation as a whole, as it was first constituted in Jacob's descendants. But why, you may ask, does this Christian address a nation most of which had rejected Jesus as king? I can only take his words as I find them: he writes to the twelve tribes, and confesses himself the servant of Jesus Christ. That is the fact, whether I understand it or not. Yet I could not understand him taking any other course. He felt his belief in Jesus Christ was the sign of his being a true Israelite; that belief let him appreciate his own position. He wanted his brethren to understand and appreciate theirs—to feel themselves truly twelve tribes, a united nation, and to know how and why they were so. So far from making them careless of their privileges, he wanted them more alive to them.

Do I suppose he was addressing only those *not* baptized into the holy Name, who did not confess Jesus as the Christ? No. He could not exclude Jewish believers from the twelve tribes even had he wished; they gloried in their national position, and he had taught them to prize it. He could make the best use of their claim to the privileges of the Old Covenant, using it to instruct them in the nature of the new.

He evidently assumes those he addresses were passing, or likely to pass, through great afflictions—which does not limit the letter to converts. Converts often suffered from their countrymen, but no worse than the whole body of Israelites underwent throughout the empire in the quarter century before the city's destruction. James addresses these sufferers in paradoxical language: "Count it all joy when ye fall into divers temptations." Our translators chose the right word, though often censured for not preferring another; James's meaning is lost if we suppose the *trials* were not *temptations*. Those in them would be tempted to distrust God, to think He had deserted them. Precisely because they would be so tempted, their state was good for them: if an enemy was turning their circumstances to ruin their faith, a friend was using them to awaken it. God, ordering all things for them, was not tempting them to *evil*—let no one imagine that. He was cultivating their trust and patience. Their own lusts drew them aside; but every good and perfect gift came down from the Father of lights, with whom is no variableness or shadow of turning.

Here begins James's message to the twelve tribes: the God of Abraham, their fathers' God, is the source of perfect unmixed good. No evil comes from Him. Every circumstance is to be welcomed as His instrument of good. They are yielding to something opposite to Him if anything leads them wrong. No lesson was more needed by a Jew of that time. If he believed in Jesus, he needed this truth as the only foundation his belief could rest on. If he did not believe, the truth stood, and to acknowledge it was the first step toward confessing Jesus as the Christ.

James continues: "Of His own will begat He us with the word of truth, that we should be a kind of first-fruits of his creatures." Everything is traced to God's will as the ground of their calling: they had not chosen themselves; He had chosen them. Any Jew who knew his Scriptures would recognize this as the source of their covenant and history, and a believing Jew was bound to refer every privilege to it still more thankfully. But what of the next word—He *begat* us? The idea of paternity enters here. A believer who understood his baptism would say at once: we have not merely a servant's calling but the adoption of children. Yet a reader of Isaiah or Malachi would not stumble at such language—"I have nourished and brought up children, and they have rebelled against me"; "Have we not all one Father?" How can we claim the I AM, in whose sight the angels are not pure, as our Father? That is the question James would have wished to stir in readers of the prophets; and equally he would have wished Church members to feel they had not gained some special privilege for the first time by a new magical operation, but that their highest privilege was to know fully the relation God had gradually revealed to their fathers.

James adds that they were "begotten by the word of truth." I do not insist this be read as an Alexandrian Jew would, by the great passage in Proverbs chapter 8; I do not insist that the word of truth must mean a person. James may not have meant his own language to raise that question directly—the Old Testament had already raised it. The Jews were full of conscious questioning whether the Word or Wisdom who was with the Lord, whose delights had been with the sons of men, must not be a living bond between men and God, the channel of all spiritual communication. Even in its lowest sense the phrase meant to every Jew that the unseen God, whom he might picture in no image, had held converse with his mind and heart. Whatever *more* it means, it means nothing *less* than that God, by communicating with His creature, had brought him out of natural darkness and the worship of visible things into spiritual guidance. The Israelite's circumcision, his separation from surrounding nations, signified this, and lawgivers and prophets kept explaining it as its intent. It was most needful that the believer in Jesus should not imagine *his* privilege was different, an outward economy rather than an inward government. James also reminds the Jew that, rightly understood, his privilege made him only a kind of first-fruits of God's creatures. The covenant to Abraham could not be fulfilled unless in him and his seed all the families of the earth were blessed. What hint could be more valuable to a converted Jew than that his little sect of Nazarenes was only the first-fruits of a vast coming harvest?

Here, as I see it, is the great principle of James's Epistle, from which all his exhortations and warnings follow. It is a Jewish principle, as I have tried to show—but is it any less an evangelical one? Would James have been a more spiritual teacher had he kept repeating the name *Jesus Christ* instead of leading his countrymen to study the very nature of His government? Had he taken that course, which his modern critics think most apostolical, I think he would have proved his grasp of the Gospel was more nominal than real. I do not mean the name Jesus Christ lacks precious significance and reality, or that there were not teachers—some carelessly grouped with James—whose special office was to bring out that Name in its fulness, presenting the acts of the Man Christ Jesus as objects of faith, uniting His work on earth to His work in heaven. But their task would have been inadequately done without a James to display first and foremost Him whose mind the divine word uttered, so that Jews might ask how all their old covenant's prophecies—that God would one day prove Himself their Father—could actually be fulfilled.

Does the apparent confusion of two classes of Jews, so utterly different in feeling and habit, seem strange to today's reader? I dare say it does, and I do not wonder. But I ask him first to consider whether I am putting this into the letter, or whether its superscription and much of its character do not compel this reading. Next, whether each class did not learn something it much needed through this apparent confusion. Lastly, whether the passages that clearly point to the believer in Jesus are not warnings against evils the Church was inheriting from the Jew—evils it was in danger of preserving and passing on, most criminal in the Church because they were especially treasons against the Man of Sorrows.

Consider these questions. James says that, since God had begotten them by the Word of truth, they should be swift to hear, slow to speak, slow to wrath, and receive with meekness the engrafted Word able to save their souls; but they must be doers, not hearers only. A mere hearer is like a man who sees the form of his creation in a mirror, then goes away and forgets what manner of man he is. All this flows from the truth that a divine Lord and Teacher was near, whose voice they were drowning with their own words and passions, who was ready to implant the seed of a new life, after whose image they were formed, and by obeying whose commands they proved their kinship with Him and His authority. Every Jew instructed in the law, who entered into the Psalms and Prophets, acknowledged such an invisible government. This prepared them to acknowledge the divine Teacher who spoke with human lips yet directly to the inner mind. And a believer in Jesus could little understand his position if he merely confessed a teacher born at Bethlehem and dead on Calvary, and did not own Him as the secret ruler of his heart.

Being convinced the believer in Jesus was the true Jew, James practised all the religious observances his position in Jerusalem allowed. The Acts and the passages in Josephus leave no doubt he was, in the Jewish sense, the most religious man of the city. Yet here he says that if a man seems religious but does not bridle his tongue, his religion is vain, and that pure and undefiled religion before God is to visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and to keep oneself unspotted from the world. Believing or unbelieving Jews could scarcely receive a more remarkable testimony from such a source. The unbelieving, tempted always to make devotion consist of observances, were told those observances themselves meant there was an invisible Ruler conversing with and governing the heart. The others, inclined to use their descent from Abraham as a reason for separating from Gentile brethren, were led straight to the inward principle and practical result that alone gave their privileges value.

Then comes a passage that directly mentions the faith of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Lord of glory. What prompts this apparent departure? This faith, he says, is not to be held with respect of persons. If a man came into their assembly with a gold ring and fine clothes, and a poor man in shabby dress, and they honoured the well-dressed man—"Sit here in a good place"—and told the poor man, "Stand there, or sit under my footstool," would they not be partial and judges of evil thoughts? Such things had happened in Christian assemblies, and had drawn the notice and contempt of unbelieving onlookers—themselves great worshippers of Mammon—who saw the contradiction at once among the Nazarenes. James admits and proclaims the contradiction for both classes. He grants it is far grosser in those who confess Him who was called the Carpenter's Son, who took the form of a servant, than in anyone else. So he shows by a new argument how Jesus fulfilled the law. Why should believers not treat any poor man as beneath them? Because Christ came to embody, and to let them embody, the royal law, "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself." A royal law indeed—the law in the king's own heart—which, if they would not obey, left them under another law, one that must be kept at every point, a law of slavery; whereas they might obey the law of liberty, by which they would be judged, if they did not prefer the other. What a fine way of teaching a Jew the meaning of his own Deuteronomy—and how the Christian's sin becomes a lesson on how the Jew needed Christ, that the law might not be destroyed but fulfilled.

The famous argument about faith and works follows naturally. "What doth it profit though a man say he hath faith and have not works? can faith save him?" We think this a Christian temptation; it was emphatically a Jewish one too. James's example makes it clear: "Thou believest there is one God"—thou thinkest thyself unlike other men, worshipping no idols. Excellent. But the devils also believe and tremble. Faith in a God who tempts us for our good, from whom comes every good gift, who begat us of His own will—that James had put forward as the pillar of life: trust in a living Being who would work in them to do right. But faith merely *that* there is one God, or *that* there is a Christ, or *that* Jesus is the Christ—faith not *in* a living Being but *about* one—could that save anyone, raise him from sin, make him better? No; like the devils he would believe and tremble, utterly separated from the Being he confessed, receiving no energy from Him. Abraham believed God—not merely that there is one God—and so offered Isaac, because God worked with him, and by works his faith was made perfect. As the body without breath is dead, needing a quickening influence to move, so faith that does not attach to a living person and receive a vital breath from Him is dead. Faith, in James's view, is not itself the quickening principle; that must be what responds to faith—God's power working in the man who trusts Him.

The next passage, on governing the tongue, agrees with all this. "What an unevangelical subject for an Apostle," some Pauline partisan says with lips or heart. Yes—if the dominion over the inner man, the government of heart and will that James connects with governing the tongue, belongs to mere legal doctrine, then James is essentially legal. But to some, light on this subject seems most spiritual, taking them out of the circle of laws that merely restrain conduct and carrying them to the springs of all divine life. Here especially James sets forth the law of social life, whether in nation or Church, under new economy or old. The same unbelief in God's actual government of the inner man, the same bitter envying and strife of heart, was destroying Jerusalem and threatening the Christian community then and in all days. All good things come from above; but the craft that set them plotting against each other was not from above but earthly, sensual, devilish. Yet the other wisdom was given them, though they might pervert it—the wisdom first pure, then peaceable, gentle, easy to be entreated, full of mercy and good fruits, without partiality and without hypocrisy.

This part ends with the law of peace, kept by obeying the divine Spirit of peace. The next opens: "From whence then come wars and fightings among you?" Surely from the lusts in your members warring against that Spirit of peace. Submission to Him is the secret of all quietness; resistance, of all discord within and without. All pride, self-glorying, and planning for the future spring from the same unwillingness to submit to the inner Ruler, the same unbelief that He is working in us to do right and withstand wrong.

The last passage, our fifth chapter, refers directly to a judgment the Apostle believed was approaching. The hour was coming when the proud rich man would answer for the labourer's wages he had fraudulently kept back, when his gold and silver would be found cankered, and their rust would witness against the hoarders and eat their flesh like fire. To Mammon-worshipping Jews, whether professing faith in Jesus or rejecting Him, the words applied equally. James was no respecter of persons; he exempted neither them nor their heathen persecutors, but wished each to take the lesson home—and each could also strengthen himself with "Be patient, therefore, brethren, unto the coming of the Lord." If they acknowledged Christ come in the flesh, they could not rest until it was shown to all that He was the King. If they had not, but still looked for a great king and judge, let them wait—only asking whether any but such a judge as this, One who had cared and suffered for men, could be the deliverer they wanted, or one they could bear to look upon. The patience of Job, of the prophets, of all before them, was recorded for their encouragement. They might be patient as these were, checking restlessness and waiting for deliverance, and they would not be disappointed—deliverance was coming. But if they were weary, discontented, hopeless—Jews, Christians, why was that?

Such, I think, is the tenor of this Epistle, which some would have us believe was written to discourage faith and teach conformity to an outward law. It is, I take it, one of the most wonderful exhortations to trust in an inward spiritual teacher, law-giver, and life-giver. It is the Sermon on the Mount re-delivered after the Spirit had descended who could make men perfect as their Father in heaven is perfect. Had its writer not acknowledged a Son of God and Son of Man who had set up His kingdom among men, his Epistle would be a string of incoherent sentences, useless for any moral purpose. That faith turns it into a living whole, one of the most practical discourses any age could meditate on.

### ST. JUDE.

We know almost nothing about St. Jude. He called himself the brother of James. Placed at the start of a short letter, that description shows he wanted to claim a bond with James that was more than mere kinship. In any case, he must have been writing to people who knew his name and held it in a reverence they felt for no other Apostle, perhaps for no other man. Although his opening address is general in form, and although his warnings belong to every age, we may fairly assume he meant them especially for the church at Jerusalem, and we must assume they applied to things he actually saw happening in his own time. Anyone who imagines that words so direct, personal, and terrible were merely composed in a study for the benefit of later generations, and were not meant to warn his own contemporaries of a coming judgment and a coming apostasy, has given up belief in the writer's sincerity—and, I fear, in his own. But if that is so, and we do not intend to strike this letter from the canon (and it is hard to see why anyone would later forge a document so clearly tied to that time), then we must abandon any idea of the primitive Church's purity that clashes with this: that the Church was shaken to its center by the upheavals before the fall of Jerusalem; that there was not merely decline but real apostasy in individuals and churches; and that the judgment on Jerusalem was an even sharper test for those who had received the Gospel than for those who had denied it. All our Lord's words led us to expect this. If the plain language of the Epistle to the Hebrews means anything, it must have been so. St. Jude's letter, coming from a source utterly unlike the one from which the Epistle to the Hebrews is thought to come, gives the clearest confirmation of all its warnings.

### ST. PETER. FIRST EPISTLE.

Anyone who studies the passages in Acts about St. Peter will conclude, I think, that he stayed in Jerusalem only for a short while, though he may have returned often, especially on great occasions like the debate over circumcision. We hear of him staying at Joppa, going to Cæsarea, being found at Antioch. In his Epistle he addresses the strangers scattered through Pontus, Cappadocia, Galatia, Asia, and Bithynia as someone who has visited and lived among them. If we connect these hints with the tradition that he was at one time Bishop of Antioch and at another of Rome, and with the great church theory that makes him the universal Bishop, we may reach a clearer idea of him than these contradictory reports give on their own. Need we suppose that Peter ever held anywhere a position like that of St. James at Jerusalem? May it not have been God's intention — carried out by what would be called natural accidents — that one apostle of the circumcision should oversee the first Christian community, live in the city, model righteousness there, write from it to the twelve tribes, and be martyred there; while another apostle, also chiefly of the circumcision though the one who opened the Kingdom to the Gentiles, should exercise a general oversight, never bound to one place, and claiming no fixed jurisdiction even where he lived a while? Those who know the records of the first century will recall several difficulties about Antioch and Rome that this idea would reconcile, and will see how the opposite idea gained ground by forcing a fixed later pattern onto an earlier time. But the main reason for some such view of Peter's office, I think, comes from the Epistle itself.

At the end of the Epistle our translators put the words "The Church which is in Babylon, elected together with you, saluteth you." Nearly all modern commentators protest that this is a monstrous paraphrase of the original. But it is open, I think, to an objection as grave as the linguistic one. How could a Church at Babylon be "elected together with" those to whom Peter wrote — the strangers dispersed through several countries in Asia? One might, by a bold and unusual figure, say the Church at Philippi was elected together with the Church of Ephesus or Rome. Here the phrase has no force at all. The fact is that the word *ecclesia*, in St. Paul's sense, is not and could not be found in Peter's Epistle. For him the Jewish nation was still the body called out of all lands. Gentiles might be joined to it, but it remained the heart of any divine society that would form in the world. That is why Peter's Epistle has always been called a Catholic Epistle: not limited to any one city, it belongs to a body which, though far from universal, carried the seed of universality, and by its dispersion through many lands bore witness to a society that would belong equally to all.

In these remarks on what may seem merely the Epistle's outward form, I believe I have pointed to its most essential characteristics. Everyone sees it is in some ways very unlike the Epistle of St. James. De Wette and other German commentators exclaim that its tone is almost Pauline. There I cannot agree. In its method and immediate aim I think it is as distinct from Paul's letters as from James's. But in its spirit and ultimate object it is in harmony with both.

The obvious difference from James's Epistle is the constant naming of Christ. It was by the resurrection of Jesus Christ that the God and Father of our Lord had begotten them. Their faith was to be tried by fire so it might be found unto praise and glory at the appearing of Christ. The spirit of Christ in the prophets had testified beforehand of Christ's sufferings and the glory to follow. They were to hope for the grace to be brought them at the revelation of Jesus Christ. They were redeemed from their empty way of life by the precious blood of Christ.

Clearly Christ is here set before them as an object of faith, a centre they were to turn to and to which God was continually drawing them. Such a centre was exactly what scattered men enduring great affliction needed. Jerusalem still existed — but could they feel about it as they once had, as their capital and home? The temple still stood — but was it a real bond of sympathy to wanderers and outcasts? Peter leads them away from city and temple to Him who had given the city its worth in the eyes of all who came to it, who had dwelt in the temple and met true worshippers there. Coming to Him as a living stone, rejected by men but chosen and precious to God, they too as living stones were built into a spiritual house to offer spiritual sacrifices acceptable to God through Jesus Christ. This idea — a divine temple made of living men, held together only by a corner-stone who is Christ — runs through the whole Epistle. From first to last he tells them of a unity that existed for them and could be enjoyed despite their dispersion, if only they recognized its living ground and moved round the true centre instead of trying to exist as separate atoms apart from it.

With this aim always before him, Peter could not write to Jews simply as Jews, the way James was right and bound to write. What Peter chiefly wanted to press was not their election — though he wanted them to remember and understand it — but the bond of their fellowship with one another. And that fellowship they could realize only when they confessed a living Christ implied in law, priesthood, and temple. However the dispersed Jews might wish for brotherhood and use the name of brother, they would stay divided in heart and mind, not only in place, until they confessed as their common brother One above all place, who had ascended on high, and loved and rejoiced in Him with joy unspeakable. But they could not do this unless they believed He had actually suffered for them and with them — unless they acknowledged Jesus the sufferer to be the Christ.

So Peter's Epistle is in one sense more exclusive than James's. It seems more sharply to cut off the unbelieving Jew from the blessings of the new covenant. But it does so by no formal edict of excommunication. The Jew had a right to see himself as belonging to a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a peculiar people. He was only to understand what his royalty and priesthood and election rested on — not on anything external, but on Him by whom they were redeemed, by whose blood they were sprinkled. Their union was thus shown to be holy, spiritual, and inward, grounded on their relation to One known only by faith, who had come to deliver them from outward vanities and bring them to an inheritance eternal, incorruptible, undefiled, and unfading.

And in another sense this Epistle is more comprehensive than James's. Though the Jew is always in Peter's mind, though all his language and thought come from the Old Testament, though it is the Shepherd, the Corner-stone, the Priest of Israel he sets before them, there is nothing here that any baptized Gentile could not feel as his own and gladly claim. If this living stone is the bond of a spiritual temple, it must be because all men are spiritual beings related to Him, and apart from Him do not know their own spiritual condition. So while Peter uses the scattering of the circumcised across heathen nations as witness of a real divine federation that circumstances and persecutions cannot destroy, he also shows how a body could exist in all lands with no mark of circumcision at all, resting only on the Name of Him who called them out of darkness into His marvellous light. At times we are almost tempted to think the Gentiles were specially in his mind, as when he says they "were not a people, but are now the people of God; which had not obtained mercy, but now have obtained mercy." Yet such words would not seem strange to Jews long cut off from spiritual privileges, treated as strangers among Gentiles, with no real sense of communion with each other. And if, as I think we may rightly hold, Peter had met many descendants of Israelites who never returned to Jerusalem under the decree of Cyrus — who had known nothing of the restored city or second temple — then their recognition as members of Israel, joint-heirs of a divine inheritance, called by the divine Shepherd into His fold, must have been good news indeed.

I need not point out how carefully Peter builds every relationship — husbands and wives, parents and children, masters and servants — on the common relation to Christ; how the duties of elders to their flocks, their not lording it over God's heritage, the duty to civil governors, and all mutual services and courtesies rest on the same ground; how the whole framework of human society is shown to rest on Him who gave up Himself — on self-sacrifice, and so on that one accepted sacrifice of which all others are images and in whose power all others are offered. But I would ask whether the idea of filial obedience and sacrifice, of a Son come to make men sons of God, is not as much the subject of this Epistle as of the last — whether their great differences do not make this central resemblance stand out the more. And I would raise a question I may press another time: whether, of all the Epistles in the Bible, Peter's is not the one which — by its statements and its omissions alike — proves that Jesus Christ the Son of God, and no man whatever, certainly not Peter himself, is the rock on which the Catholic body must stand, and against which the gates of Hell shall not prevail.

### ST. PETER. SECOND EPISTLE.

People have imagined allusions to St. Paul in the Epistle of St. James; in the Second Epistle of St. Peter his name is openly named. Clearly such a reference is unlikely in the one case but not in the other. St. James wrote to the twelve tribes—people who, believers or not, regarded the Apostle of the Gentiles with suspicion and dread and were in no danger of misusing his authority to their own ruin. St. James was thoroughly practical; no reader of his letter can suspect him of fighting tendencies his disciples were not prone to, for he aimed straight at the diseases he had seen in them. St. Peter, on the other hand, wrote to those who "have obtained like precious faith with us." As his first letter's address shows, he had travelled over the very ground where St. Paul's flourishing Churches stood; and though he saw Christians not as belonging to particular cities but as members of the commonwealth of Israel, he had been first to admit Gentiles into that commonwealth. So he would inevitably meet St. Paul's converts, or people influenced by him directly or at second hand, and might—as St. Paul himself did—need to point out grave and dangerous errors they had fallen into.

No one has doubted that this Epistle throughout denounces Antinomianism. The word can be used loosely for any doctrine leading to neglect of good works; in that sense St. James certainly fights it. But Antinomianism in its strict sense—opposition to outward law—is not what St. James combats; he rather warns against forgetting the inner government God exercises over heart and tongue, and against a faith that is not in a living God and so bears no living fruit. St. Peter deals with a different mood: the tendency to make the liberty of the Gospel an excuse for disobedience and rebellion. That this could result from St. Paul's doctrine misunderstood has never been denied, though it could arise from very different causes too; and among the provincial Jews their circumstances might give it a political turn. St. Peter does not name his beloved brother to hint that Paul stirred this up, but to claim him as a fellow-witness to the truth Peter himself asserts—that God's long-suffering is not an excuse for recklessness but a power leading to salvation and holiness—while noting that the unstable and ignorant had drawn the opposite inference from Paul's writings.

The use here of words nearly identical with St. Jude's, certain peculiarities in its composition,[^fn004] and perhaps the reference to St. Paul, have made many German critics of our day suspect the Epistle is not genuine. I make no pretence of explaining the resemblance to Jude, and there are many passages I do not understand. But I cannot think that, if some book outside the Bible had been ascribed for many generations to a given author, sober critics would count these sufficient grounds for denying it to him. I do not feel much hangs on the decision. Were the evidence strong I would accept it, and I admit the difficulties deserve careful study. But there are strong internal marks that it belongs to the first age, if not to St. Peter himself—marks certain modern prejudices tempt critics to overlook.

[^fn004]: Among these is the allusion to the Transfiguration. It is true that spurious epistles tend to bring in broad, frequent references to events familiar to the reader, making a violent demand on his faith that a simple, truthful writer need not make; and the canonical epistles are remarkably free of allusions to our Lord's life in the flesh, though belief in those events must, on any view, have been common among the converts. But that one such allusion appears in the writing of the only Apostle who could have introduced it (I set aside St. John for later) seems a very strange reason for discrediting the book. Such an allusion would have been quite out of place in James or in Paul; I cannot feel it out of place in a letter whose whole aim is to set the glorified humanity of Christ before the disciples' minds.

As I said with St. Jude, it does not suit our notions that members of the primitive Church should be charged with crimes as great as those that destroyed the world before the flood or the cities of the plain. We attach to "the apostolic age" and "the first age" impressions of purity that no testimony will make us drop—until we adopt the Apostles' own consistent language and see that they write not about the beginning of an age but about the end of one. I cannot say how important I think this scriptural usage is, or what harm has come from replacing it with the opposite. For instance, it has been thought necessary to read this Epistle as applying to a time far beyond Peter's own, though every exhortation must strike plain men as unsuitable and deceptive unless it refers to the actual circumstances of his readers and to the fears their teacher held for them.

If the reader is once convinced Peter is writing about a distant future, no wonder he finds the Epistle fragmentary and disjointed, and readily supposes the opening paragraphs on Christian virtues are Peter's while the rest was accidentally tacked on from elsewhere. But if he can overcome his reluctance to believe that the Apostles feared their converts might sink into the very worst moral state, losing all hold on truth and righteousness, and that a judgment was coming on their world more terrible in principle and outcome than Noah's flood, he will find the opening peculiarly fitted to such a situation. I do not hold, with our excellent Hammond, that we must trace Gnosticism at every turn in the apostolic writings, or suppose the elaborate Gnostic systems of the next age already existed. But the Antinomian tendency of that time, as of every time, arose from separating knowledge from obedience, setting the grasp of truths against submission to commands—of that I am sure. And I can imagine no more apostolic way to meet it than Peter's. "You boast of your illumination, your knowledge of the divine and unseen. But that knowledge is first of all the acknowledgment, the ἐπίγνωσις, the inward discernment of a righteous person, one who delivers from sin." The γνῶσις was taken as something in itself, unrelated to faith and still less to moral energy or self-restraint. But faith, Peter says, will not stand unless virtue sustains it; virtue demands knowledge; knowledge requires self-government; self-government, patience; patience, brotherly affection; brotherly affection, love in its highest sense. Each grace is not to be "added on" to the last, as our translators wrongly teach, but brought as fresh fuel to keep a fire already kindled from going out. The etymology of ἐπιχορηγήσατε, and its use elsewhere—even apart from its being joined with σπουδὴν πᾶσαν παρεισενέγκαντες—surely settles this as his meaning.

And this sense runs through the Epistle. They were called to glory and virtue. Let them make their calling and election sure—actually inherit and possess what has been made theirs—so that the supply of grace always ready for them would achieve its purpose, and their entrance into the eternal kingdom of the Lord and Saviour would be real, not merely nominal: an entrance into a kingdom of Righteousness, Peace, and Joy.

To this effort the Apostle would rouse them as long as he lived in this tent or tabernacle. It was not to be a fixed tabernacle, like the one he had once proposed to build on the Mount for Moses, Elias, and Jesus, not knowing what he said. The voice that scattered that dream, "This is my beloved Son," was still in his ears, assuring him of a more fixed and eternal home and warning him that his own tent's stakes would soon be pulled up. That voice was as real now as then, and the glory that shone about them was permanent. They had not, as many supposed, been telling elaborate myths or well-made legends when they spoke of His power and presence; they had been allowed to witness a momentary showing of it, so they could call on their disciples to believe in it and enter into it. That vision let them grasp more firmly the prophetic word that spoke of this power and presence. Peter's readers were applying themselves to that word, perhaps with some fear and restlessness, each seeking his own private solution to its riddles. Their study was not overzealous; they did well to work that mine. But let them remember that the day of the Lord the prophets spoke of was to rise and shine fully in their hearts, and that holy men spoke under the power of the Holy Spirit, so uttering not their own narrow speculations but truths about the nature of God and the good of the universe.

And if they were busy with true prophets, let them not forget what those prophets so often spoke of—the existence of false prophets alongside them. Such had been in every age, and such would arise among themselves.

What follows is a very clear description of these false teachers. Their chief mark would be confusing fleshly independence with spiritual freedom, the freedom to sin with freedom from sin. They would deny the Lord that bought them—that is, deny they had been redeemed, deny He was their Redeemer. Redemption would mean something wholly different to them from its true sense; so they would crave indulgence. In such men spiritual powers would drag their fellows and themselves into deeper sensuality; the sin of Balaam would be repeated in them, for in him the scriptural idea of the false prophet was realized. He was essentially the false man, trafficking with his conscience, using divine wisdom and power to serve his gain or lust. The memory of Simon was probably in Peter's mind; if the later traditions about him can be trusted, he would exactly embody this Christian Balaam. If we take him, as all antiquity encourages, as the head and type of a class, the false prophets of the apostolic age may have been as numerous as ever they were in the days of Elijah or Jeremiah. Peter sees these men as both produced by and producing the Antinomian temper prevailing in the Church—themselves signs of a nearer apostasy and hastening the judgment at hand.

Of that judgment the Apostle speaks in the third chapter. His words, taken alone and without knowing what other apostles and evangelists said, would certainly make us suppose the last days were near, and that the scoffers who asked where the promise of His coming was belonged to that very day, like the false prophets he had just described. The Apostles had told them His coming was drawing near; the mockers would say, "Where are its signs? Where is the promise? Is not the whole course of things just what it always was? Since the first fathers of the old world fell asleep, has the world's monotony been disturbed at all?" The answer is just what the earlier passages lead us to expect: a reference to Noah's time as a parallel to the one approaching—there was to be as great a shock and upheaval in the world's order then as at the Deluge.

So much of his meaning seems clear; on other parts I still wait for light. I doubt anyone is satisfied with our translation of the passage on the dissolution of the elements: that τεθησαυρισμένοι πυρὶ can mean "reserved *unto* fire," or that τούτων οὖν πάντων λυομένων ("all these then being dissolved") equals "seeing that all these things *shall be* dissolved," or that δι' ἣν means "in which" rather than "for the sake of which" or "in consequence of which."[^fn005] The argument surely suffers from these deviations. What effect it would have on men surrounded by temptations to sensuality and lawlessness to hear that at some very distant time the present framework would dissolve, I cannot see. But if it were then dissolving—if the eternal day were emerging from night's mists and shadows—there was good reason to fix their minds on what abides forever and withdraw them from what is transitory by its very nature. This meaning must, I think, be in the words, whatever the correct version, on which I wish scholars would give careful judgment. This much at least I claim: that "one day with the Lord is as a thousand years, and a thousand years as one day" be pondered in silence and awe, not turned into an excuse for making Scripture as vague and flexible as interpreters find convenient. I can conceive no more beautiful and helpful passage, if we use it to bring before our minds that day of the Lord which cannot be measured by time's rules, which lies beyond them and yet is at certain crises shown in full brightness to eyes waiting and ready to welcome it—none that better harmonizes with what the old prophets and the other Apostles say of the day of the Lord, or that better prepares us to understand the same word used by the brother whom Peter says wrote some things hard to understand. But I am sure that if we merely use the thousand years to postpone indefinitely the judgment the first-century Christians were taught to expect in their own time, we shall make the Apostle of the Gentiles even more unintelligible than the Apostle of the circumcision.

[^fn005]: Lachmann has adopted τακήσεται ("shall be melted, consumed, wasted away") in place of the usual τήκεται ("is consumed"), and so far supports our translation of the 12th verse. If his reading καὶ τὰ ἐπαγγέλματα ("and the promises") is accepted, it must give the 13th verse a different character, though I confess I do not see its precise force in that case.

On the whole, then, we may say the two Epistles of St. Peter are emphatically about the Son of God and Son of Man—Epistles that remarkably set Christ personally before these Churches as the living bond of a society, the object of Faith, the deliverer from present evil, the refuge in a fast-approaching judgment that would test men of every class for what they were. The very features we found in St. Mark's Gospel appear here too. Christ is not showing men a Father; rather the Father is leading them to Christ. He is the actual centre they need in their dispersion to make them a people; the righteous Lord each of them needs to keep him from falling back into the abyss of corruption from which he was rescued.

### ST. PAUL.

I mean to take St. Paul's Epistles in the order the Canon gives them. But there is one passage so important in itself, so important for linking St. Luke's narrative to the inner life of the Apostle, and so important because it comes in the very Epistle that brings out the clash between St. Peter and St. Paul at Antioch—the Epistle modern Paulinists therefore love above all others—that I must use it to introduce everything I say afterward.

It is this (Gal. i. 15, 16): "But when it pleased God, who had chosen me from my mother's womb, and called me by His grace to reveal His Son in me, that I might preach him among the Gentiles, immediately I conferred not with flesh and blood." This is, strictly, St. Paul's own account of his conversion. No one supposes he made light of the vision on the road to Damascus; he tells it again and again with its details, and assumes the Galatians know it—no lesser fact could bridge the gap between the man who persecuted the Church and the man who preached the faith he once destroyed. But we want more than the outward fact; we want to know what it meant, what inner process matched it, what was disclosed to the Apostle's mind that a light in the sky could not disclose. Here he tells us: "It pleased God to reveal His Son in me." God showed me that there was One whose warnings and invitations I had been resisting, One who was my Lord, and yet whom I had been persecuting. This revelation first blinded and bewildered me, then lit up all the dark places of my earlier life. From this Son of God had come every right desire and true thought I had ever had; from myself, resisting Him, had come all that was false and evil. But was this Son of God only in *me*? Was He revealed to me only so that I might know my own good and my own evil—because I was different from my Jewish brethren, or merely because I was one of them? No; He was revealed in me so that I might preach Him among the Gentiles. I was made to understand what was true of me as a man, and so was fitted to be a missionary to men. In this way too I fulfilled the calling of a Jew, chosen from my mother's womb to help carry out the covenant to Abraham: "In thy seed shall all the families of the earth be blessed." So I could not confer with flesh and blood or lean for now on outward teaching; I went into Arabia to learn in silence what this communication meant for me and for the world, and to wait for the answer to my question: "Lord, what wouldst thou have me to do?"

So I hold we have the highest warrant for believing St. Paul's special work was to carry this message to the nations: to tell men that the Son of God was in them, that He was the true head and root of their humanity, and that apart from Him they had no life, righteousness, or unity at all. He was to show this in relation to what men found in their own minds, to history, to the calling of the chosen people, to their law, to the order of society, and to the world's condition past, present, and future. He was to show how our Lord's Incarnation, death, resurrection, and ascension bore on His relation to human beings, explained the riddle of their existence, and answered the countless facts, outward and inward, that seemed to argue against His real fellowship with them and rule over them.

This, I say, is the rough idea one would form of the aim of St. Paul's life from this memorable passage. By taking it as the basis of our inquiry we may be able to trace the distinct aims of his several epistles more clearly, without departing from the accepted maxims about them—provided those maxims are not flatly at odds with what the Apostle himself says.

#### I. THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS.

We need not deny that the Epistle to the Romans treats the doctrine of justification by faith, as every Protestant writer insists. Nor need we deny that it deals carefully with Law and the relation of Law to the Gospel. We will not skip the passages asserting the rights of the Gentiles, or those about Israel after the flesh and Israel according to promise. We will not make the letter explain St. Paul's own life and struggles any less than the 7th chapter suggests, nor explain the state of the human race under its two heads any less than the 5th chapter suggests. Whatever aspect the Epistle has shown to any earnest reader, even the most superficial, should receive full recognition from anyone who understands that Christ was revealed in the Apostle so that he might preach Him. But when the various doctrinal and practical statements addressed to this Church are seen in that light, they are no longer fragments—not a random string of opinions and arguments, nor a dry formal treatise in which affection would be out of place. The whole becomes a *Letter*, with a clear and orderly sequence of thought, in which no part could be dropped without damaging the rest, flowing freely from a rich, inspired mind, perfectly fitted to the needs of the particular people addressed.

Those who make justification by faith the one subject of Romans can find some support in every chapter, but chapters 3 to 9 are their main ground. They tend to treat the first two chapters as prologue and everything after the 9th (or the 11th) as epilogue. On this arrangement they trace the argument clearly: chapter 3 shows all are guilty before God; chapter 4, that Abraham and David were forgiven and had righteousness imputed to them by faith under the old covenant; chapter 5, how this becomes available to us through Christ's obedience; chapter 6, that faith without works has no evil moral consequences; chapter 7, how it frees the conscience from a terrible bondage; chapter 8, how it connects with hopes of future glory; chapter 9, how it rests on a divine election, not of the flesh but of the spirit.

This view seems plausible, but examined from various angles it raises many controversies. Does St. Paul really mean that all men without exception are sinners in the same sense—that the words "there is none righteous, no, not one," their throats an open grave, asps' poison on their lips, no fear of God before their eyes, apply to Samuel, Hezekiah, Isaiah? If not, where do you stop? The language sets no limits and gives no hint that it describes only the unfaithful; indeed the whole argument would collapse if it did.

Again, what faith is this that brought such blessings to David and Abraham? What kind and degree of it? Did it look to some event not yet done—was it future or present?
