They have an appearance of wisdom — will-worship, mock-humility, contempt of the body all look like promising means to higher ends — but they have no real worth, spring from the flesh, and do not even satisfy the flesh.

In the following chapter Paul still keeps the manner, and partly the phrasing, of those he warns against. The new teachers would have urged them to raise their minds to things above, not to dwell on things of earth, and to put to death their members set on earthly things — their fleshly appetites. Paul honestly takes up their phrases and turns them to another use, drawing out their true living sense, showing that their aim could not be reached by their means, and that a far higher result could be reached by the faith they denied. They were to seek things above because they had been raised with Christ, who sits at God's right hand; to think of things above, not of earth, because they were dead and their life was hidden with Christ in God, and when Christ their life appeared they would appear with Him in glory. But the members they were to kill were not their bodies but the things destroying body and spirit alike — fornication, uncleanness, base passion, evil desire, and the covetousness that is idolatry, for which, as he told the Ephesians, God's wrath comes on the children of disobedience, and in which they once walked. Now they had put off these, and anger, malice, slander, and foul speech. See how far Paul rises above the Gnostic doctors in what he thinks redeemed men may attain — not glimpses of a spiritual world or converse with aeons and archangels, but the very knowledge and nature of God Himself. And see how far below them he dives in naming the vices men are freed from, striking at the root while they played on the surface. Yet he is the plain practical man, they the speculators. He leads his disciples to common homely morality, which the others usually despised and rarely reached. He uses blunt words — "Do not lie one to another" — knowing well that Christians then, as always, are apt to lie, and that high notions of their own spirituality are no protection. *The* protection is what he has urged throughout: having stripped off the old man with his deeds and put on the new man, renewed in knowledge after the image of his Creator, where there is no Greek or Jew, circumcised or uncircumcised, barbarian, Scythian, slave or free, but Christ is all and in all.

I know of no nobler passage in all Paul's epistles, none showing more clearly how the plainest law of individual morality is bound up with the great principle of human brotherhood, and how that rests on the divine and glorified humanity of Christ. Nor does any passage, with the one following it, show more plainly how we must claim our rights as regenerate men in order to rise continually to newness of life. They *had* put on the new man; therefore put on, as God's elect, holy and beloved, mercies, gentleness, humility — the very humility the Gnostic teachers mimicked with their humble airs — moderation, long-suffering, forbearing and forgiving one another, as the Lord forgave you; and above all this clothing, that love which is the bond of perfection. That last word shows how well Paul remembers his purpose even when most carried away. The Gnostic problem was how to make a perfect, initiated man above all others; Paul solves it by saying that the love which makes you submit to all others and binds you to all others is the secret of perfection.

On this foundation he builds, as in Ephesians, the whole structure of social life that the Gnostics ignored. But first he treats the spiritual life they did prize, and for which they had so many rules. Paul has few, but very comprehensive: you were called in one body into the peace of Christ, so let it rule your hearts; be thankful; let Christ's word dwell in you richly; teach and exhort one another; sing in your hearts to God; and whatever you do, do all in Christ's name, thanking God the Father through Him. Instead of the self-exalting life, the life of men yielding to a blessed power of good that seeks to bless them. Instead of the individual life, the life of mutual instruction and encouragement. Instead of the ambitious life of the would-be sage or saint, the musical life of the churchman who feels himself one of a family and does nothing but in the Name of its Head.

And then come the plain outward family duties — obstacles to spiritual contemplation in the Gnostic's judgment, precious elements of the unselfish spiritual kingdom in Paul's. They are much the same as in Ephesians, but here serve a different purpose. Like his request for prayer that God would open a door for him to speak the mystery of Christ, like the greetings from and to friends, like the greeting in his own hand and the plea to remember his bonds, they all testify that the highest and most divine faith is the least abstract — the most human, homely, and personal.

#### THE FIRST AND SECOND EPISTLES TO THE THESSALONIANS.

These Epistles stand last among Paul's church Epistles in our arrangement, but are commonly thought to have been written first. I have not tried to fix the chronology of the other Epistles, and I will not go into the proofs offered for this view of these. The reason for placing them where they stand was probably doctrinal: the letters to the Thessalonians speak more plainly of a judgment than any before them, so, though they may be early writings, for purposes of theology and church-history they were put after the rest.

The account of this Church's founding, in Acts seventeen, helps us read the Epistles. Paul seems to have passed quickly through other Macedonian cities because they had no synagogues. Thessalonica had one, and there for three sabbaths he reasoned from the Scriptures. His subject suited the Jewish frame of mind: he did not have to prove there was a Christ, but that *the* Christ had to suffer and rise, and that Jesus of Nazareth, the crucified Man, was this Christ. A few Jews believed and joined Paul and Silas, but most of the converts were devout Greeks—apparently proselytes who attended the synagogue. Then came fiercer opposition from the circumcised party than they had yet met. First they stirred up the mob, which attacked the house of Paul's host. Then, in person or through worthless rabble, they tried to alarm the empire's officers with the old charge: these men who have turned the world upside down say there is another king, Jesus, against Caesar's decrees. I call it an old argument because it had been used against our Lord before Pilate. But it was new in Paul's experience: his sharpest quarrels with his countrymen had turned on whether Jesus was the Christ, the Son of God—they had not yet used the one plea that really weighed with their rulers. Here it worked. The rulers were troubled, took security of Jason and the others, and Paul and Silas were sent away by night to Berœa.

So this Church was founded amid unusual persecution, and the manner of its birth foretold storms in its early life. There was no doubt where the opposition would come from or what shape it would take. A violent Jewish faction would denounce the idea that Jesus was their expected King, would use the help of the rulers they most hated to oppose Him, would set up an idea of the Christ as unlike Him as possible, and would welcome anyone who embodied that idea. To Paul these signs plainly foreshadowed his nation's ruin, the events our Lord said would come before that generation passed. So the thoughts of an Antichrist and of a judgment were pressed on Paul by the Thessalonians' situation more sharply than by any other Church. He could not help seeing a hint that Gentile and Jewish maxims might strangely blend in the coming time—the new Christ might wear the purple, yet might also appear in some form satisfying those who wanted an heir of David. The vision was ominous either way. The Jew must deny the God of Abraham more openly than before to bow to an image opposite to that of a King reigning in righteousness. The tyranny of the Caesars must grow yet more brutal and godless. And Christ's dreadful saying—that love would grow cold, that false Christs might nearly deceive the very elect—suggested, even apart from the Churches' own perils, that Christians would share the world's temptations, and that many would fall into an abyss deeper than that of Jews or Gentiles, since their height had been greater. Add to this the tender, motherly affection Paul felt for this Church, like a mother's for a child in the crisis of a disease, and we have most of what we need to interpret the Epistle.

Persecution seems so common to all Churches that the reader may hardly expect it singled out for the Thessalonians. But after going through so many of Paul's Epistles, I can say boldly that persecution is a leading topic in none of them except that to the other Macedonian Church, Philippi—and there its effects differed from what Paul found here. The Thessalonians were not cast down or overwhelmed by their sufferings; despondency was not their chief failing. The opposition they met led Paul, while with them, to speak much of the coming day of the Lord, urging the hope of it as encouragement to patience. Such words were easily perverted. Why toil for bread if some great event was near, which might take them from earthly things or give them command over them as never before? With such indifference to the present often grows an indifference to ordinary morality, not full-blown but showing itself now and then. Speculation about how near or far these events were would fill their minds. With all brightness in the future and only gloom behind, the world before Christ's appearing looked blank and dreary—how sad to have lived and died then! Mixed with these tendencies, which persecution readily breeds, were the graces God uses persecution to call out—courage, endurance, brotherly affection—not wholly free of those bad habits, yet strengthened by having enemies, and often joined with a reckless defiance and proud contempt of the enemies' opinion.

The harmful influences at work in this Church appear only gradually amid Paul's earnest thanks to God for their work of faith, labor of love, and patience of hope. He rejoices that they know their election; that his Gospel came not in word only but in power, in the Holy Spirit, and in full assurance; that they imitated him and the Lord by receiving the word amid tribulation with joy of the Spirit; that they were examples to all believers in Macedonia and Achaia; that their faith was known everywhere; and that from them the word had sounded far and wide. Again and again he recalls the depth of their conversion in those memorable words—that they had turned from idols to serve a living and true God—and again and again he ties that service to waiting for His Son from heaven, whom He raised from the dead, Jesus who delivers us from the coming wrath.

These words show Paul had no intention of explaining away his preaching, or of changing the teaching that first proved so effective, once he found it perverted. This is never his method: he does not qualify truths he had thought necessary before, or swap them for others, but works to bring out their full meaning, since that is the right cure for any partial grasp of them. First he dwells with unusual detail on how he and his fellow-missionaries lived among them—not, as at Corinth, because his aims had been misrepresented or rivals set up, for the Thessalonians returned his love and had been moved from the first by his life as much as his words. That is why he is the more eager to remind them what they saw in him that they knew came from God and that reached their hearts. Was it not his watchful, tender behavior toward them while they were still Gentiles, a gentleness free of flattery and of any greed? Was it not the labor and hardship, working with his own hands so as not to burden them, by which he proved how dear they were? Was it not his holy, blameless conduct among the believers, and the warnings by which he showed them how much care they needed to keep what they had won, and to walk worthy of the God calling them into His kingdom and glory?

Paul could use such language of himself freely when it served a purpose. Any edge of self-praise is taken off in the next sentence, where he thanks God that they received the word from him and Silas not as the word of man but as it truly is, the word of God, which works powerfully in those who believe. That same word worked in the Thessalonians, for they became imitators of the Churches of God in Judea. He mentions those Churches for the reason he gives at once: the Thessalonians' sufferings came from the same kind of people who caused theirs. Jews in Macedonia showed their kinship with Jews in Palestine, who had persecuted the Lord Jesus and the prophets, and the Apostles too, forbidding them to speak to the Gentiles for their salvation, thus filling up their sins—for the wrath had come upon them to the uttermost.

Both his references to his own life and to the Thessalonians' Jewish enemies bear on the weaknesses he saw in them as well as on their hopes. Without directly telling them they were *not* working with their hands, *not* walking wisely toward their heathen neighbors, *not* careful to keep the light burning within and shining without, the memory of what their first teachers were, and the impression that made, was the most effective rebuke he could give. And the hint about the Jews did more than remind them Paul suffered more than they from the same foes: it told them the cause of Jewish hostility—a godless, inhuman refusal to let others share their privileges. It reminded them that the redemption they awaited, and rightly awaited, would also be a day of wrath, not accomplished until God's chosen people had suffered that terrible downfall which, as he told the Romans, he would himself have been accursed from Christ to prevent.

The appearing of Christ, then, which Paul had taught them to expect, was bound up with sorrow and tribulation both for themselves and for the world. It was not something he or they could look to as the fulfilment of selfish dreams. He tells them he had longed to come to them, it seems, for the very purpose of saying this: "Are you not our joy and hope and crown of rejoicing before our Lord Jesus at His appearing?" The phrase seems thrown in by the way, bursting from the fulness of his heart with no bearing on his argument. But an inspired man's words, however little they seem to fit a dry argument, must unfold the thought governing him. In telling them his feeling toward them, he has taught them a whole lesson about the day they both await: it is a day he desires for their sakes and the Church's, because Christ will be glorified in them and shown to be Lord of both Jew and Gentile.

Unable to come himself, he says he sent Timothy to comfort and strengthen them, so their tribulations might not give the tempter an opening but be received as their appointed discipline. Timothy's report cheered him and called out fresh thanks to God, making him pray the more earnestly to see them. He trusted God and the Lord Jesus would direct his way to them; but he trusted still more that God would make them overflow in love to one another and to all, so as to establish their hearts blameless in holiness before God at the appearing of our Lord Jesus with all his saints.

Everyone must see, from how Paul keeps returning to it, that "the appearing" is the key phrase of this letter. We have already noticed how frequent it is in all his epistles, but nowhere does it stand out so plainly, for nowhere else does he so set himself to clear up the perplexities bound up with it and to tie the Church's life to it. The next passage, which we all know as a standalone exhortation, should never be cut off from what comes before it. The way to prepare for Christ's appearing was to remember those ordinary, plain precepts he had given them and which, in their eager anxiety about the future, they were forgetting. For though such moral rules may look merely hard and external, they rest on the deepest theology. The command "abstain from fornication" rested on the truth that God's will is your sanctification. The rule not to overreach a brother rested on the truth that the Lord is the Judge. Both would be kept if they remembered God had given them the Holy Spirit. Paul was glad they excelled in love to the brethren throughout Macedonia; but he would have them remember their duty also to those outside—that quietness, minding their own business, and working with their own hands were spiritual duties, part of their divine calling.

Let it always be remembered that such lessons were prompted precisely by Paul's belief, which he wished them to share, that the day of the Lord was at hand. The deeper that impression settled in them, the freer they would be from restlessness, the more quiet amid strife, attentive to common duties, and considerate of others. But there was one anxiety that was not selfish, and that grew from the very hope he encouraged: what had become of friends who had died before the Lord's appearing? Was it a calamity to have died before it? The thought was very natural—more natural than we, with our theories of the departed and of ourselves that have little to do with any appearing of Christ, quite realize. Their minds, formed in the Apostolic school, could imagine no blessedness apart from His appearing. Paul's answer gave them his clearest account yet of the nature of that appearing. They were not to limit it by their notions of time or place. If they believed Christ died and rose, they believed in a union of both worlds, a breaking down of the barrier between the state before and after death—the seen and the unseen. If they truly believed this, they would be sure that those they called dead had only fallen asleep, that Christ was as much with them as with Jairus's daughter or Lazarus, that they could hear His voice as those had; and that, being members of Christ's body and drawing their life from Him, God would bring them with Him.

And so he tells them, by the word of the Lord, that "we, the living ones, we that are left over unto the appearing of the Lord, shall not anticipate those that have been put to sleep. Because the Lord Himself with a cry, with the voice of an archangel and with the trump of God, shall descend from Heaven; and then we, the living, shall together with them be caught up in clouds to meet the Lord in air; and so shall we be ever with the Lord." These are among the most musical sentences of Scripture, to which most hearts strangely respond, yet they trouble the understanding as much as any we know. May I suggest that if we followed our hearts, took the words as they stand, and tried less to measure them by our own notions, some of the difficulty might vanish? The first question we ask is: Was Paul then alive at the appearing of the Lord, even granting that appearing was some event of that generation? This question touches a subject we have often dwelt on before; reconsidering it may help us trace the unity of the New Testament books, and would be the best key to the link between the history of the Church and the prophecies of both Testaments.

All the hopes of the old prophets pointed to an appearing of a King of Israel and Son of God. They interpreted the longings, conscious or not, of all men, and said what kind of person "the desire of all nations" must be. I have tried to show the Gospels present such a person. They say: This Jesus is the Son of God and the King of Israel. We tell how, while on earth, the friend of fishermen, the carpenter's son, He showed He held both characters. His crucifixion was the most necessary part of that manifestation and of His work; His resurrection was the final earthly proof that He was what the Sanhedrim and the governor denied; but that resurrection meant He would not remain on earth—it was completed in His ascension to God's right hand, so He was declared Lord and Christ; and the descent of the Spirit at Pentecost showed the ascension meant no separation from His witnesses, enabling them to be real witnesses that He is the risen and victorious Lord and Redeemer of man. Was nothing more involved in the ascension and the gift of the Spirit than this? The more spiritual light the Apostles received, the more certain they were that there was. If Christ had died, risen, and ascended, if He was Lord and Christ, if their testimony was true, there would be an appearing of Him in that character—as real as His appearing as a servant, carrying that appearing to its result as the crucifixion carried out the incarnation, the resurrection the crucifixion, and the ascension and Pentecost the resurrection. All these, strictly, were parts of one appearing, acts of one drama leading to a catastrophe that was inevitable if its great Hero, who had governed the world since its creation, had really come to claim His right over it. It was fully in the prophets' manner to treat all the events of a given generation as parts of one appearing or day of the Lord, though the first might be years from the last, though the events might differ utterly—some bright, some gloomy—though a plague of locusts might lead to an outpouring of the Spirit, and that to some battle in the valley of Jehoshaphat. The link between them all was that they proved God present and at work to put down evil and set up righteousness. John the Baptist, the King's herald, had proclaimed the coming wrath as the great result of His coming, the sign that the kingdom he announced had truly been revealed. And all our Lord's discourses, most of all the one before the last Passover, agree with this.

How then could Paul, steeped in the old teachers' lessons and believing the revelations made to himself, doubt that he was living in a day of the Lord—that day toward which all others had led? Whether or not he would still be on earth at its close—at the appearing which, on his Lord's own word, he fully believed would come in that generation—he was already one of those "alive and remaining" unto that appearing, since he had witnessed the incarnation, resurrection, and ascension, since Christ had been revealed to him on the road to Damascus and had been revealed *in* him, and since he had preached that Son as the Desire of all nations to Greeks and barbarians. This was no play on words. By it he met the Thessalonians' perplexity, removed their fear about their dead, enlarged their sense of the Lord's revelation, and gave them fresh assurance of the revelation still to come—so near—by treating it as one of a series of events, many already fulfilled.

If we have got past this first difficulty, the rest of the passage will not trouble us; indeed, if we accept it, it will be as full of comfort as Paul told the Thessalonians it was. He certainly affirms the Lord would descend from heaven with the archangel's voice and the trump of God. If we believe our Lord's own words about the earthquakes and the distress of nations in that generation, we may well believe a trumpet did sound and an archangel's voice was heard by mortal ears. It does not matter, for Paul's statement, whether many or any of those bound in "the muddy vesture of decay" recognized it or knew it meant the Lord's descent. What he declares is that those who had fallen asleep before Christ's earthly appearing would hear His voice and rise, as those did who heard it in the days of His humiliation—not, like them, to return and walk again among men, but to be with Him and share His glory. And he says that he and the Thessalonians who had not fallen asleep before Christ's appearing, having survived to that great day, would share the same blessing, since none higher was possible. Only there would be this difference: since Christ died and rose, the grave could no longer be seen as receiving those He had redeemed. Even the word *sleep* would no longer fit. The departure of those in Christ would truly be a translation—they would not sink but rise, not go down to meet the worms but ascend to meet Christ. The phrase "meeting the Lord in the air" seems deliberately used to discourage the carnal notion of His coming down as an earthly king to reign visibly. This translation was an object of faith, not sight. The Thessalonians were not to imagine Him robed in the purple of mockery, but coming in the glory of His ascension, the glory of the Father and the holy angels, that those who shared His sufferings might share it.

I said this very literal reading—the most literal I can conceive, and the most in keeping with the rest of the New Testament—must have brought great comfort to the Thessalonians, and may to us. If events soon to occur showed them that the Lord who died and rose was truly present and reigning over the universe, then, however terrible in themselves, those events would fulfil the hopes of one dispensation and open a higher one. They would mark a crisis in the history of the universe that affected not chiefly its visible conditions but the relations between the living and the dead, and set a wholly new meaning on earthly life and death. And if we had courage to believe that what Paul said actually happened, and that we live in that better age he looked toward, we might claim as ours now many privileges we suppose *may be* ours in some distant future; we might see that far more has been done for us and for mankind than we ever dreamed; and we might look with far greater confidence to what will be done when the mists clear and we walk in the full light of God's face.

But to gain that comfort we must attend, as the Thessalonians had to, to the words that follow: "But concerning the times and the seasons, brethren, ye have no need that it should be written to you. For you yourselves perfectly know that a day of the Lord as a thief at night so cometh. When they say, Peace and safety, then a sudden destruction is standing over them... But you, brethren, are not in darkness that the day should come upon you as a thief. For ye are all sons of light and sons of day... let us not sleep, as the rest, but watch and be sober... having put upon us the breastplate of faith and love, and as a helmet the hope of salvation. Because God hath not appointed us to wrath, but unto the procuring of salvation, through that Lord of us, Jesus Christ... that whether we wake or whether we sleep, we may live together with Him." (Lachmann reads "thieves," a tempting change one would adopt if the previous verse did not seem to forbid it, and if it were not too obvious a substitute for the usual reading.) Here lay the great correction of both their irregular conduct and their confused belief. The day of the Lord had presented itself to them as a date to be worked out by calculations which, though drawn from the prophets, followed the very methods of the astrologers and soothsayers the prophets denounced. Paul did not want to make them doubt the day was near—that they were on its eve, indeed *in* it, though its sun had not yet climbed high—but such restless reckoning about particular events and supposed predictions hindered rather than helped their confidence and diligence. By "a day" he meant not twenty-four hours but day as opposed to night, light as opposed to darkness—as he explains to the Romans and Corinthians, and here with more detail because of their particular errors. The more we watch his language, the more we see with what divine skill he opens their minds to a deeper grasp of the truth while giving it a practical force and awe they were likely to lose, and how all his corrections encourage rather than dampen their faith and hope. They had not thought too highly of their present standing; they had only made the future an excuse for belittling it. They were sons of light and of day *now*, not to become so later. Yet they were to expect blessings not yet received, a full salvation from all that crushed them. That hope was a helmet, the best guard against the deadliest assaults; keeping it, they stayed awake; losing it, they would sleep.

The exhortations that follow, though fit for any Church, suit this one's temper especially. One can easily see how restless speculation about the coming time, and the advantage it gave clever diviners over homelier teachers, would keep them from knowing and valuing their appointed guides and their quiet labors of love. Though Paul witnessed to their brotherly love, the Church's peace must often have been broken by rival predictors. An impatience of government, matched by a loss of the gift of governing, may have bred unruly spirits needing admonition. The despondent would be left uncomforted by those busy about times and seasons. There would be intolerance of the weak and that pride of half-knowledge most opposed to sympathy and long-suffering. Some instance of revenge or bitterness toward outsiders may have prompted the warning against returning evil for evil. The other exhortation—to pursue good toward one another and toward all—reminded them they were a Church, a body of witnesses whose conduct to each other testified to the world. "Rejoice always" and "pray without ceasing," like the like commands to the Philippians, gain new force from the tribulations both faced. "In everything give thanks, for this is the will of God in Christ Jesus unto you" reminded them of the truth that persecuted men, and men expecting a judgment on others, easily forget—that God's will is a will to all good, and they were right only when rejoicing in it and yielding to it. The Thessalonians seemed less likely than others to quench the Spirit or despise prophesyings; but the Spirit of God is not the spirit of divination, and prophecies, in the Apostolic sense, are not predictions. Our interpretations have cruelly mangled the beautiful precepts that follow: "Prove all things. Hold on to the good. Hold off from every form of evil." For we have taught a doctrine well suited to our cowardice, but wholly against Christ and His Apostles—that we must abstain from all that *appears* evil in men's eyes. He had told them to test everything to see its worth, and yet we make him tell them to shrink from whatever looks bad to those who judge by appearance and not righteously! Had he preached so at the close of a letter all about Christ's day and judgment, the contradiction would be flagrant. But he is guilty of no such thing. He ends his exhortations with a prayer—not that they abstain from what seems evil to evil men, but that the God of peace would make them wholly holy, and preserve their whole spirit, soul, and body blameless to the appearing of our Lord Jesus Christ.

No one, I think, has ever doubted the connection between the two Thessalonian Epistles. It is closer than that between the two to the Corinthians: the subject is the same in both, and the second is commonly, and perhaps rightly, thought to have arisen from mistakes about the first. From its opening it is clear the Thessalonians' tribulations had continued and grown, while Paul says their faith, patience, and love grew too. This, he says, is "an evidence of the righteous judgment of God, to the end that ye may be made worthy of the kingdom of God; for which also ye are suffering: seeing that it is a righteous thing with God to recompense to those who trouble you, tribulation, and to you the troubled, rest with us in the unveiling of the Lord Jesus from heaven, with the angels of his power, in the flame of fire, giving out recompense to them who know not God, and to them who do not obey the Gospel... Who shall receive the judgment of eternal destruction from the presence of the Lord and from the glory of his power, when he shall come to be glorified in his saints..." I quote this partly to show how much Paul is occupied here, as in the first letter, with a coming judgment, and partly so the reader may weigh the words he uses for its nature and compare them with like expressions elsewhere. No passage would be so readily taken as a classic and central one on the whole question of judgment and punishment. Let us examine it, to get some light on subjects that so deeply concern all men in all ages.

The Thessalonians' faith, patience, and love were, Paul believed, means to their being made (or counted) worthy of the kingdom of God. If that kingdom is one of righteousness, peace, and joy, then patience, hope, and love are the instruments by which it is enjoyed, as a clear eye is the instrument by which a landscape is enjoyed. So our Lord taught in the Sermon on the Mount: the poor in spirit would have the kingdom, the pure in heart would see God—not paid for these qualities by future gifts, but, being poor in spirit, not craving to rule themselves but glad to have the true Lord rule their hearts; and, being pure in heart, able to know Him in whom they live. The righteous judgment Paul awaits is that which declares who are living as children of God's kingdom; and the righteous judgment on the persecutors is its opposite—the trouble and confusion belonging to those who exalt themselves and trample others. In the revelation of Jesus Christ with His angels—that great daylight of the universe showing all things as they are—there will be recompense to those who have not known God and do not obey the Gospel. This recompense he calls eternal destruction from the face of the Lord and the glory of His power. The wicked man's recompense corresponds exactly to the righteous man's: God's true judgment settles the condition of both, and each gets what he sought. As knowing God and being like Him is the one's reward, not knowing Him and being without Him is the other's punishment. This is called eternal destruction, the most terrible state a spiritual being can reach. We are not told how long it lasts. Time enters as little here as in our Lord's discourse in Matthew twenty-five; in both, the language describes a moral condition, and in both the revelation of Christ, which shows each person for what he is, separates those on the right from those on the left. Nor must we forget this judgment, like that in Matthew twenty-five, was the one that would close that age of the world. Those who had called Jesus a blasphemer for saying He was the Son of God would find out what Being they had worshipped under God's name, and what Being they had rejected when they said the crucified Man was not His true image. What better blessing could a faithful man want than to see in that crucified Man the real King of the universe, the full glory of the Father? So Paul, while cherishing the Thessalonians' hope in this judgment of Christ, shows plainly what was to be feared and hoped in it. It was to be earnestly desired because it was true, not because it favored Christians, Jews, or Gentiles—because it would bring out the true image of God and confound all false images set up by anyone, Jew, heathen, or Christian.

And now, on the strength of Christ's appearing and that blessed gathering into Him which fulfils their life as members of a Church, he begs them not to be quickly shaken or agitated—by spirit, word, or letter claiming to be from him—into thinking the day of the Lord was coming instantly. "Let no one deceive you in any wise," he says, "because it will not come unless the apostasy come first, and there be revealed the man of sin, the adversary, that lifts himself above every one called a god, and above every form of worship, so as to set himself in the temple of God, showing that he is God." I could spend volumes on this if I ranged over eighteen centuries asking which famous name best fits and fulfils the description. But since reverence for Paul's words and the harmony of Scripture have forced us to suppose he speaks of events in his own day, I set such questions aside and only try to learn from the passage itself what kind of manifestation is meant. Then we shall better judge any historical facts that answered to the words at that time, and may afterward deal fairly with all the alleged later fulfilments.

There is no doubt, then—few have doubted—that this describes the appearing of an Antichrist, anticipating the appearing of the Christ Paul has spoken of so much. Whatever is opposed to the lowliness of the Son of Man—to the King who came meek on an ass, to Him who ministered instead of being ministered to, who gave Himself for the world—is gathered and concentrated here. The man of sin is set against the Lord our Righteousness; the son of perdition against the Saviour; he who exalts himself above all called God against Him who made Himself of no reputation; he who sits in God's temple showing himself as God against Him who said, "Father, not my will but Thine." Clearly a power is described that claims rule over both the Gentile and the Jewish world, exalting itself above every Gentile worship and proclaiming its superiority to the one Jehovah. The word *apostasy* seems to mean that reverence was paid to this power not only by Jews and heathen but by professed disciples of Jesus. Now, since the deified Emperor always strove to set himself above all called God and to claim all the empire's gods and worships as his own, since much in the old Roman law and faith resisted this, since the temple at Jerusalem was the one abiding witness against it, and every holy man upholding its true worship was a personal check on this final brute-worship, I take it that the whole period from Augustus onward was an approach to this antichristian idea, but that it was never embodied even in Nero. It is no new theory of mine—biblical scholars have often suggested it—that Vitellius shows all the marks presented here. That is better considered when we examine the Apocalypse. I do not ask anyone to accept it, nor pledge myself to it. I feel the uncertainty of even the best such identifications the more strongly as I feel the truth and certainty of Paul's assertion that such a power did exist, and had not yet reached its worst development.

He asks whether they remember he told them these things while with him. Their special opposition from the Jews made such teaching more needful for them than for others. He could not tell them Jesus was, or would be shown to be, the Christ without telling them what a different, opposite idea of a Christ was abroad, and how the world in all its parts would at last find a representative of that idea. He says they also knew what the restraining influence was that kept this mystery of iniquity from breaking out fully at once. Later he seems to make this restraint a person: "He that lets will let till he be taken out of the way." Since Paul must have looked for the great divine center of resistance to the antichristian principle in Jerusalem, and must have felt its Pharisees and Sadducees more possessed by that principle than any worshipper of Jupiter, it is a not unreasonable supposition, held by many, that he located the restraining power in the church at Jerusalem, and especially in the Apostle James. The effect of his "being taken out of the way," on the city that revered his righteousness, and still more on the Church, would answer strikingly to the words of the brother-Apostle so often called his rival. When that great obstacle was removed, the mystery of iniquity long at work seemed to burst forth most mightily where least expected, and nothing but the destruction of the city and temple—so nearly contemporary with the fall of the Capitol, the downfall of Vitellius, and the beginning of a better era—could have kept that hideous, godless tyranny, upheld by all lying wonders and the tricks of diviners, from fixing itself permanently over the world. To the old prophets the overthrow of such a power would have seemed a day of the Lord indeed, far more wonderful and pregnant than the fall of Sennacherib or Belshazzar. But linked with the incarnation, death, and resurrection of the Son of Man, it could be seen as nothing but His appearing to claim the kingdom He had proved to be His Father's, which the power of evil sought to wrest from Him.

On this firm ground Paul can raise thanksgivings and arguments as earnest as in the first letter. He thanks God for choosing them as a first-fruit unto salvation through sanctification of the Spirit and belief of the truth. He prays God would comfort their hearts and strengthen them in every good word and work. He entreats, as he might command, that they keep the rules he gave and separate from every brother who walks disorderly. He explains this by bringing out more plainly a charge he had hinted at before: some were not workers but overworkers—busybodies. "If a man will not work, neither let him eat" is his rule, first shown in his own example. He will have no trifling here—no dainty saints so busy with their spirits they neglect their manual callings, no waiters for the Lord who will not do what the Lord commands. There must be discipline with such people, only they must be warned as brothers, not treated as enemies. Then he asks the Lord of peace to give them peace always in every place, and prays that the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ might be with them all.

#### THE PASTORAL EPISTLES.

The same objection raised against the Epistle to the Colossians is raised against these letters. With the Epistle to Timothy, the objection I already discussed—that Gnosticism is dated too early—is pressed even harder. But the further objection, that these letters assume a Church organization that did not exist until after Paul's death, bears on them as pastoral letters, and touches far less the letters addressed to the various societies in Italy, Greece, or Asia.

The subject is interesting, and I would be neglecting the purpose of these lectures if I passed it by. There is good ground for saying that the nature of the Church in the Apostle's day should not be inferred from what we read of it even at the start of the second century. For example, it would be a great mistake to think the Roman Empire's feeling toward Christians under its *worst* rulers in the first period was as hostile as under its *best* rulers later. Nero treated Christians no more cruelly than his other subjects; he persecuted them at all only by accident, impulse, or temporary convenience. Trajan persecuted them on principle, from the very policy that made him kind to his subjects generally. That vast difference presupposes others. Since it was the Church's organization in the great cities that alarmed intelligent rulers like Trajan or Marcus Aurelius, there is ample excuse for supposing such organization was lacking earlier. And this view is strengthened by noting how little the canonical epistles say of what later seemed the most conspicuous feature of Christian society. "The names of bishops, presbyters, or deacons occur here and there in the letters to the Churches, but their appearance seems accidental; the doctrine and even the practice of the epistle seem hardly affected by whether they are present or absent. So it is easy to suppose they were inserted later, when they were felt indispensable to the Church's existence. And where they do come forward prominently, in these letters, is that not a reason to suspect *those* letters are spurious?"

If the conclusion we reached about the Epistle to the Thessalonians is right, we must certainly expect the Church to stand out much more substantially in the coming age, after the great crisis Paul speaks of, than before. Consider that crisis and the reason is plain. The national organization the Jew regarded as sacred and divine—the world's central organization—was to be shaken and broken up. The expectation was that the Church would emerge from this wreck as the root of a new human community. Only in that form would it be the fully developed society of which Pentecost's society was the germ; only then could the world the Cæsars ruled feel it as a rival kingdom. Before that, Romans still saw it as one of the Jewish sects, as the hostile Jews always sought to represent it. How did it seem to the Church's own members, teachers, and Apostles? We have seen this cannot be answered simply; the answer emerges gradually in different aspects. James and Peter were anxious to recognize the old Jewish organization, to speak of the twelve tribes, to make their countrymen aware of their peculiar calling, to present Christ as the son of David come to gather them though scattered among the Gentiles. These Apostles of the circumcision were of course reluctant to seem to be *setting up* a society; they wished only to witness for the true Lord of the society they belonged to and gloried in. Still they *were* a body of witnesses. They needed ministers; deacons were chosen to meet a new necessity; the apostles held the office Christ gave them; the reviving family character of the old Jewish commonwealth suggested the position of the Elders. When an *ecclesia* of Jews and Gentiles grew up in cities Paul visited, all the various functions and gifts appeared, as in the Epistles to Corinth and Ephesus, and were traced to the one Lord and one Spirit. Everything necessary to a body politic was found in each. And Paul carefully reminded each that it was a divine society called out by God, not one he had built or could organize. Here, as at Jerusalem, we hear of the Elder and the Deacon—but their existence is assumed, with no formal account of their creation. They unfolded like trees or flowers, not inserted by any plan or paper constitution. The other name, "Overseers," so important later, appears with no formality in these Epistles; the name is full of meaning, but the meaning is not drawn out. We feel we are in an order—but an order set within another it is not yet able to displace, one that, until God decrees its end, is dear and venerable to the Apostle of the Gentiles as to the Apostles of the circumcision.

My conclusion is this. There is nothing in the Epistles to the Churches, and I think we shall find nothing in the pastoral Epistles, that anticipates the complete, expanded society we meet after the Apostles have left the earth. But there are all the seeds and preparations for that developed order—just as we found at Colosse the seeds of the various later forms of Gnosticism. The pastoral Epistles carry us one step further than the Ecclesiastical Epistles in the history of the Church's constitution. But it is just the step made necessary by what we read there, the step that makes the facts of the second century not incredible or unintelligible.

##### FIRST EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY.

It is generally agreed that Overseer and Elder are not carefully distinguished in Acts or in the Ecclesiastical Epistles. But when someone is left to watch over a Church's affairs, we feel at once that the first name suits his function and the second clearly does not. Without assuming that "Elder" necessarily implied age, we may take it that a young man like Timothy would not have borne that name in a Church he belonged to locally. And the opening of this Epistle gives the clearest support for my remark that the Church's offices evolved by a divine law, not by an artificial rule.

The Apostle is traveling from Ephesus to Macedonia. He sees at Ephesus certain people introducing strange doctrines, giving heed to fables and endless genealogies that stir up disputes. He wants Timothy to stay and warn these men of the harm they may cause. Here at once, in a particular case, we see how the need of a Christian overseer might arise. From this starting point we may learn by degrees what other duties fall to him and what else his office implies.

The phrase "interminable genealogies" is thought to carry us a step further into the history of Gnosticism than the Epistle to the Colossians did. The descent of the various æons and their spiritual relationships are so prominent in the second-century heresies that we assume these must be meant. The opinion seems not unreasonable, but Paul's surrounding language obliges us to qualify it. These genealogists were clearly Jews who set great store by the law. If they owed anything to Persian or Greek teaching, it was wholly subordinate to what they took from the Hebrew sages. So I think we should read the words by what we know of Jewish notions about angels and archangels rather than by the Gnostics in Tertullian or Irenæus. We may hereafter need to reckon with the Jewish element in Gnosticism much more than some modern historians allow. At all events, the men Paul set Timothy to control substituted law for Gospel, and thereby, as Paul says, "hindered that which is the true end and issue of the commandment, love out of a pure heart, and a good conscience, and faith unfeigned."

The words we translate "godly edifying which is in faith" are made a little harder by Lachmann, who reads οἰκονομίαν (stewardship) rather than οἰκοδομίαν (building). But either reading—Lachmann's perhaps even more—hints at the character of Timothy's office, borne out by the rest of the letter. Preserving the Church's order and all that made for true fellowship among its members was to be his first and special aim. He was to understand distinctions in order to preserve unity; to keep notions from replacing belief and action; and for that end to detect what is true in each notion, so that the assumption and exclusiveness that made it false could be rejected. The great opposition of Law and Gospel supplies the test at once. "The law is good, if a man use it lawfully." But the Law exists to restrain and condemn crimes that oppose the healthful Gospel of the glory of God, with which Paul felt entrusted. To put the mere prohibition of evil in place of the power that delivers from it, the righteousness of letters in place of the righteousness of a Person, the righteousness that merely condemns the sinner in place of the righteousness that saves him out of sin—that destroys the life of the Church and of the individual alike. Whoever truly watches over one will also protect the other.

Then, after asserting this great theological principle Timothy is always to keep in view, comes one of those passages of personal feeling and thanksgiving that seem at first to break the argument but in fact bring out its meaning better than the plainest logic could. He thanks Christ, who enabled him for the ministry into which He put him, having judged him faithful—him who was before a blasphemer, persecutor, and injurious. Why do these words come in here rather than elsewhere? Why speak of receiving mercy because he did it ignorantly in unbelief, of grace abounding with faith and love, of the saying more fitted for a young convert than a bishop—"Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners, of whom I am first"? Because in this way he shows most strikingly how the Law could not raise a man who kept its precepts most diligently out of the most evil habits of mind, into which he fell through his very zeal for the Law's authority; because he would show the Gospel doing for him what the Law could not; because he would proclaim to all sinners the blessings he felt he had a claim to only as a sinner. So the words that follow gain full force: "on this account I found mercy, that in me first Christ Jesus might show forth all longsuffering for a pattern to them that should hereafter believe on Him unto life eternal." He himself is the sign, to the hardest legalist and the most corrupt Gentile alike, of the power in Christ to raise them out of both conditions and bring them to that eternal life men crave and find in Him. And perhaps it is not over-refinement to think that in "eternal life," and in the doxology that follows—"to the King of the Æons, the incorruptible, unseen, only God, honor and glory for the æons out of æons, Amen"—there is a silent allusion to the opinions beginning to attach to these words, and a striking testimony against them, since Paul draws all thought away from periods and from spiritual beings to the one original ground of both. However that may be, Timothy's commission clearly rests on this Gospel and its relation to the law, as made necessary by the confused teaching of Rabbinical speculators and allegorists. The words "according to the prophecies which went before on thee"—or rather that were pointing toward thee—seem to say clearly that Paul was not choosing his delegate arbitrarily but conforming to indications that, in every man's case if he will look, declare what he is meant for, and that an Apostle could only regard as divine. I cannot explain the words "in them" in the next sentence; perhaps they mean Timothy should wage his warfare remembering those warnings and foretellings of his calling, needing them when his belief in it grew weak. In any case, the words mean his life, in himself and in the Church, could be nothing but a fight, and that the fight would be won if he put on the armor of faith and of a good conscience—one, he hints, being necessary to keep the other safe. He had seen a tendency to separate them growing in the Church: Hymenæus and Alexander had failed to keep a clear conscience, and so their faith ran aground and was shipwrecked. He had delivered them to Satan—the same formula, doubtless with the same meaning, as with the Corinthian offender—not to shut them out of the kingdom, but to teach them not to blaspheme.

The second chapter opens with hints about a Church's order. Nothing is more worth noting than Paul's first exhortation on this subject: "I exhort then first of all that there be made petitions, supplications, thanksgivings for all men." The Church's order is founded on these prayers. The man left expressly at Ephesus to guard against strange doctrines is told "first of all" to see that prayers are made for all men. If his aim were only to block Gnosticism, no diviner means could be conceived, for Gnosticism is by nature separating and exclusive, busy dividing the initiate from the ordinary Christian and man. But since Paul's zeal against the innovators was wholly subordinate to asserting the Gospel and the divine kingdom, we can assign no lower ground for this command than that he saw common united prayer for all men as the very expression of the Church's mind, the sign of its calling, the means of its triumphs. He adds, "for kings and all those in authority, so that we may lead a quiet and peaceable life in all piety and reverence." But lest this be mistaken for the ground of the prayer—lest Christians think they pray for Nero to disarm his hostility or to escape his cruelties—he adds at once, "For this is good and acceptable in the sight of our Saviour God, who willeth all men to be saved, and to come to a knowledge of the truth." A startling assertion whose strangeness we can hardly grasp. You are to pray for all men, even the worst tyrant; because it is not proper in men's eyes but good and acceptable to God, who hates mere appearances, since He wills that *all* men be saved and come to know the truth. He must mean that the prayers, and so the life, of a Church—which depends on and is realized in its prayers—are possible only while it acknowledges a Will to good, a Will to salvation deeper than anything Christians can imagine, so deep that we must suppose the whole world, and the very worst man in it, an object of God's care, and therefore a fit object for the care of those whose wills seek to conform to His. And lest this Will be thought other than the one Paul preached, he adds: "there is one God, one Mediator also of God and men, the Man Christ Jesus, He that hath given Himself a ransom for all"—the testimony to be made in its own times—"unto which have I been appointed a herald and an apostle. I say truth, I do not lie, a teacher of nations in faith and truth." That Will that all men be saved is the Will revealed in the one Mediator Jesus Christ. When we call Him a Mediator or a ransom, we mean this or we mean nothing. He is a ransom for all or for none. If anyone is excluded, the Incarnation and Sacrifice mean nothing; there is no Gospel from heaven to earth. So Paul speaks vehemently—as those who know his style know he does only when some great point is at stake, a matter of life and death. He swears he is no liar but a truth-speaker when he says this Gospel, so contrary to men's narrow notions, so outrageous to the pride of Jews and Christians, is the good news entrusted to him, and that if he may not say it, he is no teacher of the Gentiles at all. Such language is not new to us; we have found it in every Epistle. But here it comes out more broadly than almost anywhere, because he would have Timothy understand that unless he sets this as the ground of the Ephesian Church's life, he fails in his office and is no true overseer.

Then he shows this united prayer as what keeps the men and women of the Church in their proper relation—uniting them in Christ, yet teaching them their divine, original distinction, and the subordination due from the weaker to the stronger. He wills that the men pray everywhere, "lifting up holy hands without wrath and disputation"—for these are *their* special temptations, rising as readily in the Church as in the world, from theology as from business, and cured only by the awe and fellowship of prayer. He wills the women to join too, avoiding the outward vanities of dress that are *their* special temptation, which interfere with humble, reverent prayer, and from which such prayer and its higher graces are the deliverance. But the woman might have another weakness—not from her own sex, but from ambition to be like the man. She was to pray with the man; there they were one and equal. But he was to teach and she to learn; quietness was her ornament. Adam was formed first, then Eve; the woman was deceived; the man fell by yielding to her. Yet the promise of a child, which was the first woman's deliverance, shall be a deliverance and blessing to all who follow her, if they remain in faith, love, and holiness, with modesty.

After laying down these fundamental principles about the Church, Paul turns to the office of its guardian. Most who read the description of a Christian overseer or bishop in the third chapter have been somewhat startled that the ideal is not loftier, that the qualities named are mostly what one would ask of an ordinary superintendent of a village or city, not the transcendent spiritual qualities we imagine belonged to a ruler in Apostolic times. Much is to be learnt from this. Had Paul been setting up an order separated from the world, pursuing objects the world had no part in, this commonplace morality—forbidding a ruler to be "a striker, given to much wine, greedy of filthy lucre"—would have been passed over or assumed, and only the marks of the abstracted, spiritual man dwelt on. But since he believed he spoke of a society God, not he, was setting up in the world, one meant to explain what human society is, to be light and salt, he would rather assume the inward spiritual qualities implied in its very nature and its relation to the invisible God, and would show its ruler how in his own person he might display that simple, broad, manly character the world honors but cannot produce—how in daily life he might prove there is another life than the animal one, another end than money, a power to restrain passion not in those who cultivate indifference but in those full of burning zeal. Probably a closer look at Church history would show that keeping this gracious, orderly, humane bearing amid the provocations and degrading influences of society has been harder, rarer, mightier, bringing less honor to man and more glory to God, than maintaining the separate ascetic devotion often set up against it. Some, like Bernard, were called to that other service, and deserve all admiration and love. Between the dangers of the two positions there may be little to choose, and whoever rushes into either without a vocation may bitterly repent it. But it must be a great mistake, against Paul's teaching, to hold that he who can order God's household and keep in it the character of a Christian family is not bearing as true a witness for the kingdom as those who retire from men and leave the impression that heaven is for solitaries, not for society. If people read the Epistles literally, without importing their own notions, it would be almost needless to add that Paul does not merely permit but commands that Overseers be taken from those already husbands and fathers. I see no way to avoid this inference; and the further we read in Church history, the more we find its explanation in the nature and temptations of the office.

Those who have accepted the maxim of three orders in the Church without weighing it are puzzled to find no mention of the presbyter here, Paul passing straight from overseer to deacon. I stress this precisely because I value the presbyter's office highly, and because I do *not* think the usual objections hold against the view that his office in the developed Church is the flowering of the Jewish priest's office. I welcome the omission here as proof that, while the Jewish polity stood and sacrifices were offered daily in the Temple, his office—though it existed in the Church, with a far more domestic character than the Jewish office it was translated from—had not yet acquired its full meaning, and had more to do with the Church's discipline than its worship. This confirms remarks I made on the Epistle to the Hebrews, to which I shall return when I connect that Epistle with the Epistles to the Gentile-city churches. I must note that the allusions to deacons are in the same spirit as those on overseers. All show a Divine order, but one that, because divine, is also human and *natural*—natural as opposed to artificial. The deacon is to be tested before admission, and a chief test is that he be the husband of one wife, ruling his children and house well. His wife too is part of the Church order; her gravity, sobriety, and freedom from gossip are signs of a divine calling. Here as throughout the New Testament, work or service is regarded not chiefly as a means to reward but as itself a reward. He who does a lesser work well may hope for a wider sphere; but to be discharged from work in this world or any other would be a penalty, not a prize.

The famous passage in the sixteenth verse of the third chapter has caused endless dispute—on one side, surely needless. Those who feel the Bible from first to last sets forth God's manifestation in Christ can hardly care whether the word θεός (God) stands here. If it does not, perhaps they may connect it with "the living God" in verse 15, treating what comes between as parenthetical; or connect it, against grammar, with "*mystery*," though then the masculine needs explaining; or understand "*Christ*," the relative used oddly for the demonstrative. In no way can the doctrine depend on this passage. In every way it must witness to some divine mystery that "was manifested in the flesh, justified in the Spirit, seen by angels, preached among the nations, believed on in the world, received up into glory." Since such language is monstrous except of a person, those who believe the mystery at all will identify it with a person. Far more important than this word-fighting is Paul's assertion that the Church is a Church of the living God, and that the truth on which it stands and which it upholds is the union of flesh and spirit, visible and invisible, human and divine.

The reference right after to those who in later times would fall from the faith explains, I think, the teaching Paul dreaded at Ephesus and how he wanted Timothy to counter it. Some may think the doctrines he names—forbidding marriage, commanding abstinence from meats—do not deserve such strong language or signal a coming apostasy. But taken with all he has been saying, and especially these last words, one sees that whatever despised human relationships or led men to think the earth unredeemed was in fact a denial that Christ was manifested in the flesh, justified in the Spirit, received into glory—a denial of the Gospel entrusted to Paul, destroying the very meaning and life of the Church, which exists to witness the reconciling of heaven and earth. Such a revolt also denied the great truth the Jewish people were set apart to proclaim, indeed every truth implied in civil polity among the nations. If every creature of God is not good, if we may not thank God for it, if it is not made holy by His word and prayer—the evil being not in things but in our selfish appropriation of them—then the earth is the devil's property, and Christ's baptism, temptation, and agony have not taken it from his hands. All Paul's exhortations, showing that a truly heavenly society must be a truly earthly one, that there is no inward spiritual root from which the fruits of common social morality do not spring, had been striking at these errors. He now bids Timothy openly denounce them as profane, old wives' fables. But to do so effectively he must exercise himself in real piety, which holds the promise of the life that now is and the life to come. Paul is not here preaching the doctrine he held devoutly as a Pharisee and renounced when God revealed His Son in him—that men are paid for piety by prizes in this world and a larger reversion in the next. His piety was itself the entering into a divine life, now and hereafter. He exercised himself, and bade Timothy do so, in faith and trust in a God "who is the Saviour of all men, specially of those who trust in Him." For this he labored and agonized, hoping in the living God, this Saviour of all; and to keep that hope he had to fight principalities and powers in high places. It was the cause of all the hatred of those who cast out his name as evil.

The exhortations that follow concern Church-order and government, and will be best understood by those of longest experience, even in a commercial western city rather than Ephesus, in the nineteenth century rather than the first. Timothy is told to let no one despise his youth, to remember the gift in him given through prophecy with the laying on of the presbytery's hands. Having this gift, he can be a pattern of the believers in word, conduct, love, faith, purity. He can give himself to reading, exhortation, instruction, dwelling in these things so that his progress becomes plain to all. He is to exhort older men as fathers, younger as brothers, older women as mothers, younger as sisters, with all purity. He is to respect the true widow, but not provide for widows who have children or descendants to care for them; to let those care for their own is acceptable to God. There were to be no "Corbans" in the Christian Church: anyone who did not care for his own house, especially his relations, had denied the faith and was worse than an infidel. Timothy was not to respect people zealous for the Church's work who neglected their own. For this reason younger widows were not generally to be used for Church service; they were apt to be idle triflers and gossips. Better for the younger women—even, from the context, the younger widows—to marry, bear children, and rule their households. Elders who had presided well (here we have the name, and a not very distinct allusion to a special kind of government) should be held worthy of double honor—meaning, I think, some financial provision as well as respect, for he quotes, "Thou shalt not muzzle the ox that treadeth out the corn."

Then the discipline of offenders is touched on. No charge against an elder is to be heard except before two or three witnesses. Offenders are to be rebuked openly before all, that the rest may fear. Timothy is solemnly charged to remember these rules. He stands in the presence of God, of Jesus Christ, and the elect angels; how dare he let prejudice or partiality enter his decisions? Here again the Church's spiritual principle—that men are actually brought into God's presence and the invisible world—becomes the security for ordinary human justice. And it cannot be repeated too often: whenever churches or churchmen forget or disbelieve their high calling and God's continual judgment of their hearts, they become far *more* unjust, partial, and unprincipled than states or statesmen at their worst. They become corrupters of states, or, while statesmen keep any sense of moral duty, they draw down righteous reproof and bitter contempt. So we should pray to fall into the hands of any court rather than a religious one, unless it is truly possessed by the truth Paul here presses so gravely on the bishop of Ephesus. Timothy is further told not to lay hands suddenly on anyone, however eager to send out laborers, lest he share in other men's sins—their false teaching and bad example becoming his. He is to keep himself pure, and careful of his body's health as of his spirit's. How the next passage connects with this I cannot say, nor its exact meaning. It might suggest that all bodily weaknesses are in some sense signs of inward evils, yet that those in whom such weaknesses show are no worse off than those whose evils stay hidden; there is the same righteous judgment, the same revealing to the man of what he is. This might comfort Timothy, whose bodily weaknesses perhaps discouraged him and, hindering his activity, seemed sentences of God. Both good and evil deeds, Paul seems to say, come out into view before the day when all secrets are revealed; and since what makes manifest is light, we are to receive such discoveries as from Him who is light. I do not affirm this is Paul's meaning, but I have found no other that fits as naturally here.

Then arose the questions that constantly troubled the early Church about master and slave. I will return to this with the Epistle to Philemon. Here the principle of Divine order—ultimately the great principle of civil freedom—is asserted. Let the Christian slave show all respect and honor to the heathen master, out of reverence for God's name and doctrine. Let him honor the Christian master all the more because they are brothers. Then their services become mutual, benefits given and returned, and what was a bondage becomes a relation.

These plain practical exhortations Paul calls healthy words, the words of our Lord Jesus Christ, the doctrine according to godliness. Those who set themselves against this teaching, who have a more refined Christianity, he treats with little ceremony. Such men are puffed up, have no real knowledge, and love controversies and word-fights, from which spring envy, strife, evil suspicions, the busy quarrels of men corrupted in reason and estranged from truth, who suppose godliness a means of gain. He dwells on this charge, feeling he has touched the very root of the evil. In that rich trading city, trade notions were sure to mingle with Christian thought. In his allusions to bonded goods and the seal, Paul had shown how trade could be turned to spiritual profit—but it could as easily be turned the opposite way, infecting all visions of the spiritual world and rendering them useless for reforming the visible one. The fine Gnostic speculators were really *trading* speculators, teaching men how to get a greater return for their sacrifices than others, how by certain trains of thought or outward rules to increase their happiness here or hereafter. Paul admits godliness is great gain when joined with contentment; the poorest man with food and clothing has an infinite treasure, for though we bring nothing into the world and take nothing out, we may have God as our eternal portion. "But those who will be rich fall into a temptation and a snare, and many foolish and mischievous lusts, which drown men in ruin and perdition. For the love of money is the root of all evil, through aiming at which some have wandered from the faith and pierced themselves through with many sorrows." The reader knows these words. But has he weighed them alongside the warnings against false teachers just before, and the whole letter? Has he seen that the love of money is the root of all evil not only because it draws men from the spiritual world, but because it makes that world a mere collection of prizes to be seized, so that selfishness becomes the law of heaven as of earth?

"But thou, O man of God, fly these things," he goes on; "pursue righteousness, godliness, faith, love, patience, gentleness." These are the true prizes of the kingdom, the treasures brought near to men, which Paul calls his dear son to claim for himself and his brethren. "Fight the good fight of faith"—believe earnestly that these are God's own nature, given to you in Christ. "Lay hold of eternal life"—the life of a spirit, of a man, opposed to that of an animal, of time or sense—for you were called to it and confessed the good confession that it is yours before many witnesses. And you are not the first: "Christ Jesus witnessed it before Pontius Pilate. Therefore I call upon you, as in His sight and the sight of God who quickens the dead, to keep the commandment spotless until the manifestation of our Lord Jesus Christ, which He will make in His own time, who is the blessed and only ruler, the King of kings and Lord of lords, who alone hath immortality, who dwells in light inaccessible, whom no man hath seen or can see, to whom be honor and eternal power. Amen."

Here, as in every such rapturous passage of Paul, the practical aim is never for a moment forgotten. He is still applying a direct remedy to a gross evil. This declaration of the heavenly wealth laid up in God, belonging to the spirit of man, ends in the exhortation it should never be parted from: "The rich in this world command not to have proud thoughts, nor to hope in the uncertainty of wealth, but in the God who giveth us all things richly for enjoyment; to do good, to be rich in noble works, ready to give and share, treasuring up for themselves a good foundation for the future, that they may have hold of that which is truly life." Here is the proper earthly sequel to his vision of heavenly things.

And the whole letter is wound up with an exhortation to Timothy himself that shows its perfect unity, and how the purpose Paul set at the start stayed with him to the end: "O Timothy, preserve the trust given thee, turning away from the profane empty talkings and oppositions of what is falsely called knowledge, which some professing have gone astray about the faith."

##### SECOND EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY.

The second Epistle to Timothy is somewhat more personal than the first—personal about both writer and receiver. But all its indications about the Church of Ephesus are like those we have already noticed; only Paul's sense of the evils from the false teaching he denounced is keener and more plainly expressed.

The passage from verse three to verse fifteen of the first chapter seems to show there had been great discouragement in Timothy, and perhaps the timidity that discouragement breeds. As always, Paul first dwells on the tokens of faith and earnestness Timothy had shown, and strengthens him by the example of his mother and grandmother, before hinting at his weaknesses. But neither early zeal nor others' lessons could sustain his position at Ephesus. Paul reminds him of the gift of God in him by the laying on of his hands, and that he has received a Spirit—not of cowardice, but of power, love, and a sound mind. Assured of that mighty Helper, he can bid Timothy "not be ashamed of the testimony of our Lord, nor of himself His bondsman, but be a fellow-sufferer in the Gospel by the strength of God, who saved us and called us with a holy calling, not according to our works but according to His own purpose and grace given us in Christ Jesus before the world began, and now manifested through the appearing of our Saviour Christ Jesus, who has destroyed death and brought life and immortality to light through the Gospel." I think we have never felt the force of these words, or how their parts relate—why speak of a purpose before time, a manifestation in time, a calling and salvation that do not depend on men's acts but originate them, of the death Christ destroyed, the life and immortality He brought to light—until we connect them with the feebleness and despondency of a minister of the Gospel, and see how the loss of any clause would rob him of something he needed to keep from utter hopelessness. Then comes, as always, Paul's own experience and sympathy: he too is a herald, apostle, and teacher of this Gospel like Timothy, a sufferer for it, with every temptation to be ashamed of it. "But he knows in whom he has believed, and is persuaded He is able to keep that trust against that day." Let Timothy keep before him the wholesome words heard from Paul, interpreting them by the faith and love in Christ Jesus, and keep the goodly trust committed to him by the power of the Holy Spirit dwelling in them both.

One cause of Timothy's depression appears next. There had been a general falling-away from Paul among his disciples in Asia, two especially named. Paul trembles and hopes for another—Onesiphorus—who had often stood by him bravely in Rome and Ephesus. But whatever others do, he begs his own son to be strong in the grace in Christ Jesus, and to commit to faithful men, able to teach others, the words heard from him. Timothy must remember he is Christ's soldier, to suffer with his Captain; to please Him he must not entangle himself in worldly business that would hinder his warfare. Or let him think of himself as a wrestler—no crown unless he wrestles by the rules; or as a husbandman, who ought himself to share the fruit he ripens. These parables he must lay to heart, and God will give him understanding of their meaning. Let him always keep in mind Paul's Gospel—of Jesus Christ, of David's seed, risen from the dead—or rather keep Him in mind. Then he would understand why the servant suffers as a criminal, since the Master so suffered; that God's word is not bound though the speaker is in bonds; and the great reward of suffering. Paul suffered for the elect, that they too might gain salvation in Christ with eternal glory. The word may be trusted: fellow-suffering is the way to fellow-life. If we endure with Him we shall share His kingdom; if we deny Him He will deny us; but our faithlessness will not make Him unfaithful, for He cannot deny Himself.

These great maxims for the Christian minister himself he was also to witness, as in Christ's sight, to others. Paul begs him again to have nothing to do with word-fighting, but to present himself as an approved *workman* to God. Not that words are not sacred: he had a word of truth to divide rightly, not to set one part against another. Those who used words otherwise cultivated impiety; their words became vain talk, indeed cancers destroying life instead of feeding it. For instance, Hymenæus and Philetus had become gossips and arguers about the resurrection, and, perverting Paul's own words, taught there was no future resurrection.

But this did not weaken God's foundation. There were two great practical rules for them: "God knoweth them that are His," and "Let every one that nameth the name of Christ depart from iniquity." The first would keep them from judging others; the second would tell them their own privilege and duty. Timothy need not be discouraged to find in God's house, as in any great house, vessels precious and worthless, for honorable and dishonorable uses. Each must see that he is purified from what is base, a vessel fit for honor, useful to the Master, ready for every good work. Let Timothy avoid the youthful lusts that corrupt, and pursue righteousness, faith, love, peace with those who call on the Lord out of a pure heart. Let him have as little as possible to do with foolish questions, even in argument, for they breed strife.
