And God's servant should not be striving—this we may suspect was one of Timothy's youthful faults—but gentle to all, ready to teach, patient, meekly instructing opponents, in hope that God may give them repentance and knowledge of the truth, and that they may escape the devil's snare, having been led captive by him to do his will.

It is no part of Paul's encouragement to tell Timothy he will meet no such opponents or that bright times are near. On the contrary, he says bad times are at hand. Even without his hints in other Epistles, we cannot doubt the "last days" here are days Timothy was beginning to experience and would experience more and more. The men who would be lovers of self and money, braggarts, proud, evil speakers, disobedient to parents, unthankful, unholy, having the form of godliness but denying its power, were men Timothy was to turn from. So graphically does Paul describe them that every age has rightly been sure they apply to itself. Always there have been religious teachers who "creep into houses and lead captive silly women, laden with sins, led about with divers lusts, ever learning and never able to come to the knowledge of the truth"—the trade of both Romish and Protestant deceivers, who find their most profitable converts among such women. But because the lesson is universal, it is no less true that it belonged specially to that time: everything Paul said was realized by his son in the Ephesian Church, and everything it foretold came to pass in that generation. It was Timothy who needed reminding of his country's traditions about the magicians who withstood Moses, and assuring that the new enchanter would be foiled like the old; Timothy who needed reminding that his master and father had not crept into houses or courted popularity but had believed, loved, suffered, and been persecuted everywhere; Timothy who needed telling to abide in what he had learnt and to keep to the holy Scriptures he had known from childhood, able to make him wise unto salvation through faith in Christ Jesus; Timothy who needed telling that every inspired writing is also useful for instruction, conviction, restoration, and training in righteousness, that the man of God may be perfect, fitted for every good work.

Paul is so sure a time was near when Church members "would not bear sound teaching, but according to their own lusts would heap up teachers, having a restless itching to hear, but turning their ears from truth to fables," that he uses one of his most solemn charges: Timothy is to "preach the word, be instant in season and out, reprove, rebuke, exhort, be sober in all things, suffer evil, do the work of an evangelist, fully fulfil his ministry." There is one more reason for this. Paul thinks he has nearly finished his own fight; his race is almost won. He looks to a crown that, as always, is not a crown *for* righteousness but a crown *of* righteousness—the very thing he told the Philippians he was pursuing, which he now hopes fully to attain: to dwell wholly in God's righteousness, forever delivered from his own. This crown he believes the Lord, the righteous Judge, will give him at His appearing—and give also, for how could he bear a selfish possession, to all who love that appearing. Even with this in view he craves human sympathy. He longs to see Timothy, mourns the loss of Demas, wishes for Mark (probably the John Mark over whom he had parted from Barnabas), and speaks sorrowfully, not bitterly, of his loneliness of earthly sympathy when he first defended himself before the Emperor. Among these notices of friends is an allusion to an enemy, Alexander the coppersmith, whom Timothy must beware. The seeming harshness of the parenthesis vanishes if we take Lachmann's reading, "The Lord will reward him according to his works." There is no real severity in wishing the law "whatsoever a man soweth that shall he reap" to be fulfilled in every case. If anything in Alexander was right and true, the righteous Judge would acknowledge it; what was false and opposed the truth in him or others, the same Judge would destroy. It was fitting that an Apostle who had fought the good fight and was about to be offered up should desire that in this and all things God's will be done on earth as in heaven.

##### EPISTLE TO TITUS.

The Church at Ephesus must always interest us more than the Church in Crete, of which Acts tells us nothing and later history very little. The Epistle to Titus gives a few unflattering hints about the islanders' character, which here as elsewhere was reflected in the Church's character. Even if we had only the one passage showing how carefully Paul studied every people he came to, and how sure he was that every national peculiarity would affect the faith and conduct of Christians and need watching by His ministers, the letter would be of great value—correcting many confused notions and interpreting the experience of pastor and missionary. There is a notion, constantly alternating with the practical discoveries that refute and embarrass it, that a man born from above has nothing to do with his own past or his people's life, that he is moved into a wholly different state with no inherited temperament from his ancestors. So it should be carefully remembered that few letters speak so much of the washing of regeneration and the renewing of the Holy Spirit as this very one, which quotes the Cretan prophet that his countrymen are "liars, evil beasts, slow bellies," and makes that a reason for Titus to rebuke sharply—not the unbaptized, but the baptized.

The reasons for leaving Titus in Crete are not quite those for leaving Timothy in Ephesus. First, Titus is told to "set in order the things that are wanting, and establish presbyters in each city"—no such command was given Timothy. The difference seems to be that an island the size of Crete needed only one central overseer with a number of distinct city rulers looking up to him. Here is a further step in Church organization, though still nothing giving the presbyter his later character. The next difference is that the Cretan teachers needing control, though of the circumcision and given to Jewish fables and men's commandments, seem far less Gnostic than those we heard of lately. Questionings, genealogies, and legal strifes are noted; but the context suggests the Judaizers were drenching the Church with idle legends and Rabbinical debates about trifles—things people in every age call religious because they touch places or persons in the Bible—rather than with the high-flown speculations that amused a more educated people. To these he refers when he says, "All things are pure to the pure; but to the defiled and unbelieving nothing is pure, but their mind and conscience are defiled." A good meaning and use could be found in these fables, as in the Jewish rules; possibly Titus himself had found such profit and doubted whether to discourage them. But Paul, wholly manly and practical, had learnt to see the harm this trifling did—even when harmless and childish in itself—to a people who needed moral life and restoration, a power to make them right, true, orderly citizens and men.

I have called Paul's mind practical and moral, and his object a moral society in Crete. Anyone reading honestly must see this: his wholesome doctrine aims to make old men sober, grave, reasonable; old women not slanderers or wine-bibbers; young women lovers of husbands and children; young men sober-minded; servants obedient, masters just; the overseer himself a pattern of uncorruptness, dignity, healthy speech, hospitality, love of goodness, justice, holiness, moderation. But all these great moral effects had no other origin than the saving "grace of God which had appeared to all men, teaching us that, denying ungodliness and worldly lusts, we should live temperately, righteously, and piously in this present age, awaiting the blessed hope and manifestation of the glory of our great God and Saviour Jesus Christ, who gave Himself for us to redeem us from all iniquity and cleanse for Himself a peculiar people zealous of good works." Thus the good news that God had shown His love and glory in Christ, who gave Himself to deliver men not from punishment but from sin, and would at last show His righteousness and glory as the great reward, was the only power by which Paul hoped to accomplish anything, for Crete or for the world.

And he has the same hope for Cretans as for himself. He has not called them hard names without being ready to call Paul harder. Though bred at Gamaliel's feet and striving to keep all the law's righteousness, he knew in himself an ignorance, disobedience, malice, and envy that put him at least on their level. "But when the goodness and philanthropy of our Saviour God appeared, not from works of righteousness we had done, but according to His mercy He saved us, through the washing of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Spirit, poured out on us richly through Jesus Christ our Saviour, that being justified by His grace we might become heirs according to the hope of eternal life." This doctrine Paul declared trustworthy, and would have Titus very confident about it, that those who believed God might be careful to lead in good works. These things he sets, as good and useful, against the foolish questions and debates he wished to put down. A heretical man who persisted in raising such disputes was to be rejected after one or two rebukes; the overseer's act would only ratify a sentence the man's own conscience had already passed on him.

#### THE EPISTLE TO PHILEMON.

This beautiful little Epistle gives me a chance to make a few remarks about how the teaching of the New Testament has shaped later ages. That such a short letter was kept in the canon, when so many others that must have looked more important to the Church were left out, seems to me a striking proof of the divine care watching over the Church's judgment. Mr. Canning, in one of the debates on freeing the West Indian slaves, said that Christianity grew up amid the tyranny described in Juvenal's sixth Satire, that it accepted the institution of slavery, and therefore could not be called essentially opposed to it. That challenge deserves a fair answer, and I think the Epistle to Philemon supplies one. In his letters to the Churches, Paul had not declared slaves free of their masters, nor demanded that masters dismiss their slaves; he had simply said they were brothers. Here he explains what that means. He asks a master to take back a runaway slave, as both a servant and a brother. He says he could command this as an Apostle, but instead he begs it for the love of Christ and for the love Philemon bears him, Christ's prisoner — because such pleading is stronger than commands. This is how the Apostle and the Church destroy slavery: they strike at its root by declaring that a man can never be a thing, a piece of property. And they strike not only at that one arrangement which produced and sanctified that hateful notion, but at every arrangement that stands in the way of recognizing God's Fatherhood, Christ's Brotherhood, and the dwelling of Christ's Spirit in men, so that their true manhood may be drawn out.

So we see that the principle I have traced through all Paul's Epistles — "It pleased God to reveal His Son in me, that I might preach Him among the Gentiles" — is the law of this Epistle too. And we see how any theory that confines this teaching to the Church, and so keeps the Church from witnessing it to the world, destroys its meaning and effect. If Christ is not in every man, then Christians can and will treat as property, or worse, all who do not bear their name. And soon they will feel entitled to treat men as property, or worse, even if they *do* bear it. No faith will be kept with heretics — for what has a heretic to do with Christ? No faith will be kept with those we think ungodly or who differ from us — for what have they to do with Christ? So, in our zeal for Christ, we end by destroying all the life and morality He brought into the world, and we have to invent a new morality of our own to replace what we have lost. May God grant that earnest, faithful study of Paul's Epistles, and the discovery how perfectly they agree with every part of the New Testament, may save us and our children from this terrible danger and strengthen us in the true faith of God's elect.

### CONCLUSION.

In my Lectures on the Epistle to the Hebrews I did not try to settle whether St. Paul wrote it. Having now looked at all the other letters ascribed to him, we might be better placed to pursue that question. But I still find it, as I did when studying the disputed letter itself, so secondary and unimportant that I do not wish to take it up at the close of a volume devoted to a far greater aim: showing the essential agreement of the first three Gospels, the Epistles of James and Peter, and all the letters we ascribe to Paul, with each other and with the principles unfolded in a book so unlike them all in form and style—the Epistle with which I began. I have wanted to show that the plainest Evangelist is setting forth the same Son of God and Son of Man who is the subject of Hebrews; that the Evangelists, like the writer of that Epistle, see Jesus' acts as the fulfilment of the Old Testament—not because they matched particular predictions, but because they revealed Him who was implied in all the Jewish institutions, whom the Prophets had learned from those institutions to see already as the Mediator between God and man, the King, High Priest, and Prophet from whom all their own kings, priests, and prophets drew their functions and their light. I have wanted to show that the first three Evangelists, like the writer of Hebrews, saw their own time as the winding-up of an age to end in a great judgment, and expected that judgment to bring in another age, the revelation of the kingdom of heaven their Lord had spoken of; and that the Apostles of the circumcision, as well as Paul, speak of an apostasy likely to precede the end, one that would somehow affect the Church, the Jewish nation, and the heathen world.

But if these facts show the unity of all these New Testament writers—a unity from their common testimony to the Son of God and His kingdom—we have equally had to acknowledge their diversity. Each Evangelist saw his Lord under a particular aspect; James and Peter, though usually grouped together, each had his own character and function; and Paul was markedly different from both. It has been a chief concern of ours to find out what his distinctive principle and work were: how he came to seem at odds with the original twelve, and what connects his letters, though they are addressed to churches and persons so different, and each is plainly fitted to its readers. I have gladly taken help from the name the Church has always given him. He is emphatically, by his own declaration, the teacher of the Gentiles. But that name needs explaining. If we take it to mean that he thought little of his Jewish calling, gained nothing from his training under Gamaliel, or did not first invite Jews into the fold and call them to acknowledge the Son of David before turning to Greeks or Romans, both his acts and his writings refute us. Likewise I am grateful for the hint of our Protestant tradition that he above all is the teacher of Justification by Faith. The immense worth of that doctrine has become ever clearer to us in Romans and Galatians. But if we put any notion or dogma about Justification before the revelation of Jesus Christ, made of the seed of David according to the flesh and declared to be the Son of God by the resurrection, then the meaning of Romans escaped us and his Gospel became a dead letter rather than the power of God unto salvation. And if, to make the Lutheran principle stand out, we overlooked the words in Galatians, "It pleased God to reveal His Son in me, that I might preach Him among the Gentiles," the whole purpose and coherence of that Epistle perished, and instead of defending Luther we drained his teaching of life. But if we take that passage as the key to Paul's mind, we can understand that he was the Apostle of the Gentiles because he was the Apostle of *Humanity*; that he preached Justification because he testified that Christ, Son of God and Son of David, had justified *Man* by rising from the dead; that the baptized Church was the witness of this Justification, and that each person, by believing in the risen One, himself became a justified and righteous man. And so all his messages belong together: the mystery in Ephesians, hidden from ages but now revealed, that the Gentiles are fellow-heirs of the same body; the mystery of Jesus Christ crucified, the wisdom and power of God and the living bond of human fellowship, announced to the Corinthians; the mystery of Christ's resurrection, the goal of all his striving, as he told the Philippians; the mystery of Christ in us, the hope of glory, unfolded to the Colossians; the mystery of Christ's unveiling as the judgment and deliverance of the world, set before the Thessalonians; the mystery of Christ the Mediator between God and man, the ransom for all, to be the subject of Timothy's preaching; the mystery of God our Saviour's kindness and love, by which Titus was to lift the Cretans from their debasement; the mystery of Christ the Brother of man, which broke the chains of Onesimus. All belong to the same Pauline message, and mark not only the same divine and universal Teacher but the same human agent.

If, then, those who deny that Paul wrote Hebrews rest their denial only on peculiarities of style they think impossible for the Jew of Tarsus, or on the weight of the Alexandrian fathers and the Western fathers who agreed with them, or on any external evidence whatever, I shall not argue with them—partly because I am nearly indifferent to the outcome, partly because I know how much our prior habits of mind sway us in weighing such arguments, and partly because I value their learning above my own. But if they argue *this*—that Paul, as Apostle of the Gentiles, was unlikely to write to the Hebrews, or that if he did he would surely dwell on the calling and privileges of the Gentiles—then I object. It is vital, in understanding Paul, to remember that he saw his calling to preach among the Gentiles as the carrying out of the promise to Abraham, that he adapted himself to every people he wrote to, and that his teaching about Christ as *the* Man who fulfils the promise that man should have dominion over all creatures, made a little lower than the angels to be crowned with glory and honour, is the characteristic doctrine of all his Epistles. Or if it is urged that Paul must have written formally about justification by faith and would not have spoken, as this Epistle does, of kings and priests and sabbath-days and the temple and the sacrifice, I protest that this objection rests on a theory that narrows and misrepresents the purpose of all his writings, and makes them useless for defending the very truth it tries to confine them to.

This is all I care to say about authorship. But I cannot close without noting those distinctive marks of this Epistle that are rightly held to be more prominent here than anywhere else. Although the idea of Sacrifice is present in every letter and every line of Paul we have read, nowhere is it developed as it is here. Though I have shown in my Lectures that it does not stand alone but is bound up with all the institutions of the Jewish nation, the common sense that it holds the central place among those institutions—and therefore in the book that expounds them—is the more justified the more we reflect on it. And though I have been careful to show that the Son of God and Son of Man of the first two chapters is the subject of the Epistle, as He is of all the New Testament, and that a doctrine of Sacrifice must not be put in His place here any more than a doctrine of Justification in Romans, it is plain that this Person is brought before us here especially in His twofold character of Priest and Sacrifice, His other characters not swallowed up but in some sense subordinate to these. Far from wishing to overlook this, I would earnestly point the reader to it. Whether or not Hebrews is Paul's, it is needed, I think, to complete the circle of thought into which Paul leads us. It is needed to show us the deep common ground of those societies, each with its own purpose in God's kingdom. It is needed that we may understand how each nation, and the particular Church that represented its character, was connected with that one Nation whose foundations were laid in God's eternal and righteous Will and in man's surrender of his will to it. It is needed to show how impossible it was for that nation's calling to be fulfilled, or reconciled with other nations, until there was One who, an heir of David, could say in stooping to the death common to Jew and Gentile, "Lo, I come, in the volume of the book it is written of me, to do thy will, O God"—One who through the eternal Spirit offered Himself without spot to God, that we, purged from dead works, might serve the living God. It was needed that we might know how a spiritual and universal order could rise out of the ruins of a legal and national one, and in time make each nation capable of institutions more truly divine, of a life more orderly and free, than the Hebrew ever knew. And it is needed, I think, so that we may connect the varied aspects of humanity we find in Paul with the profound and simple theology of the Apostle John, and with his vision of the New Jerusalem descending from heaven, as a bride adorned for her husband.

### LECTURE I. HOW THE NEW TESTAMENT FULFILS THE OLD.

HEBREWS I. 1-4.

*The passage:* In earlier ages God spoke to the fathers through the prophets, at many times and in many ways; but in these last days He has spoken to us by a Son, whom He appointed heir of all things, and through whom He made the worlds. This Son is the brightness of God's glory and the exact image of His being, and upholds all things by His powerful word. When He had by Himself cleansed our sins, He sat down at the right hand of God on high, having become as much greater than the angels as the name He has inherited is greater than theirs.

Christian apologists have perhaps overrated the argument for their faith drawn from its small beginnings and its wide spread. The facts do impress anyone who meets them for the first time. A thoughtful man hears how Jesus, walking by the sea of Galilee, called Simon Peter and Andrew from their nets, and they followed at once. He remembers that he hears this in some Western capital, among people utterly unlike the Galileans in manners, government, and knowledge, yet a people who have for centuries revered the names of Andrew and Peter and treated these records as oracles. Or hearing the Advent Gospel, he reflects how One who rode on an ass into the chief city of a Roman province, cheered by a few of the poorest, then cast out by its rulers as a blasphemer and put to death by their heathen masters, has been acknowledged as King of the World by the most enlightened part of the empire and by nations Tiberius never knew. Such thoughts *may* lead to the question: whence came this wonder, and to what power should it be traced? But once the surprise wears off, they as often lead the other way. The contrast between Christendom as it now is and the Book it appeals to seems so great as to break all real connection between them. "These Jewish records and this European world of ours — what have they truly in common? They may have left us a legacy, but what can they tell us of the changes worked in our belief by the mixing of races, the progress of society, new discoveries, and greater corruption? How can they explain why Christendom is divided about the very meaning of its own existence — why one body claims the name for itself alone and others denounce that body as having abandoned the very principle the name rests on?" Such questions were often asked in Bishop Warburton's day; he foresaw they would still be asked in ours; he believed they could be answered. He wished his Lecturers to face the whole difficulty squarely: to defend the truth of Christianity precisely on the ground that the Prophecies of the Old and New Testament correspond to the history of the Christian Church, and not to shrink from the hardest part of the problem — the Romish Apostasy.

These instructions can perhaps be obeyed literally if we treat prophecy as certain words fulfilled in certain isolated events. But the Bishop plainly meant us to examine the character and setting of later history, and I doubt whether we can grasp the Jewish Prophets at all unless we examine the character and setting of their national history. Their chief work is not to unfold the future but first to explain the present: they told their countrymen what their situation meant, how they had abused it, how full of significance everything around them was, and how empty their lives had become. We wrong them badly if we treat their words as oracles blurted out in wild inspiration, or their books as scattered Sibylline leaves. Their power to announce what must be seems bound up with their divine gift of seeing the eternal beneath the temporary, the substance through the shadow. They are certain that God's purpose will stand; men's unwillingness to see it will not defeat it; through mercies and judgments He will unfold it ever more clearly to the heart of the true Israelite; and in due time it will be *fulfilled*.

Fulfilment, then, seems to mean the carrying out of a purpose that was hidden in earlier times, gradually made plain to the divinely taught teachers, and through them to all who learned from them. If so, we are surely right to ask the Scriptures what this divine purpose is, by what education men in the old time were made aware of it, and what results are promised from its full revelation. If we then compare those expected results with the state of the world since the Scripture record ends, we at least show that we do not shrink from the test Warburton wished us to use. This way of applying it also suits a modern historical student better than any other. He usually cares little for coincidences, and those offered by Scriptural interpreters he tends to think either accidental or produced by twisting words and facts. What he wants is a law that connects the various facts of church or general history; he will listen to whether Scripture recognizes such a law, but he will grant its superiority only if it explains the actual course of events better and clears away apparent anomalies more successfully than any law of his own.

Yet such a student may reasonably distrust anyone who claims to draw the whole purpose of Jewish history out of the Biblical documents and to trace its rise from dawn to noon. And no onlooker's distrust can match what a man who has attempted the task, and failed repeatedly, must feel about himself. So the student may fairly ask: can your New Testament really be a key to the Old if it contains no book especially devoted to this subject? And the man who trusts that God will not leave His creatures without the help they need to know what He has revealed will search hard for a guide through this maze — and, I believe, will not search long.

From the earliest times there have been disputes about who wrote the Epistle to the Hebrews, and even about its place in the canon. But no one, I think, has ever doubted that the writer, whoever he was, meant to set out the relation between a dispensation he believed was passing away and one he believed was beginning. No outside evidence is needed to show this; it appears at every turn to the most careless reader. To an ordinary historian today it does not matter what the book claims for itself; he may view it simply as the work of a very early Christian Jew who knew his country's institutions thoroughly, loved them, yet was sure a death-sentence lay on them and that they would give place to something better. Seen so, it must be interesting, and *may* supply the key we want. Those convinced of its authority will read it differently, expecting and gladly finding in it a guiding light on God's dealings with man. And all will admit its usefulness for my task, since it bears so directly on the chief points between Protestants and Romanists, forces us to consider them historically as well as theologically, and, above all, is so solemn and practical that it reminds us we are dealing with realities that concern our own lives and every human being's — restraining, if anything can, the mean and spiteful tricks of religious dispute.

In my first three Lectures, then, I mean to show that the writer of Hebrews sets out a principle he says was implied in the Old Testament and fulfilled in the New; that he traces its gradual working through the Old; and that he helps us see what changes its full manifestation would bring.

If I am allowed to give next year's three Lectures, I mean to show that this same principle, under different aspects, is central to the other books of the New Testament: the first three Gospels, the Epistles of James, Peter, and Paul, and the writings of John.

In the third year I hope to apply this principle to the history of Christendom — in its growth out of the Roman Empire, in its direct conflict with Mahommedanism, and in what is called the Modern Period, dating from the beginning of the fourteenth century.

This inquiry will bring us into direct contact with the Romish system. But to complete the course and fulfil the Founder's intention, the last three Lectures should consider that system formally, in relation to the Scriptural principle we have been drawing out, to its working in the Church, and to the future of the world.

This plan spares me from having to open with a debate about the Epistle's author. The learned who reject the usual tradition do so because they think the writer's purpose, though it harmonizes with Paul's, is not identical with it; differences of style and the opinions of several early Churches, especially in the West, support their conclusion. On the other side, obvious external differences have not stopped earnest students from still thinking it Paul's, because they feel, consciously or not, that his life's aim would be less intelligible if this letter were not one expression of it.

For every reason, then, we should put off this question until we have found the letter's main character and can compare it with other parts of the New Testament. I am not afraid its credit will suffer while the point stays open, or whatever the answer proves to be. Those who have found by experiment that it binds the books of Scripture to each other and to human history will believe it belongs among those books, whoever wrote it — for its tone and its peculiarities, great and small, fix it in the apostolic age. Those who care nothing for its contents will hardly attend to it even if they thought the whole college of apostles had written it.

The questions to whom the letter was sent and what occasioned it cannot be so set aside, for they bring some of its most striking features into view. Everyone must notice how intensely earnest its practical exhortations are. In a book more systematic and logical than any other in the New Testament, one might have expected these to be kept in the background, not to interrupt the argument. Instead they recur constantly, enter into the very substance of the letter, and everything else seems subordinate to them. They differ in kind from those found elsewhere: they are not merely warnings against particular faults or false opinions, as in Corinthians and Galatians, but express very plainly the writer's fear that his readers are likely to become apostates from the faith of Christ.

People with exaggerated ideas of the purity of the apostolic churches — ideas certainly not drawn from the one authentic record we have — may well be startled by such warnings. But the surprise deepens when we consider *what* body was being addressed. Other signs in the Epistle leave no doubt. Its title, if we could trust it, might point to the Jews scattered through the Roman provinces who believed Jesus was the Christ — those Peter addresses. But even if such Jews were not absent from the writer's mind (and it is hard to think any part of his nation ever was), they were not the ones to whom a letter chiefly about the temple worship, the priesthood, and the holy city would most apply. Distant Jews only imagined these things; those in Palestine saw them daily. We must conclude, then, that these were the members of the mother-church of the world, the successors or survivors of those baptized when the Spirit came at Pentecost — and one who knew and loved them well feared their faith was weakening daily and that they might lose it entirely.

Yet, remarkably, these Hebrew Christians are not charged with openly abandoning any truth their early teachers gave them, nor with plainly deserting the duties of their position. The one complaint is that they had stayed content with their first shallow understanding, had not worked toward fuller and deeper knowledge, and were still taking the food of children when their years should have made them able to eat the food of men. This, the writer hints, was their besetting fault. It may look far from deadly, and their friendly teacher is quite willing to think it is not; still, he could trace too many signs of decaying belief and energy resulting from it to doubt that, if allowed to grow, it would make useless all the teaching they had received from apostles and martyrs.

It is worth finding out the grounds of this fear, both for our own good and to explain the Epistle.

1\. We should always remember that the Jews who believed Jesus was the Christ regarded themselves as the true Jews, and accused their countrymen of rejecting the real heir of David, who had come to fulfil the covenant made with their fathers. So when the apostles went up to the temple at the hour of prayer after Pentecost, this was no concession to others' prejudices, no lapse into old habit. They felt they had more right to that worship than anyone. When priests and Levites let sheep and oxen be sold there, their Master had driven them out, refusing to let His Father's house be a market. He had warned them that a day was coming when not one stone would stand on another, and every event since made them sure some awful judgment hung over the nation. But they were not to anticipate it: while the Temple stood it was God's temple, and while the sacrifices were offered they were the sacrifices He had appointed. Stephen may have said words that gave color to the charge that he had spoken against the Temple and the customs of Moses, but nothing in his defence shows he thought either should now be abandoned; rather, he deeply revered them as the shrines of a presence their fathers and they, uncircumcised in heart, had been despising. Peter's visit to Cornelius changed nothing in this feeling; the opposition to it showed how thoroughly Jewish the disciples were, and even when they yielded to the divine demonstration they saw in it only the fulfilment of the promise that David's Seed should reign over the Gentiles.

When Greek proselytes mixed with the Hebrews in the church at Antioch, and the heathen there gave them all the name of Christians, the Jerusalem Christians were affected only by being led, perhaps, to make more zealous use of their own privileges. The same happened when they heard that Paul had founded societies of Gentiles more than Jews, had appointed elders and deacons over them, and had called each one an *Ecclesia* — a body called out or elected — language that seemed strangely to threaten the idea of Abraham's children as *the* one elect family. Yet Paul in every city went first to the synagogue; his churches rested on a Jewish base; he too spoke of the promises to the fathers, went up to the feasts, and purified himself in the temple. From the conversation in Acts twenty-one it is clear that James, the presiding apostle at Jerusalem, not only kept the Mosaic law strictly but practised even its voluntary rituals. The Jewish historian and the oldest Christian annalist confirm this and agree that the Jewish people at large deeply revered him; every party in the city felt him to be its righteous man. His practical holiness was the strongest possible witness against the Pharisee's formalism and the Sadducee's heathenism — against his nation's endless religious divisions and moral debasement. It witnessed that the disciples of Jesus were not, as their enemies said, a Nazarene sect binding themselves by a new name; it witnessed to the sacredness of the old dispensation, without which the Gospel would have become mere speculation and its other preachers unintelligible to their warmest admirers.

Yet it is easy to see how men calling themselves James's disciples, and thinking they were because they zealously took his part, might pervert his very example, even turn it upside down. What in him was a thankful resolve to use all the blessings given him as a Jew so as to come nearer to the Lord of the whole earth would become in them a resolve not to mix with the uncircumcised. What in him was being set apart from a divided, exclusive world would become in them exclusiveness and division under a new pretext. What in him was the confession of his Master as the divine Fulfiller of the covenant with Abraham and David would become in them a notion of Him as merely that covenant's servant. And this vast difference might be quite invisible to these Jewish Christians. Their phrases might be borrowed from James; their reverence for holy places and love of their Jewish calling might look like an exact copy of his. But the contrast would show in the whole tone of their lives, and above all would reveal itself, after his death, to those who, like him, expected the destruction of the City and Temple and felt that the loss of such an intercessor, and the crime that caused it, must hasten the judgment. Such men would see that while James by word and life was preparing himself and his flock for that day, it would fall on his pretended admirers like a thief in the night. The Jewish institutions they had wrapped all their devotions and hopes in, for whose sake they had stopped assembling as a Christian community, would pass away with a great crash. Where would they be found after such an earthquake? What faith had they that could survive it?

2\. But it would be unjust to think these Christian Jews merely wanted to preserve the Jewish state with all its present wretched accidents. They knew the priesthood had been degraded by the Herods and made a mere tool of the Roman procurator; they knew the law had been corrupted by Pharisaic traditions; they knew society was in a worse state than in Jeremiah's day before the great captivity. But had not Christ come to restore the fallen tabernacle? Had He not declared the true meaning of the law? Had He not, by His poor life, borne witness to a more than patriarchal simplicity?

Thoughts very like these James must often have set before his disciples. In them lay the deepest truth, and they seemed almost impossible to misuse. Yet how easily one could dwell on the words "Reformer" and "Restorer" until their meaning was wholly lost. If Christ were only what those words imply, He might have told certain great truths and hinted at desirable social improvements, but He would have accomplished nothing. He might be greater than all earlier teachers, yet the universe, and men, and their relation to God would still be a riddle. All the questions men had asked for so many centuries, all that laws and governments and priesthoods and sacrifices implied, would not have been really explained by this great Prophet. There had been a vision of something bright and good in the world, which surely could not vanish without leaving some shadows; but it had vanished, and whatever these Hebrew Christians had bound up with it would vanish too. If so, then the covenant with Abraham, and all the institutions they spoke so much of, had been hollow pomp — promising much, performing almost nothing.

Would not such reflections press more and more on those who used this language? Would they not take on an awful meaning, a seeming confirmation, from the day close at hand — so that they must look forward to it not as a day of redemption but as one of defeat and dismay? And when it came, would they not be found to have lost not only their Christian but even their Jewish position?

3\. That last cast of mind led the Hebrew Christians to dwell on our Lord's poverty and humiliation, since these were the great contrast to the pride and pomp He came to purge from their countrymen. But calling themselves James's disciples, they could not always rest there. They knew their teacher had spoken of Him not only as poor, not only as sharing every human sorrow, but as divine. He had asserted both truths plainly, and did not feel them to be irreconcilable. But how were they to be reconciled? The Jewish Scriptures spoke of *angels*. Might these not be intermediate beings, hovering between earth and heaven, far below Him who fills all in all, yet far above His creatures here? Might they not, for great ends and at set times, take on real or apparent human flesh and live among men? Might our Lord be one of them — perhaps their chief? Speculations of this kind, closely tied to the idea of the Messiah, occupied the deep-thinking Alexandrian Jews and appeared in cruder, more practical forms among the despised Samaritans. Soon after the fall of Jerusalem they were working powerfully in the minds of believers in Jesus, mingling with the seemingly simple view of Him as the great Reformer, the poor man. In the next Lecture I try to show how little the Angels actually named in the Old Testament belong to this region. These ideas, then, must already have taken hold of the Church in Judæa. But where would they lead?

That middle region between heaven and earth is the home of all the demons of Greek myth, indeed of all the objects of human worship that in any age have shadowed forth men's deepest wants — their sense of their own evil, their sense of the blessings and curses of the outward world. So the very forms of idolatry the Jewish law had condemned, which the Jews believed the God of Abraham would destroy, were likely to invade the heart and conscience of anyone who entered this strange circle. Those forms had by now lost much of their definiteness and beauty, but not their power. It was an age when men talked much of emanations from the Divinity, when old idolatries were evaporating into personified qualities or natural forces, and shapeless monsters of superstition were bred from atheism joined to the terrors of a guilty conscience, afraid where there was nothing to fear. Even in Rome, where religion had become mere state machinery — where Cicero's wonder that two augurs could meet without laughing grew daily more reasonable, and the emperor was the one truly acknowledged god — dreams, portents, and Babylonian numbers never had greater sway over tyrant and slave, soldier and matron. Where, then, could a Christian Jew who once began using the invisible world as a playground for fancy be expected to stop? His Lord would soon become one of a crowd of godlike figures, symbols, or emanations, and his plain Christian faith would vanish altogether. And it would carry with it his Jewish reverence for the one Lord Jehovah. Indeed, the paganism left behind would be far less real than the paganism of old — a heap of shadows with scarcely a subject, images with scarcely the dream of an original.

There was ground enough, then, for the fear that seemed so strange at first — that the Christians of Palestine might become, in the fullest sense, apostate. And yet anyone who rudely attacked the notions and habits leading to this would have attacked precious truths and undermined their reverence for the apostles and martyrs from whom they had received them. Unless each of these convictions could be shown to rest on real ground — unless their sense of the sacredness of the Father's law and covenant could be deepened — unless they could see that Christ had indeed come to fulfil that law and covenant, had actually fulfilled it, and had stamped on it exactly the permanence Scripture said belonged to it — above all, unless their dim sense of something Divine and something Human in their Lord could be lifted out of the cloud-land of fancy and shown to rest on an eternal foundation able to sustain both past and future — they could not be readied for the coming calamities, or saved from utter shipwreck of faith. To these points, then, the writer turns in my text, which I believe is the key to the whole Epistle.

"*God*," he says, "*who in sundry times and divers manners spake in times past unto the Fathers by the Prophets, hath in these last days spoken unto us by a Son, whom He hath made heir of all things, by whom also He made the worlds; who being the brightness of His glory, the express image of His person, and upholding all things by the word of His power, when He had by himself purged our sins, sat down on the right hand of the Majesty on high, being made so much better than the angels, as He hath by inheritance obtained a more excellent name than they*."

The balanced, antithetical form of this paragraph is obvious and often noticed. But it is strange to suppose the form was a mere rhetorical trick familiar to a Hellenized Jew trained at Alexandria. We need not refute this by proving the Book's divine authority. If a writer who believed his country's last and saddest crisis was upon him, and who feared for the moral state of his readers, could deliberately sit down to balance sentences and make words jingle, we must dismiss him as devoid of truthfulness and human feeling, unable to teach anyone. A good man writing at such a time would have chosen the roughest words that could carry his meaning. But if any part of his truth came out more clearly in this antithetical form, he was bound to use it — for him it was the simplest, most natural way. The charge of artifice really lies against us, who invent rules of composition and canons of taste, calling one style easy and genuine and another laboured, without ever testing a style by the only true measure: its fitness to carry the thought entrusted to it. The more we study this passage, the more we feel that had the writer refused to be antithetical he would have sacrificed not accidents but the very essence of his meaning, and failed utterly to make his purpose plain.

The paragraph opens with two words our translators render "in sundry times and divers manners." The second phrase is fine. The first is generally felt to be unsatisfactory. Paul's words, "We know in part, and we prophesy in part," exactly explain the original. It does include the idea that the prophets' teaching was fitted to the *times* in which they appeared: a plague of locusts called for one kind of revelation, an Assyrian invasion another, an approaching captivity a deeper one still. But the leading thought is that there were many parts or divisions in the prophetic harmony; no single utterance held the whole mystery, and each part had its own "manner" — the form of history, discourse, song, or vision best suited to set it forth.

To Hebrews of the Christian age these prophecies appeared as documents to be read, translated, and annotated. The power to view them as parts of a whole was valuable, but could easily become a curse — as the Scribes and Pharisees had made it. For while they worshipped the letter of prophecy, they forgot it had come from the lips of living, suffering men and gone to the hearts of actual men. So it is not idle that the writer keeps reminding his readers throughout that the words were "spoken": if they were to be felt, they must be felt as spoken words, not merely book-words. Felt so, the phrase "God spake them" would also be recognized as real, not formal. Those who heard the prophet, whether they repented or turned away in rage, confessed his voice a divine voice. It exposed evils in them no human eye could see; it brought what was working confusedly in their spirits into terrible clearness; it set past, present, and future before them as one — one for them and one in Him who is, and was, and is to come. They did not ask how this could be. If they believed a man a prophet at all, they believed God was speaking in him. They had no doubt that He had spoken to their fathers out of the fire and proclaimed the law kept in the ark; it could not be a different voice now driving that law into their inner man, making them sure it was as true for them as for their fathers, that they had broken it as their fathers had, that its blessings and its curses alike applied to them. Indeed, did not this human speaker's voice seem in one sense even more divine than the thunder? It was less terrible to the ear, but seemed to bring more the assurance of a personal presence to the heart. The whole training of the Jew was meant to draw him away from outward, visible things. The material terrors of the plagues and the wilderness had passed; if renewed, it was in plagues and earthquakes or in Sennacherib's army. God's voice might be heard in these, but the words of the man who made that voice intelligible, telling why it spoke and what inner corruptions had provoked it, must seem to come from an even more secret and holy place.

Yet even while their fathers confessed at each crisis that God was speaking in the prophets, it must often have been hard to reconcile this with what they were told — and what the prophets themselves told them — of the Unseen, Eternal Jehovah. Why did holy men arise except to testify that no creature may measure itself against the Creator? How then could these creatures, men of like passions with themselves, seen going in and out among them, dare to say God's word was in them? This was the great perplexity of the Jew's life, woven into every thought of his nation, himself, and the divine Lord. His race was taught to think itself elect and holy, yet it was stiff-necked, of unclean lips, guilty of rebellions no heathen could match. Being in the covenant, he must count himself righteous, and when he drew near to God he knew this was no delusion — yet he was conscious of endless evil. He dared not think of God except as the High and Holy One inhabiting eternity, yet dared not doubt that men, that he himself, were meant to bear that High and Holy One's image.

These are strange contradictions, which, had they rushed on any man all at once, must have crushed him. But they came, like the prophet's revelation, in separate portions and various ways; and as each new difficulty rose, there came with it the sense of a solution — one he could act on even then, and which would one day be complete and all-sufficient.

That day, says the writer, has come; we are "at the end of those days" the prophets belonged to. Now God has spoken to us by a *Son*. Here is proof how far the antitheses are from being oratorical ornament, and how essential to the meaning: our translators made a slight, natural oversight that has, I believe, darkened not one sentence but the whole Epistle. By putting the possessive pronoun for the indefinite article — *His* Son for *a* Son — they shifted the emphasis from the right word and led readers to take "Son" as a mere synonym for Christ or the Messiah. If that were all, there would be no link between the chapter and this opening, and neither would apply directly to the Hebrew Christians' case. The Old Testament passages he quotes are not meant to prove that the holy Israelites expected a Messiah; every Pharisee granted that. The Palestinian Christians did not need to be told Jesus was the Messiah of prophecy; every act of their lives professed it. The real question to debate with the first was: what kind of Messiah did your fathers expect? and with the second: what manner of person is the one you call by that name? The writer's answer is: only to a Son of God belong those Old Testament words that, as you Pharisees agree, mean a Messiah; and only to a Son of God belong those acts that you Christian Jews attribute to Jesus of Nazareth.

I leave the working-out of these two claims, and how he links them, to another Lecture. But for now it matters greatly to notice *how* he describes the Son in whom God spoke: "*whom He hath constituted heir of all things, by whom also He made the worlds*." We often say revelation is progressive, and this writer fully justifies the word. But by *progress* some mean a continual journeying away from the centre, toward the circumference. Here we are taught that each step brings us nearer the ground of things — nearer the throne of God. Revelation in this sense is truly God unveiling Himself. First He speaks in the mere things He formed, which are furthest from Him; then in men whom He made to rule those things; last in Him who by eternal law is heir of all things, in and for whom they were created. The world's order and the succession of ages spoke of God's permanence; here He speaks in Him through whom He framed that order and that succession. At each step we are led into a higher, more awful region — yet not one more remote from humanity and its sympathies; rather into one where humanity has reached its peak, where every faculty and affection has its full growth and fruition. Cold, lifeless things are found to have a personal centre; the dead, abstract course of time is found to have a living Mover. It is the Son of God, "*the brightness of His Father's glory, the express image of His substance*." We have glimpsed His glory in creation, and more brightly in the tenderness of human beings. Here is the source of both; here is the mystery the prophets saw in fragments and spoke in various ways; here is the whole Word of which they uttered separate syllables. Men are told they are made in God's image, but knew not how. Here is His exact image — shown not in the heaven above nor the earth beneath, but in a man. In Him creation has held together despite all its inner confusion and discord: "*He has upheld it by the word of His power*." In Him we see how humanity has been a holy thing, though each man felt himself unholy: the moment He clothes Himself with its vilest accidents, it becomes actually holy, and the sinfulness of each man's separate nature is *purged* out of human nature when He inhabits it and takes it to Himself. In Him it is proved that man is meant to dwell with God: having purified the soul and body He took, He "*sat down at the right hand of the Majesty on high*," claiming for all men the privileges of spiritual beings — the power to rise above the limits of space and time and enter fellowship with Him who fills all in all. And since He, in His human nature, has been made "*so much higher than the angels, having by inheritance obtained a more excellent name than they*," man must not think himself subject to angels or place them in any middle space between himself and the Lord of all. He must confess that he too is a spirit, as they are, brought into direct relationship with Him before whom they veil their faces.

Such a doctrine was surely an awful one, and the Hebrew Christians must have felt it so — their teacher wished nothing more than that they should. A feeble, frivolous temper had grown in them, glad to lose itself among shadows and unwilling to face realities; they would have hidden among the forms and images of their ritual as Adam hid among the trees. Under pretence of exalting their privileges as Jews, they were shrinking from the greatest privilege of all — knowing Him who had called them to be His people. The writer wants to convince them that they can no longer deny their spiritual rights. They must either acknowledge and walk in them, or sink into a far thicker darkness, a deeper atheism, than their fathers ever knew. That is why fierce rebukes and awful warnings mix so strangely with words of the highest hope and encouragement: in his mind the two were never apart. A spiritual creature must have capacities for the highest vision and infinite love — and also for utter self-concentration and despair. A crisis was near that would test everything. Not earth only but heaven too would be shaken; not an ordinary civil state but the divine polity itself would seem overturned. If the outward forms of that polity were their only proof of a bond between God and His creatures, their belief would vanish. But if through those forms they had risen to grasp a relationship with God — of which all human relations are lower forms, grounded on the divine nature itself, on the union of a Son with a Father in one Eternal Spirit — then they would know there was something that could not be shaken but must remain, and that this something was a resting-place for the hopes of their race and of all creation.

### LECTURE II. THE DIVINE EDUCATION OF THE JEWS.

HEBREWS II. 6—8.

*The passage:* What is Man, that you keep him in mind? What is the Son of Man, that you visit him? You made him a little lower than the angels, crowned him with glory and honor, set him over the works of your hands, and put all things under his feet.

The opening words of this Epistle are the key to the whole of it: "*God spoke to our fathers*." This states the principle on which the Jew's history and life rested. Everything earnest and brave in the Jew came from the conviction, "We are the Lord's servants; He has made Himself known to us; He has chosen us; He is reigning over us." Everything cowardly, sensual, or idolatrous came from lacking this conviction—from forgetting the Covenant, from the feeling that after all the law came from no unseen Being and the prophet did not utter the Word of the Lord. Whatever explanation of the Old Testament we adopt, whatever interpolations we imagine, we can build it on no other basis than this. The moment we try, the records become a heap of incoherent fragments. It takes no learning to prove they do not form a whole; they *cannot*, once what unified them is gone. The Jews were no nation unless the Divine Covenant was real, not a fiction; they have no history if their books record only certain limited speculations about God, not what He spoke to man.

*God has spoken to us by a Son*. This is the fact the New Testament unfolds. Put any other fact in its place—say these books set forth an idea, not a fact, or are occupied with many subjects rather than this one—and it takes no great argument to show they too are a collection of incoherent fragments. You have taken away the bond that holds them together, and they must fall to pieces. Try to rebuild them on some other ground, and you find a hopeless, unintelligible mass that no re-forming can bring into any reasonable order.

But the writer wants to do more than state what a revelation assumes and what the later revelation was; he wants to show that the later was implied in the earlier—that the prophetic speech was a riddle until the speech in a Son explained it, and that only those who acknowledged that speech as the one adequate Divine utterance could understand their own oracles. To establish this he refers to many Old Testament passages. Understanding how he uses them is the only real difficulty in interpreting the Epistle. He who evades it must throw the whole book aside as unintelligible; he who overcomes it will, I think, find brightness where he had found the most obscurity.

Some quotations in the first chapter—important for the subject and for correcting a practical error—raise no difficulty. They concern angels. In the Jewish scriptures, the writer says, angels are always spoken of as mere servants, ministering spirits. No divinity is given them; they are sent on errands of mercy, doing works for man, not claiming to be his masters or to mediate between him and his Creator. That idea of mediation does run through the Old Testament—not in what it says of angels, but in its words about one of whom it is written, "Thou art my Son, this day have I begotten thee."

Here the difficulty begins, one that has troubled simple readers and theological students still more. The passages that follow seem tied to events happening when they were written, events in which the speaker or the nation was deeply concerned. Are we to suppose they did not refer to those events? Did they have a double sense, like a heathen oracle? Or was a later writer allowed to put in a sense not originally there? Every commentator has had to meet these questions, and the answers given have chiefly been three.

Some assume that a New Testament writer, by his commission and inspiration, could declare such and such a meaning to be the true one, and we are bound to accept his interpretation however far it differs from what we would naturally give.

Another class say: no doubt we should accept an inspired writer's word, but should we expect from one so gifted merely a set exposition of a few passages, rather than receiving him as a guide to the right principles of interpretation? May we not suppose the writer of Hebrews is sanctioning the use of allegory, stamping it as the true Divine method of reading the Old Scriptures, and so confirming beforehand the later doctors who applied it?

An opposite and bolder school cries: would it not be truer to say the writer had learned the allegorical system of the Alexandrian Jews, and adopted it readily—partly because it suited his feeling, partly as an argument aimed at those he addressed—and that by doing so he helped form a very perverse and perplexing system in the Church? In spite of his authority we must return to a fair reading of the original text, from which his translations sometimes depart widely, and give it the most literal meaning we can.

I fully agree with the first school that the interpretations in this Epistle are the safest to adopt in reading the Old Testament. I agree with the second that neither a Divine nor even a great human teacher merely gives us samples of a method without initiating us somewhat into the method itself. And I am wholly with the last in holding that reverence for Scripture binds us to seek an exact meaning for the passages quoted, found only by considering the occasions that called them forth and their historical contexts. But I differ from the first where they think it ever safe to treat Scripture as a mere set of words; and I differ from the other two in their common belief that the writer ever resorted to allegory, or ever once overlooked the circumstances under which the words he adopts were first spoken and the meaning they must have carried to those who first heard or spoke them.

There is an ambiguity in the word "literal." Sometimes it means "exact"; sometimes it is the opposite of "spiritual" or "inward." In the second sense it names the result of *merely* staring at words and syllables—a result almost worthless, for no one who spells out a sentence without asking who spoke it, to whom, when, and where will find what it means, or even that it means anything. The most exact interpreter takes most heed of everything that raises a sentence from dead to living. Such exactness, I hope to show, belongs to every reference in this Epistle; the absence of anything allegorical must be tested another way. Compare it calmly with any book, Jewish or Christian, that is confessedly allegorical, and I shall be surprised if you are not struck by a direct opposition between them. Two hints, out of many: the writer, mentioning the Temple-worship, names the cherubim and the mercy-seat, and says, "of these we cannot now speak particularly." Could an allegorist have resisted speaking particularly on exactly those things, drawing endless spiritual analogies from their form and position? Again, he spends a whole chapter on the faith of the Jewish elders—illustrated by their common acts and ordinary daily history: Abraham living in tents, waiting for a son, ready to offer him up; Moses hidden three months, refusing to be called the son of Pharaoh's daughter. Could an allegorist have endured such plain events, rather than dwelling on the meaning of the patriarchs' names and treating each act as a type of something Divine or future? In general, this writer is impatient of shadows; he always wants to turn them into something practical and substantial. Where the allegorist avoids history and treats institutions as merely earthly things, this writer shows that the history and institutions of the land were the very instruments through which the Divine revelation was made and by which minds were awakened to receive it. The passages he quotes were all bound up with parts of the Jewish constitution, familiar to the Palestine Jews in that connection—and far from hiding this, he wants them to notice it. His whole art seems devised to press this on them. Sometimes he dwells on a significant word; sometimes he neglects words—not quoting or translating with strict accuracy—if by a plainly recognized departure from the letter he can bring the subject more vividly before them, and show that the principle lay not in the words but in the fact or institution they spoke of. By this he counteracted what was harmful in his countrymen's reverence for institutions while justifying it and setting it on its right ground; for he showed how what they were tempted to worship for its own sake had been a method of Divine education to bring out the great idea of a Son—the idea realized in Jesus Christ. The steps of this Divine education mark, it seems to me, the pauses and stages of the Epistle; tracing them, we shall better value it as an exposition of Scripture and history.

I. The passages quoted in the first chapter plainly refer to the office of the Jewish King. With one exception they are all from the Psalms, and that exception is very significant: "I will be to him a Father, and he shall be to me a Son," from 2 Samuel 7. There David, after overcoming his enemies, plans to build a Temple. A prophet tells him the purpose is approved but will not be carried out in his reign: when he sleeps with his fathers, God will set up his seed, who shall build the house and whose throne shall be established forever—"I will be his Father, and he shall be my son." Every Hebrew would turn to this as the title-deed of the Jewish kingdom, the statement of the principle on which it stood. The words said directly that God had set it up; that the visible king held from the Invisible and witnessed His power and presence; that David's continuing house meant the permanence of the Divine rule and of the nation in Him. All this would be granted. The writer's aim is to show that this document held more than the mere setting up of a human kingdom by Divine will. When God said "I will be to him a Father," He signified a real relation between Himself and the human king—indeed between Himself and the nation and each of its members. This was the mystery every true Jewish king felt to be the basis of his power, what made it real power and not arbitrary; losing that faith, he became a self-willed tyrant. Yet it was a mystery, and one it might take ages to unfold.

That a Greek should look up to one dwelling on a Thessalian hill among a council of warriors like his own chiefs, and call him "Father of gods and men," was nothing strange. But that a Hebrew king should dare such language about the [I Am]{.smallcaps}—Him whom none had seen or could see, who dwelt in thick darkness, from whom the Law came amid thunder and lightning—this was wonderful. Was it that awe of Jehovah had grown less since Moses? No; nowhere is that awe deeper than in the shepherd-king's songs and in Solomon's consecration prayer. As nearness to the Divine Majesty was felt more, its awfulness was felt more too. How this could be must be learned from the experience of those who lived under this kingdom, and the Epistle turns to them next. The quotations from the Psalms seem chosen from different parts of the history, to show through what opposite circumstances and conflicts the idea grew in holy Israelites' minds—the idea of One to whom "I will be his Father, and he shall be my Son" might strictly be spoken, and in whom they must be spoken to the visible King.

The second Psalm speaks of a time when Jewish and heathen foes both rebelled against the anointed king. The holy man—king or another—is sure they will be confounded, because a king sits not in the palace but on Zion, where the Invisible Presence dwelt, to whom the Lord says, "Thou art my Son." The writer of the 97th, perhaps surrounded in Samaria or Phoenicia by worshippers of heroes and demigods who taunted him with atheism and may have stirred his own doubt whether homage to an unseen being could be real, finds relief in "The Lord reigneth"—He is the true King—but expands it into the assurance that God would raise up a true image of Himself to put down all false images, One to whom He was even then saying, "Worship him, all ye angels," heroes, gods, whatever you be. The singer of the 45th, at some festivity celebrating a royal wedding, sees a higher king, perfectly loving righteousness and hating iniquity, the true bridegroom of the nation and of humanity. The writer of the 102nd, "sitting like a pelican in the wilderness," reviled all day by enemies, is sustained by belief in a king who would reign over his own people and the heathen, because the earth is God's, who laid its foundation of old; the heavens are His work and may perish, but He endures. Finally the writer of the 110th, who probably saw no signs of royalty but lived when a priest ruled, sees that the perfect King must unite priestly and kingly offices, and that to such a one the Lord says, "Sit at my right hand till I make your foes your footstool."

A Hebrew would gladly dwell on these as showing his nation's glory and its privileges held in reserve. But was that all they meant to the holy men of old? Surely not. David asked, in my text—which throws the clearest light on all the rest—"What is *man*, that you are mindful of him? or the son of man, that you visit him? You made him a little lower than the angels, crowned him with glory and honor, set him over the works of your hands, and put all things under his feet." Through this revelation of a Divine King he was led to feel not just his own greatness, or Israel's, but the greatness of *man*.

The Jew's elevation could not be meant to degrade the human race he belonged to; it must raise it, and witness to the state God created it for. The first records of Jewish history had said all creatures were put under man, that he was made king of the world in the image of God. As yet those words seemed poorly realized: the ruler seemed slave to the servant; man's homage from the creatures was often exchanged for his homage to them; one death claimed both. Yet the feeling lay deep in all hearts that dominion over the earth and intercourse with heaven were meant for them; they had wrestled with Death as a strange intruder and unlawful usurper. So the vision of one in whom not the Jewish nation only but humanity had its true head and representative must be realized. And did not the Hebrew Christians believe there was one who had exercised kingship over all material things here on earth, proved the great tyrant an intruder, and overcome him? Though they did not yet see all things put under man, did they not see Jesus, who was made a little lower than the angels for the suffering of death, crowned with glory and honor?

II. The Jewish king chiefly embodied dignity, glory, and superiority. These were often shown in deepest weakness and involved condescension to the meanest—David's history showed the anointed ruler might be a fugitive and outlaw—yet these were not the first things one thought of about the office, nor did the writer want them to be. Those he addressed tended to lower the dignity of man, to make him the servant of angels. They showed this even when they spoke nobly of Christ as the poor man, for they meant He was nothing else and had no royalty.

But one order of men brought this suffering character before the Jews directly. The prophet was the reprover of kings; he interpreted the office and showed how it must be fulfilled—yet he was himself the suffering Israelite. The next quotations plainly relate to this: they touch the new prophet's person, his own feelings, and the witness he bore by his life more than his words. They show how the very inspiration that most raised a man above others made him more completely their brother, even in the depth of sorrow; how it bound him to greater trust and dependence than other men, and turned human relations into the means by which he and others grasped the Divine relation. There is a strange ambiguity in the words the writer wants us to notice—removed only when it was clearly shown how "he who sanctifies and those who are sanctified are all of one." As the idea of a king could be realized only in one shown to have dominion over nature and man, so the idea of a prophet could be realized only in one who had full fellowship with the lowest human estate—who, because the children shared flesh and blood, himself took part of the same, and because they were all their lives subject to the fear of death, himself entered it and overcame it.

III. In both offices, King and Prophet, there were plain signs of incompleteness, witnessing to something mightier behind, and each obviously needed the other; neither's meaning could be fulfilled till they were united. But was there this incompleteness in Moses, their first legislator? Was not the Divine King's authority marvellously joined in him with the suffering Prophet's? The awful Name was spoken to him from the bush; he spent forty days and nights on the Mount in communion with the [Lord God]{.smallcaps}; yet he was also the guide of the people through the desert, bearing their burdens and sins, feeling with them, interceding for them. Every Jew must have thought of this, and these Christian Jews, who liked to see their Master as the great reviver of the Jewish law and polity, would be especially tempted by it. Had the writer followed chronology he would have met this first. But he could not meet it effectively without first showing what was implied in their later history—in the covenant with David and the teaching of the holy men after him. From those he had gathered that a Son of God was promised to the Jewish nation, and that its greatness could not be realized except in such a person. Now, granting all Moses' glory, it was certain he never claimed this honor—he expressly disclaimed it. He proved himself a faithful *servant* in a great household God was raising up. If there was such a Divine family, it must have a ruler as well as a servant. So Moses' acts and words and office must also have been a prophecy; a Son of God must have been implied in them, one who would fully realize the idea of one admitted into the Divine converse yet sharing human griefs. The legislator was a witness of things to be spoken later. Did not Israel's journeys through the wilderness, felt then and interpreted after, betoken an imperfection? Their state was one of progress and transition; they were on their way to a rest Moses was not allowed to bring them to. All the way the promise was set before them; they were to trust it would be kept, and their distrust caused them, and even their leader, to die without seeing the land.

IV. Into that land Joshua led them. Was this, then, the promise fulfilled? This question leads the writer to another Jewish institution—one Moses pronounced holy in words showing it was held from an earlier time, one that deeply affected every Jew's life and was still kept with almost idolatrous reverence. The sabbath had been expressly linked with the hope of rest in Canaan, but it had also been enjoined in solemner words linking it to the Rest of God. It embodied a twofold idea: God resting in beholding what He made, and man resting in beholding God. Through the writings of holy men we trace a sense of a higher rest not gained by conquering Canaan, of a day implied in that sabbath, of something in its meaning not yet accomplished, though the works were finished from the world's foundation. No Jew, the writer argues, could understand the Scriptures or expect the promise to the Fathers fulfilled who was not looking for such a rest; otherwise he was repeating his forefathers' very sin—the sin for which they fell in the wilderness—resting in the present and visible, not confessing his relation to an unseen Lord, and not looking to have the darkness over that relation removed. Only the acknowledgment of One in whom both God and man could rest—in whom the fellowship of heaven and earth was fully realized—could satisfy the sabbath's meaning and the hopes it awoke in those who truly profited by it.

V. And such a one, he goes on, must be a priest, the High Priest of the Universe. This office naturally stood out most to a Hebrew Christian then. However degraded its present holders, it was the office Aaron fulfilled; they had the law that ordained it and its whole history of preservation and restoration. From here it becomes the Epistle's leading subject. The argument runs thus. First, what a priest must be: he is appointed for man in things toward God, so he must share men's feelings and wants, and there must be clear evidence he does not take the office himself but is called by God. The Jew would grant this and say, "Our priest meets both conditions—he is one of us and feels with us, yet he belongs to a tribe the Law openly designates for this service." True, the writer says, but does that legal designation satisfy the idea of the High Priest that your holy men, raised under the Law, themselves held? The 110th Psalm speaks of a Priest after the order of Melchizedek as the highest conceivable order. What order was that? Your early records tell how Abraham, returning from the slaughter of the kings, found a priest already dwelling in what later became the holy city. His name marks him a king. Nothing is said of his parents or nation, yet Abraham, father of your nation and ancestor of the Levites, recognizes his priesthood and does him homage. And the Psalmist thinks *this* priesthood—resting on no formal enactment, marked by no tribe or nation—the highest of all. He evidently supposes a priest may be, and the highest Priest must be, called in some more direct, absolute way than by an outward Law, however solemnly proclaimed. And the Psalmist himself hints what that designation must be: the oath "Thou art my Son, this day have I begotten thee" must lie at the root of it—only a *filial* priest can satisfy the idea. Only one who, like Melchizedek, is both king and priest, shown by some tokens to be a Son of God, can meet the first condition of a perfect Mediator. And only such a one can meet the second: the priest's own sinfulness was the great hindrance to the full sympathy with a sinful creature that the office implies. Only One holy, harmless, separate from sinners could truly be touched with the feeling of their infirmities and so be their needful Intercessor. Only One who had been on earth, suffered death, and passed into the heavens, now at God's right hand, could have that permanent priesthood which the legal succession in Aaron's family had only pointed to. Only One who had actually gone from the visible world into the presence of the unseen God could lead men in.

VI.
